<meta>This page continues the Latin translation of Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the union of the soul with the physical body and the resulting sensory confusion.</meta>

# Plato’s Timaeus.

Folio 10.

And a conflict tormented [the soul]: whenever the body in its tossing encountered the shock of fire, or a solid collision with earth, or the slippery flow of waters, or the violent blasts of winds. Through all these interruptions traveling through the body to the soul, the stimulated mind would seethe. Indeed, these agitations are called "senses" <note>Latin: *sensus*</note> both from the beginning and even now, stirring up the greatest and most violent motions. While the natural flow continues to stream in and out without ceasing, they shake the circuits of the soul as if with certain whirlwinds. They hinder that provident motion of its counsel—namely, the motion of the "Same" <note>The "Circle of the Same" represents rational, constant thought in Platonic cosmology.</note> whose revolution is circular—and they work against the direction in which it moves, rejecting its command. Furthermore, the circles of the "Different" <note>The "Circle of the Different" represents the wandering motions of the planets and sensory opinion.</note> are enslaved to various and uncertain motions. This is to such an extent that the limits of those double and triple quantities—and likewise the triple intervals on each side confirmed by the means of the sesquialters <note>A ratio of 3:2</note>, sesquitertians <note>A ratio of 4:3</note>, and epogdoons <note>A ratio of 9:8; these are musical/mathematical ratios used by the Creator to build the soul</note>—could be dissolved by no one except him who had joined them; yet they were long harassed by every kind of tossing. By contrary motions, they distracted the soul and its entire substance in different directions, so that it is carried along with the bonds of its own construction, but without reason, inasmuch as the motions are discordant and the allure of the senses depraves the right path.

Therefore, various inclinations of the soul exist, both oblique and contrary, and likewise similar to falling flat on one's back: as if someone, changing the natural position of their limbs, should fix their head on the ground instead of their feet, and lift their feet high instead of their head. Then, I think, both to the one who suffers this and to those who watch, the right parts seem left, and the left parts seem right. The circuits of the soul suffer this very same thing in turn. Therefore they err in the contemplation of the "Same" and the "Different" <term>Same and Different: Plato's categories for eternal truths versus changing physical realities</term>: asserting through weakness of discernment that what is different is the same, and what is the same is different. This opinion of theirs is full of error and falsehood, arising from the depravity of the senses; such a wandering path has no certain leader. And when external senses strike the soul more vehemently and possess it entirely, then that soul, subjugated and serving, is falsely thought to retain some high-priestly power. The soul is sick with these passions: both at the beginning when it was first embodied, and for as long as it is affected in this way, it will be mindless.

<margin>Nn</margin>
¶ But after the stream of growth and nutrition flows out in a thinner rivulet, and the circuits of the soul perform their way with a more tranquil motion, and the processes of time become more settled—inasmuch as they deviate very little from the competent motion of their own nature—they then easily discern the vices and goodness of the "Different" nature, and the honor of the "Same," with a desire for the one and a detestation of the other. They will protect a person so established with full <margin>† measure</margin> and unharmed prudence. But if the diligence of honest education and moderation comes to his aid in this † state, he will lead a life free from all perturbation and sickness. If he neglects it, crawling along the lame path of life, he will at last be called back to the lower regions with his familiar folly. But these things happen last, according to the merits already placed in life.

<margin>Oo</margin>
¶ Now, however, it is fitting to look at the task of divine providence regarding the rational conformation of the limbs, which celestial foresight prepared for receiving vital vigor. In the beginning, the divine powers to whom the duty of forming the body was entrusted, borrowing from the shape of the universe, fashioned the shape of the head to be smooth <margin>†painted/fashioned</margin> and spherical, and they entwined within it two circuits of venerable divinity. Moreover, the head is more honorable than the rest of the body and possesses a certain noble eminence...

<sig>b ii</sig>

<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's Latin translation and commentary on Plato's *Timaeus*, specifically focusing on the physiology of the human body, the mechanism of sight, and the nature of dreams.</meta>

# 10. With Chalcidius as Interpreter

To which the remaining members obey and follow as the dominant part, being rightly and deservedly subject to it, lest the head, lying humbly and without a fixed seat at the very bottom, should encounter the rough obstacles of earthy hollows—both inclines and declines—when it moved; especially since it was necessary for it to experience all kinds of motions. For this reason, therefore, it is supported by the vehicle of the body as if it were a citadel. There was also added around it the extendable and flexible substance of the arms, so that the use of grasping, releasing, proceeding, and resisting might be ready at will, while the divine burden of the head remains eminent. Furthermore, the divine mind, deeming the function of moving forward more convenient than retreating, willed that man should move forward rather than backward. Judging also the front parts of the body to be better than the rear, the Creator placed the mask of the countenance in man first of all, in the specific region of the head; he called this the face, and assigned to it instruments to assist the provident movements of the soul. From these, the first light-bearing orbs of the eyes shine forth, given for the following reason.

¶ There are, I believe, two powers of fire: one is devouring and destructive; the other is soothing with a harmless light. Therefore, the divine powers devised for the eyes a domestic and familiar body derived from that fire from which the light that brings the day is spread. For they willed that the internal fire of our body—being a sibling to the transparent, serene, and purified liquid fire—should flow and stream out through the eyes. Thus, through the smooth and densely packed orbs of light, which are curved as if with a firmer solidity, yet have a narrow and more subtle center, the serene fire flows out through that same middle point. 

And so, when the daylight applies itself to the stream of vision, then surely these two similar things, encountering one another, cohere into the appearance of a single body; there the flashing rays of the eyes meet, and there the ray of the internal stream flowing out is struck back by the encounter with the adjacent image. <note>Chalcidius is describing the "extramission" theory of vision, where the eye sends out light to meet the light of the world.</note> Therefore, this whole, having obtained the same and similar experience, and because of the indifferent similarity of the effect of that experience, whenever it touches something else or is itself touched by another, spreads the motion of the touch through the whole body, extending through the body even to the soul, and effects the sense which is called sight. 

But after the kindred fire has departed into the night, the vision, deserted by the help and fellowship of that light, is as if widowed. Since it proceeds into something dissimilar, it is changed and extinguished, having no natural communication with the nearby air, which lacks the splendor of fire; and it ceases to see, overcome by the allure of sleep. For the divine powers engineered the healthy covering of the eyelids for the eyes; when these are closed, that power of the internal fire is restrained by the blinking of the cover; being compressed, it spreads itself through the limbs, and as they are softened and relaxed, a healing rest grows strong. 

¶ When this rest is more intense, whatever remains of the movements there are, and in whatever places those remains are, such and similar phantasms of dreams will be born; and memory will accompany those who have been awakened. 

¶ But as for the images that arise in mirrors, and even the shadows seen on a wet surface, the explanation is easy to grasp. Since the meeting of both fires—the internal as well as the external—falls upon some smooth surface of matter, and being formed into many and various figures, the images bounce back from the sight of the polished body. Furthermore, the parts that are on the right appear on the left in these same mirrors, in an unusual manner; because the vision of the right parts is positioned against the left part of the mirror, and the left against the right, the image of the movement is gestured from the opposite side of the part from which the movement originates. ¶ But truly, the right parts of the body appear on the right, just as they are...

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the Latin translation of Plato’s Timaeus, specifically focusing on the mechanics of vision and the teleological purpose of the senses.</meta>

# XI.
-> Plato’s Timaeus <-
<note>This is a Latin translation of Plato's dialogue, likely by Chalcidius or a Renaissance humanist, discussing the nature of the physical world.</note>

In mirrors, likewise, left-hand parts appear as left-hand parts <note>original: "siniſtręq item siniſtrę videntur"</note> when the image shines back, gathered into a more solid form from the union of the sight and the brightness from the mirror. This occurs whenever the smooth clarity of a rounded mirror, with its sides being more swollen or extended into a mass here and there, casts the right part of the sight onto the left of the mirror, and likewise the left onto the right. If, finally, such a reversal of this mirror is made so that those higher parts are placed one above and the other below, the faces of the viewer will appear upside down; because of the brightness of the light, the top of the face is cast from the upper margin with the highest parts of the head, and the head itself is cast toward the lower parts; and similarly, the chin and cheeks are raised toward the upper parts.

<margin>Te</margin> <note>Likely a reference to "Teleology" or "Text."</note>
¶ These senses indeed serve the actions of God the Craftsman <note>original: "opificis Dei"</note> as he strives for the highest, best, and primary form. But commonly, they are thought to perceive through themselves and to perform some action, as if one were to attribute the authority of a finished work not to the craftsman, but to the iron or the axe. Yet the causes and those things which follow a cause differ greatly from one another. For although the foundations of all senses are corporeal, that which actually perceives—being foreign to the nature of the body—receives the sensation with a pure and incorporeal vigor. Indeed, bodies receive cold and heat, constriction and relaxation, but they do not *feel* them. Nor truly do they know reason and understanding in matters that must be done with reason and prudence. But out of all things that exist, the soul is the only one that possesses intellect and prudence. This, furthermore, is invisible. But fire and water and other materials are visible bodies. It is necessary, however, for a lover of the discipline of the intellect to search for the primary cause of a most prudent nature, not the mere supports of the primary cause. Those things which, being moved by others, move others in turn, must be considered secondary causes. We, therefore, must act in the same manner: we must discuss both kinds of causes. But we should speak separately of the best causes, which, with intellect and prudence, bring about all things honorable and good; and separately of those which, being void of mind and prudence, produce confused and disordered effects, acting blindly and as they please. And concerning the cause of the service of the eyes, for which they obtained that power of seeing which they have, enough has been said; however, a more suitable place for discussing the principal utility of their work will follow shortly.

<margin>Vv</margin> <note>Likely "Visus" (Vision).</note>
¶ For sight, according to my opinion, is the cause of the greatest benefit to most people who are not born to idleness, and it was established for the very purpose of what we are now discussing. For no one would inquire about the universe unless they had first seen the stars, the sun, and the heavens. But now, the alternating turns of day and night having been introduced to us, the months and the passing and circuits of the years were born, and from them, the counting of these very things. And from counting, the perfect and absolute concept of number emerged. Then came the remembrance of time, which taught us to seek the nature of the universe and instilled in our minds the care of investigation, opening <margin>†Planting</margin>† <note>The text notes a correction from "Pangens" (planting/fixing) to "pandens" (opening/spreading).</note> a kind of nursery of philosophy. No greater good than this has ever come to the human race from divine munificence. This, therefore, I say is the greatest benefit of sight and of the eyes. I pass over other lesser benefits; those who are removed from philosophy, lacking these, live a sad and mournful life as if weak and blind. But it seems we must state and demonstrate the cause of the divine gift which providence has healthfully devised thus far. God gave eyes to men for this reason: so that, noting the circuits of the mind and of providence that occur in heaven, they might bring those similar and kindred movements into use for their own minds. Thus, the circuits of the soul—which are called reflections or deliberations—might be made as similar as possible to the provident motions of the divine mind, which are peaceful and tranquil, even if our own are disturbed. And once the examination of our inborn reason is confirmed, while imitating the unswerving <term>aplanes<gloss>the fixed, non-wandering stars or the outer sphere of the universe</gloss></term> revolution of the intelligible world, we might correct the wandering movements of our own minds.

<summary>This page discusses the optics of mirrors and the philosophical purpose of human sight, arguing that vision was granted by God so that humans could study the heavens and harmonize their own souls with the divine order.</summary>

# IX. With Chalcidius as Interpreter

<sig>Xx</sig>
<margin>test <note>This marginal mark appears to be a contemporary pen trial or reference mark.</note></margin>
¶ The same principle applies to the voice and hearing, which were granted for the same purposes and for the full instruction of human life. Indeed, the communication of speech was organized for this reason: so that indicators of mutual will might be readily available. Furthermore, all the utility derived from music through the voice is granted to the human race for the sake of harmony. Now, Harmony <note>original: "Harmonia"</note>—that is, modulation—possessing modified tensions that are kindred and, as it were, consanguineous with the movements of our soul, is given to those who use the gift of the Muses wisely. It is intended for the sake of temperance <note>self-control or balance</note> rather than mere delight; for it recalls the discordant and out-of-tune movements of the soul back to the concert and adornment of concord, with the Muses assisting. Rhythm <note>original: "Rithmus"</note> was likewise given as a remedy against a nature that is often ungraceful, ignorant of numbers and measures, and lacking in charm.

<sig>Yy</sig>
¶ Now, since we have examined nearly everything—with very few exceptions—which the intellect of the provident mind established, it is necessary to speak also of those things which Necessity <note>original: "necessitas"; referring to the brute physical constraints of the material world</note> introduces. For the generation of this sensible world consists of a mixture resulting from the union of necessity and intelligence; intelligence dominates by a salutary persuasion, constantly drawing the rigor of necessity toward the best actions. Thus, with necessity conquered and obeying provident authorities, the first beginnings of things and the world were established. If, therefore, anyone intends to introduce the institution of this world truly and according to pure faith, he must also demonstrate the species of the "errant cause" <note>The "errant cause" refers to the unpredictable, chaotic element of matter that resists the order of the divine Mind.</note>. This will finally be done conveniently if we return to the effects of those things involved in errors—just as we did with those things arising from the mind—and unravel their generation and substance from the beginning. We must consider the nature of fire and earth, and likewise the other materials from which the labored construction of the sensible world was joined together. We must look not only at that true, ancient nature which existed before they were thickened into matter, but also the ancient qualities and sufferings of the elements themselves. For until this time, no one has indicated their true generation; rather, we speak as if people knew what fire and the rest are, calling them the "beginnings" of the universe, when in fact they do not even hold the rank or place of syllables in a true rational examination.

<sig>Zz</sig>
¶ It is therefore my profession that nothing should be said at present regarding either the beginning or the "beginnings" <note>elements</note> of the universe, as some suppose. This is not because there is any impediment other than inextricable difficulty, but because with the discourse thus established, it becomes quite impossible to reach an explanation of the matter. Therefore, do not expect it of me, nor should I persuade myself that I can sustain so great and heavy a burden. Instead, I will observe what I requested at the start of this discourse: that in dealing with imaginary things <note>the physical world as a "likeness" of the ideal</note> and with reasons prone to deception, I may be content with likely assertions and images of those same things, unfolding the beginnings of individuals and the origin of the whole.

<sig>AA</sig>
¶ We shall therefore even now invoke God as our helper before the start of our speech, as a deliverer from the turbulent and stormy tossing of the discourse we have begun. The beginning of this entire imaginary matter shall be as follows, and it shall be divided more magnificently. For previously, only two species entered into our division. But now, a third kind will also arise. In our earlier discussions, two seemed to suffice abundantly: one perpetual, <margin>imminence <note>original: "iminētīc"</note></margin> lacking generation, the excellence of the Exemplar <note>the perfect, eternal blueprint of the world</note>; the other a likeness and image of it, propagated for a second generation, and visible. But as for the third, we did not divide it at that time. Now, however, reason seems to meet us, imposing a necessity to join hands against an invincible...

It introduces the need for a "third kind" of being (the Receptacle or Space) beyond the eternal Model and the visible Copy.</summary>

# Plato’s Timaeus. Folio XII.

...by reason: and a fraud to all eloquence, enveloped in thick darkness.

¶ What power, then, or what nature must we think it possesses? I believe it is the **receptacle** <note>original: *receptaculum*; Plato’s term for the "space" or "matrix" in which things are formed</note> of all things that are born: and like a certain **nurse** <note>original: *nutricula*; a metaphor for the supporting role of matter</note>. And indeed, what is said of it is true: but it seems necessary to speak a little more clearly. Yet it is difficult, all the more because the edge of the mind must necessarily be confused and in a state of flux, as much concerning fire as the other materials; for which of them ought more rightly to be called and thought of as water than as earth? Since there is no certain and stable property of bodies which might indicate the natural kinship of each.

<margin>Water.</margin>
<margin>Air.</margin>
¶ To begin, let us start with water, which we just mentioned: when it is bound into ice, it certainly appears as a stone and a body of earthy solidity, and not at all fluid. This same substance, when heated and flowing and variously dispersed, dissolves into moisture, spirit, and airy breezes. Furthermore, air, when burnt up, creates fire. Conversely, when fire is extinguished, it is restored as air, having become more corporeal. Likewise, when air becomes thicker, it congeals into clouds and mists. When these are crushed and squeezed out, an abundance of rain, pools, and springs results, and finally, earthy masses are heaped up from the water. And so, in a certain circuit, the powers and nourishments of generation are borrowed by bodies from one another in turn: and since they do not persist in one and the same form, what certain comprehension of them can there be, removed from hesitation? Certainly none. Therefore, concerning all such changeable things, it must be held thus: That which often appears to us formed in one way or another, and mostly in the likeness of fire, is not, I believe, **fire**, but something **fiery**; nor is it **air**, but something **airy**; nor is it anything at all that has any stability. Finally, they are not even worthy of any pronouns which we are accustomed to use in demonstration, such as when we say "this" or "that." For they flee and do not wait for that designation which is held concerning them as if they were existing things. Therefore, we must think that fire is truly that which is always the same: and everything whose property remains. But indeed, that in which each of these things is seen to come to be, and finally to dissolve and, perishing, to pass from there into other forms—I think only that should be called by a certain pronoun. For it can rightly be said of it, "this" or "that." Furthermore, that which receives a quality—or can even be turned into contrary qualities, such as hot or white—it is not at all fitting to call by a proper and certain name that which is uncertain and changeable. But I think even now we must attempt to discourse more clearly on the same subject.

<margin>† it departs from.</margin>
¶ For if someone were to reshape all forms and figures made from one and the same material of gold, continually and without intermission into one form after another: then if someone, having chosen any one figure, should ask what it is: I believe it could be answered firmly, diligently, and without reproach, that it is **gold**; nor will it seem right to add that it is a triangle, a cylinder, or any other form. The same and similar reasoning and difficulty is found in that nature which receives all bodies. For this <insert>nature</insert> least of all † departs from its own condition. For it receives all things: nor does it draw any form from them: and while those things which it receives are formed as if within its womb, it remains itself formless: and its use is similar to a soft and yielding material into which various marks are impressed; and it is moved and shaped in every way by the things entering it, having itself neither form nor motion from its own nature. But those things which enter change the forms, and some are seen as different from others: and those very things which enter and depart are **images** <note>original: *simulachra*; reflections or copies of the eternal Forms</note> of truly existing things: formed in a marvelous and scarcely explainable way: from those same existing things: just as we shall soon avoid no effort to demonstrate.

¶ But now, I say, a threefold genus must be assumed: which...

<summary>This page discusses the "Receptacle" of the universe, explaining that while the four elements (fire, air, water, earth) are constantly transforming into one another, there is an underlying, formless substrate that remains constant, much like gold remains gold regardless of the shape it takes.</summary>
<keywords>Receptacle, four elements, transformation of matter, Plato's Timaeus, material substrate, images of reality, nature of change</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chalcidius' Latin translation and commentary on Plato’s Timaeus, specifically focusing on the nature of the "Receptacle" (materia) and the distinction between intellect and opinion.</meta>

# With Chalcidius as Interpreter

...is born, likewise another in which it is born, and furthermore a third from which the thing being born draws its likeness and borrows its form. It is fitting, therefore, to make a comparison and assign a likeness: to that which receives, the role of the **mother**; to that from which it derives, the **father**; and to that nature which exists between these two, the **offspring**. At the same time, it must be understood in this way: it is impossible for a single face to exist that contains all the forms and countenances of all things, showing various aspects of the body from every side, unless there is first an unformed substrate—a certain bodily womb—just as in paintings, a colorless base layer <note>original: "infectio decolor"</note> is spread underneath to bring out the lights of the colors.

<margin>FF</margin>
¶ For indeed, if the receptacle were similar to any of those things which it receives into itself, then when something dissimilar to those things it resembles arrived, its "face" would, I believe, clash with the face of the entering body, and it would express no true likeness. From this it follows that the bosom of the receptacle has no naturally expressed figure of its own; therefore, it is understood to be **unformed**, lacking every shape entirely. Just as those who prepare fragrant ointments take care above all that the base liquids which are to be seasoned have no scent of their own, so that they may better receive the liquid juices of the perfumes. And those who wish to impress certain forms into soft materials that yield to pressure prepare them to be perfectly smooth, allowing no shape at all to appear in the prepared smoothness. In this same way, for that which is to be rightly impressed with all the forms and figures of all things—those which are eternal and remain through the ages—no proper appearance of its own should be attributed by false opinion. Therefore, we must call the mother and receptacle of this created, generated, and visible living thing <note>The "animal" or "living world" described by Plato.</note> and of bodily substance neither earth, nor water, nor indeed fire, nor air, nor anything created from them, nor even the elements from which these themselves subsist. Rather, we should call it a certain **invisible species** and an **unformed capacity**, situated by a marvelous and incomprehensible reason between non-substance and some substance—neither plainly intelligible nor plainly sensible—but which seems able to be understood from those things that are changed within it. Indeed, the ignited part of it is fire; the moistened part of it is water—if indeed any part can be named in a thing so devoid of qualities. It also becomes earth and air by that same reasoning, if by chance it receives their likenesses into itself. Concerning each of these, a treatise of this kind must be established:

<margin>GG</margin>
¶ Is there some fire positioned apart and uncommunicable? Likewise, are there other species <note>Forms or Ideas</note> which we, conceiving them in the mind, say are separated from the gathering of bodily species to be the **archetypal models** of sensible things? Or are these things which are seen, and which we perceive through the intensity of the body, the only things that exist? Is there nothing else besides these anywhere, and is it presumed in vain that there are intelligible species of which sensible things are images—and that these species are nothing other than words? This matter should neither be left unexamined, nor should a long train of words be added that is irrelevant to the subject. But truly, if someone can settle the dispute of this great matter with a summary, it is the duty of a worker to adopt that path. I myself, therefore, will say what I feel regarding this matter:

<margin>HH</margin>
¶ If **intellect** and **true opinion** are two different things, it is necessary that these [Forms] be attained through the intellect itself rather than through the senses. But if, as it seems to some, true opinion does not differ from intellect, then all things which we perceive as bodily must be held as certain. But I believe it must be said <margin>†to be said.</margin> that they are two, because they are separated by a great difference. For indeed, instruction <note>teaching/doctrine</note> instills the one in us, while the adoption of persuasion instills the other. The one is always accompanied by true reason, while the other is without any reason. Furthermore, the one cannot be changed by any persuasion, while the other is wavering, uncertain, and always <margin>†divertible,</margin> divertible. What more? Every man partakes in right opinion, but intellect is the property of **God** and of a very few chosen men. Since this is so...

<summary>This page discusses the "Receptacle" (materia) as a formless "mother" that receives the likenesses of eternal Forms, and argues for the essential distinction between Intellect (associated with God and truth) and Opinion (associated with persuasion and the senses).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Latin translation of Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the distinction between eternal forms, the physical world, and the "receptacle" or space. The previous context likely established the dual nature of reality.</meta>

# Plato’s Timaeus

It must be confessed that there is one kind of being that is always the same, removed from the senses and existing within itself; it is without beginning and without end. It neither receives anything into itself from elsewhere, nor does it go out toward anything else. It is invisible and imperceptible to the senses, clear only to the intention and observation of the mind. <margin>II</margin> Furthermore, that which is second to this is that which is born, sensible, and sustained; it exists in some place and again recedes through change and destruction. This kind is to be known through the senses and opinion. The third kind is that of Space <note>original: "loci"</note>, which does not pertain to destruction at all; it provides a seat for all things that are generated. Yet, it is perceived without the sense of touch, being graspable by a certain "adulterous" or bastard reasoning <note>Plato uses this term to describe how we "see" space not by logic or sense, but by a sort of dream-like intuition</note>. <margin>KK</margin> Finally, when we gaze upon this with the mind, we experience what those who are dreaming do. For we think it necessary that everything which exists must be positioned in some place and occupy some region. Furthermore, we imagine that what is neither on earth nor in heaven does not exist at all. Because of this depravity of thought, and others related to it, we fail to stand firm in our mental consideration of the truly existing and truly wakeful nature. We are held back by these dreams, since we are unable to imagine any clear form of this slippery kind of being. Indeed, it has no form of its own, yet it seems to have them all, since within its bosom, forms are transfigured from one thing into another by a process of turning. This same thing is found positioned between some other substance and nothingness; it has no substance of its own, yet it is not nothing. But the assertion of truly existing things is strengthened by the clear light of reason. <margin>†to be taught</margin> †It must be said that this is one thing and that is another; neither can exist within the other, nor can the same thing become one and two at the same time. <margin>LL</margin> This is the core of my opinion: that these three things existed even before the adornment of the sensible world: Being, Space, and Generation. Therefore, the "nurse" of generation <note>The Receptacle, which Plato describes as a wet-nurse or mother to physical things</note> is sometimes moistened, sometimes ignited, taking on the forms of earth and air, and suffering all the other accompanying passions, appearing in every kind of visual form. <margin>MM</margin> However, it is not equipped with similar forces or equal powers; nothing in it is balanced. Instead, it sways in every direction, tilting toward the discordant or the heavy. It is shaken by the materials that agitate it, and in turn, it strikes and shakes those materials by a reciprocal impulse. From this fluctuation, the turbulent materials are snatched in different directions and separated from one another, just as we see happens in the purging of grain with winnowing instruments; through motion and shaking, the heavy and solid parts fall one way, while the thin and light parts go to another side. Thus, those four elements were tossed about as if in a surging strait <note>original: "Euripo," referring to the Euripus Strait known for its violent currents</note>, or like a machine made to stir things up. The most dissimilar things were driven furthest apart, while those joined by some similarity were not separated. For this reason, they occupied different places even before the world was adorned. At that time, there was an inordinate confusion of things, devoid of reason. <margin>NN †continuous †was present</margin> But when it pleased God that all things should be reduced to order, the Creator God <note>original: "opifex Deus," the Demiurge</note> first †fashioned fire, earth, air, and water. They were not then as they are now, but they bore only the †continuous traces of elements in that squalor and deformity which appears in things where divine †foresight is lacking. But now, light and beauty having been granted to each, a numerical order followed the birth of all these enlightened things, all beautiful things having been established from what was once not so. Now it is appropriate to demonstrate their ordination and birth one by one, using a new and unusual kind of demonstration; yet to you, who have traveled all the paths of noble learning, it will not be unknown, and will be clear from a slight reminder.

->THE END.<-

<meta>This is the opening page of Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus. Chalcidius was a 4th-century scholar whose translation and commentary served as the primary source of Platonic thought for the Latin West for nearly eight hundred years.</meta>

<header>Interpretation of Chalcidius</header>

# THE COMMENTARIES OF THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS CHALCIDIUS ON THE TIMAEUS OF PLATO,

<image-desc>A large, square woodcut decorative initial 'T' featuring an ornate background of scrolling foliage and a seated figure, possibly a scholar, holding a book.</image-desc>THE TIMAEUS OF PLATO HAS BEEN HELD BY THE ANCIENTS TO BE DIFFI-
cult, and judged hard to understand: not because of any weakness or obscurity born of the language (for what could be more ready than that man’s eloquence?) but because those reading it lacked experience in the <term>artful reasoning</term> <note>original: "artificiose rationis" — referring to the systematic logic of the liberal arts</note> which works to explain the questions of things. The style was so established as to demonstrate the matter under discussion not through foreign arguments, but through proofs proper to the questions themselves. For that proof alone is certain which reveals questions through congruent and fitting reasons; just as if some doubt should arise concerning the motion of the stars, the assertion should flow from that discipline called <term>Astronomy</term>. If a treatise is held concerning the variety of strings and the harmony born of diverse voices and sounds, the doubt should be put to rest by the remedies of <term>Music</term>. And indeed, that principle should be maintained which is everywhere observed by the most skilled physicians, when they apply the aid of medicines in healing according to the nature of the wounds. Furthermore, in this book, since it deals with the state of the entire universe and the cause and reason for all things the world encompasses are provided, it was necessary for many and various questions to arise: concerning plane figures, solid bodies, and the <term>incorporation of the soul</term> <note>The process of the soul entering or giving life to a physical body</note> that gives life to the sensible world; concerning its motion and perpetual agitation; and concerning the courses of the stars, both fixed and wandering. All these had to be met with the systematic remedies of certain disciplines—Arithmetic, Astronomy, Geometry, and Music—so that individual matters might be explained by their own domestic and related reasons. Therefore, to those who were not practiced in these arts, the work was least approved, as if they were ignorant of a foreign tongue. Furthermore, to others who had mastered only one of the disciplines, only that which they knew was approved upon recognition, while the rest lay hidden in the darkness of ignorance. From this it appears that this work was elaborated for, and seen by, almost those alone who were versed in the use and exercise of all such sciences. Although these men ought to have shared such great clarity of knowledge with others, they kept it uncommunicable to themselves through a detestable restriction of unhappy envy, rather than a generous pouring out of blessedness. Therefore, because I had to comply with your commands—even though the things commanded were greater than a mediocre talent could sustain—I was not content with translation alone. I reckoned that the likeness of an obscure and far from illustrious example, if translated without interpretation, would result in the vice of the same, or even greater, obscurity. And those things which seemed to me to be in some difficulty I have interpreted in such a way as to explain only those matters which were covered by ignorance of unknown arts and disciplines. For it would have been arrogant, and as if distrusting the intellects of the readers, to unravel in vain with superstitious questioning those things which lie open to the common understanding of all. Finally, concerning the beginning of the book, which contained a simple narration—a review of past deeds and ancient history—I have said nothing. Nevertheless, I thought the reason for the whole work, the purpose of the writer, and the arrangement of the book ought to be declared. For on the day before, Socrates had disputed in ten whole books concerning the <term>Republic</term> <note>original: "de Repub." — Plato's Republic</note>, a treatise he had descended into not from a primary cause, but as a consequence. Indeed, when the inquiry into justice had begun, which the orator Thrasymachus had defined as being that which was of benefit to...

<summary>Chalcidius introduces his commentary on Plato's Timaeus, explaining that the text's difficulty arises from its reliance on the four mathematical sciences (the Quadrivium). He justifies his decision to provide both a translation and an interpretive commentary to make these technical concepts accessible.</summary>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically contrasting Socrates's discourse on human justice with Timaeus's discourse on natural and divine justice.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus.

...who was most powerful: Socrates, on the other hand, had taught—or rather, preferred to teach—that which would benefit those who were least powerful. To use a more illustrious example, he examined this not through the talent of a single man, but within the crowded and popular assembly of some populous city. He painted a certain image of a city governed by just morals and institutions, enjoying a happiness consistent with its laws. Conversely, he showed how unsuccessful that state would be if it ever degenerated from its institutions, and how fatal the change of morals would be. Therefore, since in those books <note>Referring to Plato's *Republic*</note> justice seemed to have been sought and found as it exists in human affairs, it remained for an investigation to be made into natural equity. Socrates thought the execution of this great task—which he called burdensome to his own talent—should be delegated to Timaeus, Critias, and Hermocrates; and they accepted the assigned duty. From this it appears that the principal aim of this book is to establish a contemplation and consideration not of "positive" <note>Man-made or statutory law</note> justice, but of that natural justice and equity which, though written into the establishment of laws and the formulas of legal drafting, derives its substance from a twin moderation. Just as Socrates, when discussing the justice used by men, introduced the likeness of a civil commonwealth, so Timaeus of Locri—skilled in the mastery of Pythagoras and the discipline of <margin>perfect</margin> astronomy—wished to inquire into that justice which the divine race uses toward itself, within this sensible world, which is like a common city and commonwealth.

<margin>Division of the work into chapters.</margin> ¶ Now the arrangement of the book and its specific parts must be revealed. I. First, the inquiry concerns the birth of the world. II. Then, the origin of the soul. III. Then, modulation or harmony. IV. On numbers. V. On the fixed and wandering stars, in whose number the sun and moon are also placed. VI. On the heavens. VII. On the four kinds of living beings: namely celestial, winged, swimming, and land-dwelling. VIII. On the origin of the human race. IX. The cause why most men are wise, and others foolish. X. On sight. XI. On images. XII. Praise of seeing. XIII. On matter <note>original: "sylua", literally "forest" or "timber," used here as a technical term for primordial matter</note>. XIV. On time. XV. On the first materials of matter and their properties. XVI. On the different <margin>oppressions</margin> humors of bodies and on phlegm. XVII. On the senses of smell and taste. XVIII. On the variety of colors and the conversion of one into another, and things similar to colors. XIX. On the principle of vital substance. XX. On the soul, its parts, and its locations. XXI. On the members and joints of the body. XXII. On the nations of different peoples. XXIII. On sickness of the body. XXIV. On sickness of the soul. XXV. On the healing of both substances. XXVI. On the entire universe and all that the world embraces. XXVII. On the intelligible God. An exposition of all these will be made individually according to the order of the book.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial N featuring vine motifs with leaves and bunches of grapes.</image-desc>

For, in order to teach that the body of the world is perfect—and furthermore, perfect bodies are solids consisting of three dimensions: length, width, and thickness—he first explains the "epipedas," that is, plane figures, which have only length and width but no depth. He says that one "mean" <note>A mathematical middle term</note> can suffice for these, because two distant points are joined by a single intervening middle, provided there is some relationship between those two distances. He says: "For when, out of three terms—whether numbers, masses, or powers—the lowest stands to the middle just as the highest stands to the middle; and again, as the lowest is to the middle, so the middle is to the highest: then certainly the middle differs nothing from the highest and likewise the lowest. And again, those extremes having been reduced to the condition and equality of the middle, since the middle also takes the place of the extremes, it happens, I believe, that the whole matter is united by one and the same ratio."

<summary>This page introduces the Timaeus as a study of natural and divine justice, contrasting it with the civil justice of the Republic, and provides a 27-chapter outline of the work's contents ranging from cosmology to human anatomy.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Timaeus, Socrates, justice, natural law, cosmology, harmony, elements, geometry, mathematical mean, soul, humors</keywords>

<header>Chalcidius’s Interpretation</header>
<lang>la</lang>
<page-type>text</page-type>

In this way, all the members will be the same to one another: for their condition is one and the same. Furthermore, once the members are made one, the whole will be one and the same. He employs an arithmetic proof. Let a triple figure be drawn, therefore, to show how, if two things are distant from each other, they may be joined by a single mean through a related interposition.

<note>The following diagram illustrates a geometric progression (6, 12, 24) where 12 serves as the "mean proportional" or middle link between the two extremes.</note>

<image-desc>An arithmetic diagram displaying the relationship between three rectangular grids of increasing size.
1. Top left: A 2x3 grid labeled '6'.
2. Bottom left: A 3x4 grid labeled '12'.
3. Right: A 4x6 grid labeled '24'.
This illustrates a geometric progression (6, 12, 24) where 12 is the mean proportional between 6 and 24.</image-desc>

Of the square <note>original: "quadrati", here referring to a rectangular area</note> which is drawn in the first place, let one side be in units—for example, two—and the other side in three units. This, when calculated, makes the area of the whole completed square six units. For twice three are six. Of the last square, however, which is distant from the first, let one side be four units and the other side six units. This, when calculated, makes the area of the entire square twenty-four units. For four times six are twenty-four. Now this mean, which is twelve, is attached in such a way that, when pushed, it seems to join the outermost squares: it is related to the extremes. For by whatever part it is smaller than the lowest—that is, by half—by that much it is larger than the highest. For twelve is half of the sum of twenty-four. Again, the sum of twenty-four placed at the bottom is the double of twelve, which is in the middle. Likewise, the middle sum is the double of the higher sum, for twice six is twelve. Thus they are associated by the ratio of the half and the double: and there becomes one and the same condition for the outermost limits and the mean. What more? That the mean is born from the extremes? For whether I multiply the smaller sum of the highest limit with the larger sum of the lowest limit, or conversely compare the larger sum of the highest limit with the smaller of the lowest, the number twelve is born by multiplying in both these ways. For both twice six and three times four will make twelve. This same continuation of two things distant from each other by the injection of a single mean may also be revealed by geometric proofs. Let the figures, therefore, be drawn even now.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a large rectangle subdivided into four smaller parallelograms by an internal vertical line and an internal horizontal line. The vertices and intersections are labeled with a mix of Roman and Greek letters: A, Δ, E across the top; B, Γ, Z in the middle; H and Θ at the bottom. Lines connect these points to demonstrate geometric means and proportionality.</image-desc>

Geometricians call two lines placed opposite each other "parallels," and consequently they call plane four-sided figures "parallelograms." The purpose is to prove how, between two similar parallelograms, one mean may be found that does not lack a ratio. Let there be, therefore, two similar parallelograms marked by the Greek letters A Δ Θ having equal angles: one which is under B Γ Δ, to that which is under H Γ Z. Furthermore, where there are equal angles, the sides will be in proportion: for as side B Γ is to side Γ Z, so is side Δ Γ to side Γ H. I say that another rational mean of these two similar parallelograms is found. Let side B Γ be in a straight line with side Γ Z. Therefore, side Δ Γ will also be in a straight line with side Γ H. And with the lines A Δ and Θ Z being straight, let the outermost lines A E and Z E be drawn: and in this way let the whole figure be completed. As side B Γ is to side Γ Z, so is parallelogram B Δ to parallelogram Δ Z. For they are in the same quadrature <note>area/alignment</note> A E: B Z. And again, as side Δ Γ is to side Γ H, so also is parallelogram Γ B to parallelogram Z H. For these too are in the quadrature Δ H E Θ. Therefore, as parallelogram B Δ is to parallelogram Δ Z, so is that same parallelogram Δ Z to that which is below it, parallelogram Γ Θ. One mean has therefore been found—that is, parallelogram Δ Z—between two similar parallelograms, which is what was to be done. Nevertheless, the insertion of a mean is also considered in triangular forms in a figure of this kind. The purpose is to prove how one fitting mean of similar triangles may be found. Let there be two similar triangles bounded by the Greek letters A B Γ Δ...

# On Plato’s Timaeus.

<margin>A</margin> <image-desc>A geometric diagram consisting of a rectangle with a diagonal line and internal labels. The vertices and intersection points are marked with the letters A, B, Γ, and E. The diagram is used to illustrate the proportional relationship of triangles mentioned in the text.</image-desc> ...the other A B Γ A with equal angles: one which is under A B Γ, the other which is under A B E. Where there are equal angles, the sides will also be in proportion; for as side A B is to side B B, so side Γ B is to side B A. I say that of these two similar triangles, one proportional mean is found. For thus <margin>A</margin> it is granted that side A B is in a straight line with side B B: side Γ B will also be indirect to side B A: and let the line drawn in a straight line join the side which is A A. Therefore, as side A B is to side B B, so triangle B A E B is to triangle A B A A. Indeed, the letters A A are under the same height. Moreover, as side Γ B is to side B A, so triangle Δ A B Δ is to triangle A B Γ A. For these also are under the same height of the letters A. Therefore, as triangle A B E A is to triangle A B A A, so the same triangle A B A A is to triangle A B Γ A. Thus, from two similar triangles, that is A B Γ A and A B E A, a third has been made, that is triangle A B A A, which was what needed to be done. <note>This geometric proof demonstrates how to find a "mean proportional" between two areas, a concept Plato uses to explain how God bound the elements of the universe together.</note>

<margin>Kinship</margin> Therefore, he says, if the body of the world were meant to have only length and width, but no solidity <note>original: "soliditatem," referring to three-dimensional depth</note>: and if it were of such a kind as is the surface of perfect bodies: one mean would suffice to bind itself and the outermost parts. But now, since there was a need for solidity in the worldly body—and solid things are never bound by one, but by two means—for that reason the Maker of the world inserted air and water between fire and earth, balancing these same elements in a healthy measure: so that whatever kinship <note>original: "cognatio"</note> exists between fire and air, the same would be between air and water. Again, whatever is between air and water, this same relationship would consist in the fellowship of water and earth.

<margin>Two means</margin> He says the body of the world is solid and spherical. Moreover, bodies of this kind are bound by two means, insofar as in the likeness of a ball and in that circuit there is indeed a certain length: there is also a width: which we measure by a point marked in the middle of the summit: to which middle point equal lines are extended from the circumference on all sides. One mean, therefore, is situated on the surface, the other in the depth: to which that point arrives if it is pressed down as far as the middle height. For which reason, since there are twin means, multiplication must be performed not once, as in plane figures, but twice. Indeed, there we were describing two sides: one of which we established in two moments: the other in three: and multiplying two by three we found the number six to be born. But in these solid forms, on account of the twin mean, the multiplication will be doubled: so that when from two and three multiplied, that same six is born according to the ratio of one mean; then according to the ratio of the other mean, which perhaps will be in the number four, let us calculate four times six; and thus the whole sum, whether of number, or mass, or quality, may be found in twenty-four parts. And since the summit of the world, that is fire, being a solid body, possesses those three—length, width, solidity—but not equally, since in that fire there is more brightness: somewhat less of moderate heat: but a tiny amount of solidity: whereas in the globe of the earth there is more solidity: a certain amount of moisture: and very little light: let us understand the kinship which the two means of Air and Water have with the above-mentioned elements.

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

<note>Chalcidius was a 4th-century philosopher known for his Latin translation and commentary on Plato’s Timaeus, which was the primary source of Platonic thought in the West for centuries.</note>

->The understanding of these solid forms is indicated by the partnership of numbers.<-

If indeed the sum of the first boundary is half the sum of the second boundary—for the number 24 is half of the number 48—likewise the second is half of the third, and this third is half of the sum of the fourth boundary. Equally, working backward, the last is double the third, the third is double the second, and the second is double the first. And by these two means <note>The "means" or "medieties" are the middle terms in a mathematical proportion.</note> which are inserted between the two outermost forms, a generation is produced from those same extremes. For the middle of the first boundary is in the greater number, that is, six, which is born from the multiplication of these three smaller sums. Likewise, the middle of the last boundary is in the lesser number, that is, 8. Therefore, by multiplying the greater middle of the first boundary with the lesser middle of the last boundary, I create the entire substance of the second boundary, placed in the numbers 40 and 8 <note>48</note>. But truly, if I compare the lesser middle of the first boundary, which is 4, with the greater middle of the last boundary, which is in the sum of 24 born from four times six, the entire substance of the third boundary will be created, which is placed in 96. Thus, the two extremes of solid bodies are bound by two means: as the certain and least deniable authority of the discipline of arithmetic confirms, providing testimony for Plato.

¶ This same distance between two solid bodies—or the double mean—shows here how to join distant bodies by geometric ratios in a <term>fitting manner</term> <note>original: "competenti modo"</note>. But first, because it is relevant and suited to the treatise, I shall explain what this is that he calls "fitting," which is called <term>analogy</term> <note>original: "αναλογία" (analogia)</note> by the Greeks.

<margin>Fitting <note>Proportion</note></margin>
The "fitting" is twofold: one is **continuous**, the other is **discontinuous** <note>original: "distans"</note>. A **continuous** fitting is that which joins the extremes by a common middle limit. A **discontinuous** fitting is that which separates the extremes by two middle limits. A **ratio** <note>original: "ratio"</note> is the relationship of two limits to one another, a sort of agreement. Therefore, a "fitting" <note>proportion</note> exists from the comparison of several ratios. And the **continuous** fitting is found in three limits, at the minimum: as the first is to the second, so the second is to the third. That is: as eight is to four, so four is to two. Of these limits, the one that is the middle, when multiplied by itself, is equal to the square produced by the multiplication of the extremes. For twice eight is sixteen, and four times four is the same. However, the **discontinuous** fitting is found in four limits at the minimum: as the first is to the second, so the third is to the fourth; that is, as eight is to four, so six is to three. For we cannot say, repeating it, "so four is to six." In these, just as in the limits of the continuous fitting, the product of the extremes is equal to that which is born from the calculation of the means. For three times eight makes the sum of 24; equally, six times four creates the same sum. He uses, therefore, the method and remedy of the continuous fitting. This is because its nature is connective and a unifier of distant boundaries, similar to the reasoning used by the Fabricator <note>The Demiurge or Creator in Plato's philosophy</note> of the sensible world when he inserted a middle of air and water between the world's extreme boundaries of fire and earth.

<margin>That is, a joined composition of unlike things.</margin>

<image-desc>A mathematical diagram consisting of arcs connecting numbers to demonstrate proportions. On the left, arcs connect 8, 4, and 2 with the label "Continuous". On the right, arcs connect 8, 6, 4, and 3 with the label "Discontinuous".</image-desc>

Description of two distant parallelepipeds: which parallelepipeds are continued into one solidity by the insertion of two others according to the ratio of the continuous fitting, which is called **continuous analogy** <note>original: "αναλογία συνεχής"</note> by the Greeks.

Thus far, this description demonstrates two solid bodies—which geometers call **parallelepipeds** <note>A three-dimensional figure formed by six parallelograms, like a slanted box.</note>—joined together by the insertion of two other similar solids according to the principle of a fitting continuum, which will become clear both from their joining together and their separation.

Upon this quadrature, another square is superimposed, which is contained by the letters **ε ζ θ η**. The letters **β** and **α** are connected; likewise **ζ** and **β**, **θ** and **Δ**, and **η** and **Γ**. Once this is done, one solid or **parallelepiped** is completed. Another similar to it is described in this way: through line **β θ**, line **θ Δ** is extended, and likewise through **Δ Θ**, line **Θ Ξ** is extended; through **η θ**, line **Θ Κ** is extended; and from point **κ**, line **κ μ** is extended. Likewise from point **μ**, line **μ Λ** is drawn; from point **Ξ**, line **Ξ ο** is extended; and from these same points **ο**, two lines are drawn straight out.

One line is **Ξ Ν**, the other **ο Π**, and they are equal: line **Ξ Λ** to line **β θ**, line **Θ Ξ** to line **Δ Θ**, and line **Θ Κ** to line **η θ**. Likewise, the parallelograms **χ Θ Λ μ**, **Ξ ο Π**, are equal, and **Ν Κ Π μ ο Λ Ξ ο** are connected. In this way, another solid will be born from this description. I say, therefore, that between these two similar solids, two other similar solids are found according to the ratio of a fitting continuum. For I draw through line **Ν Ξ** the line **Ξ Χ**, through line **Π ο** the line **ο Φ**, through line **μ Λ** the line **Λ Τ**, through **Α Δ** the line **Δ Ζ**, and through line **β Γ** the line **Γ ρ**. Let these be equal: **Ν Ξ** to **Ξ Χ**, **π ο** to **ο Φ**, **μ Λ** to **Λ Τ**, **Α Δ** to **Δ Ζ**, and **β Γ** to **Γ ρ**. The parallelograms are also equal, which are in the same quadratures, and **χ ρ ξ λ φ τ τ ρ** and any others are connected, and there will be four solids expressly connected.

To the extent that this is proved: just as side **β θ** is to side **Θ Δ**, so in length will parallelogram **β ζ θ η** be to parallelogram **Θ η Τ Δ**. Therefore, the solid **Α Β Γ Δ Ε ζ η θ** will also be to the solid **Δ Γ ρ Ξ η Τ Δ**. But the sides included above have a fitting relationship to each other, seeing as they are under the same quadratures. The quadratures likewise fit each other because they are under the same height, as are the solids. Therefore, the aforementioned will have a fitting equality <note>original: "parilitatem," meaning a proportional symmetry or equality</note> toward each other.

Again, as side **Α Θ** is to side **θ ζ** in height, so will parallelogram **Θ Α Γ η** be to parallelogram **Θ Ξ τ η**. Therefore, the solid **Δ Γ Β Ξ Θ η Τ Δ** will also be to the solid **Θ η Τ Λ Ξ χ Φ ο**. But the sides included above have a fitting relationship to each other, seeing as they are under the same quadratures. The quadratures also fit each other because they are under the same height; therefore, the aforementioned solids will have a fitting equality toward each other.

Again, as side **η θ** is to side **Θ κ** in height, so will parallelogram **Θ η Γ Α** be to parallelogram **χ Θ Λ μ**. Therefore, the solid **Θ η Τ Λ Ξ χ Φ ο** will also be to the solid **χ Θ Λ μ η Ξ ο Π**. But the sides included above have an **analogy** <note>original: "analogiam," a proportional relationship</note> between them, seeing as they are under the same quadratures. The quadratures also fit each other because they are under the same height; therefore, the aforementioned solids will have a fitting equality toward each other. Thus, as the solid **Α Β Γ Δ, Ε ζ η θ** is to **Δ Γ ρ Ξ θ η**...

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

A, thus this very solid according to the <note>original: "$\Theta$ P T A $\Xi$ O"</note> solid. And likewise this very one according to the <note>original: "K $\Theta$ $\Lambda$ M H $\Xi$ O $\Pi$"</note> solid. In this manner it has been shown that for two similar parallelepiped solids, two middle parallelepiped solids are found according to the ratio of a fitting continuum; which is what needed to be shown.

<margin>A Question.</margin> Someone might say: it is sufficiently proven to him that two solid bodies separated by a longer interval are joined by two interjected means according to the ratio of a fitting continuum; but between fire and earth, which are solid bodies, no similarity appears, since according to Plato himself the form and figure of fire is said to be pyramidal <note>The pyramid (tetrahedron) was associated with fire in Platonic physics due to its sharpness.</note>, that is, it grows into the shape of a pyramid; while earth is a cube. Furthermore, these forms borrow no similarity from one another: because they do not have equal angles, since every cube has right angles. Therefore, because earth is in a cubic figure, it must necessarily have right angles. But indeed, the angles of a pyramid are more extended <margin>at the summit</margin> at the summit. Where, however, the angles are not equal, not even the sides will be in proportion; and for that reason, they will not be similar to one another. If other interjected solids are to connect these distant bodies, the continuation will be hindered in this way. For it is not just any two bodies, but those which are similar to each other, that ought to have solids inserted between them.

<margin>The Answer.</margin> To which it will be answered that we ought to be mindful of those things by which Plato, having foreseen this very difficulty long before, recalled those who would think this way from their error. For he said, if we remember, that similarity ought to be sought not only in forms and figures, but also in powers and qualities; when he spoke thus: "When in three things, whether numbers, or masses, or powers, the middle is to the lowest just as the highest is to the middle." <note>A reference to Timaeus 31c-32a regarding geometric proportion.</note> Therefore, if between fire and earth there is no similarity in appearance or, as it were, in face, it must be sought in the natures and qualities of the elements themselves, according to which they act and are acted upon; and in those properties by which the force and kinship of each element is chiefly designated.

<margin>On the properties of the elements.</margin> There are, therefore, many other properties of both fire and earth; but those which most especially declare the force of their properties are surely these: Of fire, sharpness, because it is acute and penetrating; then that it is thin, with a certain delicate subtlety; then that it is mobile, and always <margin>Blunted.</margin> in motion. Of earth, however, that it is blunted, that it is corporeal, and that it is always immobile. These natures, although they are contrary, nevertheless have a certain parity arising from that very contrariety. For similar things are compared to similar, just as dissimilar things are compared to dissimilar; and this is the analogy, that is, the ratio, of the fitting continuum. For what sharpness is against bluntness, this subtlety is against corporeality; and what subtlety is against corporeality, this mobility is against immobility. And if you turn it so that what is the middle becomes the extreme, and those which are extremes are placed individually in the middle, the rule of analogy will be preserved. To the extent, therefore, that these two solid bodies—whose similarity is such as we have demonstrated—will make a continuation by two other interjected solids according to the ratio of a fitting continuum, the discipline of arithmetic teaches. For if we wish to inquire what the element neighboring fire is, and from what it is composed: we shall take from fire its two nearest virtues, subtlety and mobility; and one from earth, that is, bluntness; and the generation of the second element will be found, which is below fire, that is, air. For air is blunt, subtle, and mobile. And again, if we consider the generation of that element which is neighboring earth, that is, water: we shall take two virtues of earth, that is, bluntness and corporeality; and one of fire, that is, motion; and the substance of water will arise, which is a body blunt, corporeal, and mo...

<summary>Chalcidius explains how fire and earth, despite having different geometric shapes (pyramid vs. cube), are linked through a mathematical proportion of their physical qualities—sharpness, subtlety, and motion—allowing for the creation of air and water as intermediate elements.</summary>

# On Plato’s Timaeus. Folio XVII.

...ble. And thus between fire and earth: air and water are born from the bonding of the extremes. From these, the continuity of the World is established. Furthermore, in this way, the geometric proportion is also preserved according to the principle of a fitting continuum. For as fire is to air, so air is to water; and finally, water is to earth. And backward: as earth is to water, so water is to air, and air is to fire. <note>This describes the "Golden Chain" of elements where each element shares a quality with its neighbor, allowing the universe to hold together as a single mathematical harmony.</note>

---

Thus far, the discussion has concerned the constitution of the worldly body and the kinship of the materials from which it consists. Since he <note>Plato</note> affirms that the world was made because it is corporeal—and yet that it is indissoluble, even though things that are made and born are usually dissolved once made, and perish once born—he provides a remedy for this opinion (which goes against human expectation). He declares by whom it was made, from what it consists, according to what model it was established, for what reason, and to what extent it is extended into eternity.

---

<margin>
Because the world and all things
</margin>
For all things that exist are either the works of God, or of nature, or of a human craftsman imitating nature. The origin and beginning of natural works are seeds <note>original: "semina"</note>, which are understood to be made either in the bowels of the earth for the production of tree-fruits and grain, or as the conceived germ of animals through the fertility of the reproductive members. The birth of all these things occurs in time. For the birth of nature and of time are equal and of the same age. Thus, the works of nature, because they have an origin from that time in which they began to be, have also been allotted an end and a passing away within the series and continuation of that time. 

But truly, the origin and beginning of the works of God is incomprehensible. For there is no certain mark, no evidence of time from which they began to be. Only the cause, perhaps, is known, and even this is scarcely understood—why any of them exists or for what reason. It is certain, indeed, that nothing was made by God without a cause. Therefore, just as seeds are the foundations for those things procreated by the law of nature, so the foundations of those things which God instituted are the *causes*, which are the clear signs of divine providence. 

Moreover, God exists before the institution of time, and through eternity <note>original: "aeuum," referring to non-temporal duration</note>. For time is the image of eternity. Therefore, the causes of all the works of God are older than time; and just as God exists through eternity, so also do the causes exist through eternity. It follows that whatever is made by God is not temporary; and what is not temporary is held by no law of time. Time brings about the change of age through disease, and old age through passing away. Therefore, that which is instituted by God is immune to all these things; its origin is causal, not temporal. And the sensible world is the work of God. Therefore, its origin is causal, not temporal. Thus the sensible world, although it is corporeal, having been made and instituted by God, is eternal.

---

Furthermore, it consists of materials that are pure and whole: from *all* fire, he says, and from the *entirety* of spirit <note>air</note>, and from the rest, without the lessening of any part. Thus it happens that no part, not even the smallest, of any body is left outside the circuit of the world. Because all these bodies are partly cold and partly hot, no improper arrival of cold or heat from the outside can bring sickness to the World. Therefore, placed beyond the necessity of inconvenience, it is eternal. But the nature of a body is fluid; and the World consists of a body. However, the loss and death of parts is not found in the "flow" itself, but in "out-flowing"—that is, flowing outside. It loses...

<summary>This page explains the geometric harmony of the four elements and argues that the universe is eternal because it was created by God based on eternal causes rather than temporal seeds.</summary>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the relationship between the physical world and the eternal model, and the nature of the World Soul.</meta>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

Indeed, that which has fallen away from the whole is restored. Since all things are gathered back within the world, nothing certainly exists outside the world's embrace. Thus, that which flows according to the nature of a body has no place to flow out to. It flows in, therefore, rather than out; and returning at last to the sum of the universe, it provides a restoration for parts exhausted by the long passage of time.

<margin>D</margin> ¶ What of the fact that it was established according to the pattern of another intelligible and immutable eternity? Now no one doubts this: those things which are established in the likeness of an eternal model possess a likeness of perpetuity; and perpetuity exists in eternity <note>original: "æuo". Chalcidius distinguishes between *aevum* (eternal, timeless existence) and *tempus* (linear time).</note>. Therefore, the model is the intelligible world through eternity, while that which was established according to the model—namely, the sensible world—exists through time. And indeed, it is <margin>The property of time and eternity.</margin> characteristic of time to move forward. The characteristic of eternity is to remain still, always persisting in the same state. Likewise, the parts of time are days, months, nights, and years; eternity has no parts. Likewise, the species of time are past, present, and future. The substance of eternity is uniform in a single and perpetual present. The intelligible world, therefore, always is. This world, its image, always was, is, and shall be.

<margin>E</margin> From here, Plato proceeds to the treatise on the World Soul. He first takes care of that which was <margin>On the origin of the soul.</margin> discussed out of order regarding the constitution of its body before the inspiration of the soul was addressed: since it is established that the soul is more ancient than every body. Nor does he depart from his purpose: because he asserted the world was made by God, he says its soul was also made by God. He is observed to do this in this book alone—to devise births for eternal things. I believe he does so lest, if men hear there are certain things born from an origin, they might ever think the supremacy of the Highest God is diminished; if they were of the same and equal excellence of antiquity as God, not knowing that the origin of eternal things is spoken of far differently than that of perishable things. For the authority and origin of mortals is that which precedes the birth of others, as a father is the origin of a son, and founders are the origins of peoples and cities. But truly, the origin and art of divine races and eternal nations is considered not in the anticipation of time, but in the eminence of dignity. Either because we are accustomed to ascribe the authority and power of a finished work to the hands rather than to the disposition of the mind; or because many, judging divine nature by their own mortal nature, think that all things are born, and that there is nothing which is not born. It would be difficult to persuade minds so conditioned that God is the author of the world, unless He constructed it like some craftsman with His hands and the labor of other arts. Certainly, I think the purpose of the writer must be considered. For if he were primarily acting in this book to prove the soul is immortal, it would not be fitting to devise a birth for the soul, because all things that are born perish. But truly, since the constitution of the World—that is, of the universal thing—is being treated, it was relevant for the present to introduce its soul as being born and brought, as it were, into the light; so that those listening might imagine some form of the soul, just as they see the form of all things that are born. ¶ And so, God, he says, devised a third kind of soul in this manner: From the indivisible substance that always persists in its own state, and from another substance which, as the inseparable companion of bodies, is thought to divide itself through those same bodies, He placed a third mixed kind of substance in the middle between...

<summary>Chalcidius explains the distinction between the eternal, intelligible world and the temporal, sensible world, while justifying Plato's use of "birth" or "origin" when describing the eternal World Soul as a teaching tool rather than a literal temporal beginning.</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, eternity vs time, intelligible world, sensible world, Plato's Timaeus, Chalcidius, divine origin, immortality of the soul</keywords>
