# On Plato's Timaeus. Folio XVIII.
<sig>c ii</sig>

...both substance, and so forth. What is it that he says? He teaches us that substance—or as
<margin>G</margin>
Cicero calls it, *essence*—is twofold. One is indivisible, the other divisible through bodies.
The indivisible is indeed that kind to which all eternal and incorporeal things belong,
which are called "intelligible" <note>Things graspable by the mind rather than the senses.</note>. The divisible, however, is that which is the cause of existence for bodies. For every
single body certainly exists; and that which exists has essence. And since bodies are many,
there is one divisible essence found in all bodies. Therefore, that which is found as one
among many is rightly thought to split itself, and is rightly called "divisible." Therefore, from these two,
he says God the Craftsman mixed a third kind of essence, and placed it as a medium between
both substances. Similarly, he teaches that nature is also not simple, but "two-membered"
<margin>Two-membered nature.</margin>
or bipartite; and from these two, by the command of the Craftsman, he caused a third kind of nature to exist: which, being equally
two-membered, was placed as a medium between both substances, namely the indivisible and the divisible.
To what extent, then, is nature two-membered? He says all natural things are either the "same" as themselves, or
"different": the same in genus, but different in species. For example, a human and a horse are the same
in that both are animals. But they are different in species. For one of them
is a rational animal; the other is irrational and mute. One is a biped; the other
a quadruped. Therefore, all these existing things—the Same, Essence, and the Different—God mixed
(he says) into one species: and from the three he made one: the Same, the Different, and the Divisible.
That "Different" nature was difficult to manage, resisting the mixing and uniting of the
kinds. And again, he divided that one composite of three into parts, so that each
individual part possessed those three powers: that is, so that the "Same" was not only the same, but
also substance, and had something of "Difference"; and "Essence" was both essence and
<margin>H</margin>
the "Same" and the "Different." The "Different" also was both different and essence, and had some-
<margin>Divisible and indivisible substance.</margin>
thing of that which is called the "Same." ¶However, it was debated by the ancients what exactly
Plato meant by "indivisible substance" and "divisible substance," from which God, the maker of the
sensible world, forged the third kind of substance, and for what reason he fused them. Did he call the
form of the intelligible world—according to whose likeness he transferred the shapes conceived in the mind to
bodies—the "indivisible substance"? And the "divisible" substance, the *sylva* <note>original: "sylvam"; referring to "hyle" or raw primordial matter.</note>
which is like the source and fountain of bodies? So that the third mixed kind of substance
should be understood as the "Form" by which both the world and the other
bodies the world contains are shaped? Or rather, did Plato judge the "indivisible substance" to be
the higher Soul, which is not subject to any embodiment, and whose venerable
purity is violated by no contagion of the body? And did he call that soul "divisible substance"
which gives vital vigor not only to all animals, but even to shrubs and trees? So that from the two
is forged a third, rational kind of soul, lest all things that obtained life—
namely animals—should be mute and lack reason. Instead, there would be a kind of living being
capable of reason, discipline, and intellect, which, understanding the admirable logic
and arrangement of the divine work, might venerate the author of the world's creation.
Therefore, those who thought the form of the intelligible world (because it is always the same and never changes)
was called "indivisible substance" by Plato, and the *sylva* was the "divisible,"
argued that this lacked logic when the generation of the world was being established. For a suitable soul
was being sought for the Living Being <note>The Universe as a whole.</note> that was soon to be; and that soul which gives life
to mute animals, shrubs, and trees was not yet said by Plato to exist, as animals were not yet born,
nor indeed were the trees and other things to which it would give life. For that reason, they held that the
indivisible substance was the form of the intelligible world, in whose likeness [the world] was shaped...

<summary>This page explores Plato's metaphysical construction of the World Soul, debating whether the "indivisible" and "divisible" components refer to the distinction between pure intellect and matter, or between higher rational souls and lower vegetative life forces.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Timaeus, World Soul, Essence, Indivisible Substance, Divisible Substance, Rationality, Hyle, Cosmology, Cicero</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the composition of the World Soul and the mathematical ratios of its creation.</meta>

# XVIII
### Chalcidius’s Interpretation

They used to say that Plato named the **sensible world** <note>The physical world perceived by the senses.</note> as such; furthermore, they called it the **divisible matter** <note>original: "sylva" (forest/wood), used here as a technical term for prime matter.</note>. But those who asserted that a third kind of soul—the **rational** kind—was compounded from a **divisible substance** (that is, one free from the incorporation of the soul) and another **inseparable companion of bodies** (that is, the material or "vegetative" soul), stood by their own opinion in this way. First of all, they argued it would be backwards if, after the constitution of the worldly body had been treated in the preceding sections and that discussion was finished, one arrived at the **birth of the soul** which was to give life to the sensible world, only to then go backward to the earlier discussion so that the treatment of matter, the bodies of the world, and their forms could begin anew, thus receding from the birth of the soul. Secondly, they argued it is said in vain that the soul could be the **material source** <margin>the world,</margin> of the world when it could not yet exist, for the reason that the living beings it was meant to animate did not yet exist. For they held that the power of both soul and body had always existed, and that God did not make the world out of things that did not exist, but rather **ordered** those things which were without order and limit. Therefore, He adorned those things which existed rather than generating those which were not. Indeed, by assigning **intellect**, He reduced the unordered errors of the soul and its agitation (which is like waves) from disordered tossing to order. He also reined in the unstable motion of the body with a healthy and moderate agitation, and gave to it a form and figure that was congruent and a fitting adornment; as He Himself said in the earlier passages, deeming the lot of ordered things better than the unordered. Wherefore, since a soul fitting for the sensible world is established, its origin proceeds from one **indivisible** part, which is mind and intellect, and another which is **divided** and sheared through all bodies. This soul is located as a **mean** between the indivisible and the divisible, so that while it remains free from incorporation in the **intelligible world** <note>The realm of pure ideas/forms.</note> forever, it might also assist the material nature of things that are mute and likewise feel nothing. This "mean" soul—because it was necessary that there be a genus of animals in the world that used **reason**—would minister life and spirit to this same genus. Placed between two natures, it might contemplate the same and yet different divinity of that nature, lifting its gaze to the sublime and then to the depths; and turning toward the seat of a different nature, it would dispense these things according to the decrees of the Craftsman <note>The Demiurge.</note> and impart **providence** to earthly things. Then, proceeding further, Plato demonstrates the **partition** itself. "One portion," he says, "he first took from the whole. After which, a second portion double the first. A third, indeed, one-and-a-half times the second, but triple the first. Then he took a fourth, double the second. A fifth, triple the third. The sixth was an assumption of parts seven times more inclined than the first. The seventh was twenty-six parts greater than the first."

->In this form, the likeness of the soul's powers results as if from a mirror<-

<margin>The Psychogonia figure</margin>
<note>This "Lambda" diagram represents the mathematical harmony of the soul's creation using two geometric progressions: 1, 2, 4, 8 and 1, 3, 9, 27.</note>
         I
      II   III
    IIII   VIIII
  VIII     XXVII

This **partition** is indeed a consideration of the powers and an ordering, as if of its members,
<margin>The principal disciplines</margin>
actions, and offices; and it designates the congruence of all duties, with three principal disciplines asserting this: **Geometry**,
<margin>Geometry.</margin>
**Arithmetic**, and **Music** <note>original: "harmonica".</note>. Of these, Geometry holds the place of the foundations, while the others serve as the superstructure. For that which has no parts,
<margin>The Line.</margin>
and therefore falls under no senses, yet exists and is discerned by the mind, the Geometers call a **point** <note>original: "notam".</note>. A **line**, however, is length without breadth, which is defined by its points.
<margin>The Surface</margin>
Furthermore, that in which there is some breadth, they call a **surface**; so that a surface is length that has obtained breadth.
<margin>The Body.</margin>
That which consists of three dimensions—that is, length, breadth, and depth—they name a **solid body**. Therefore, this description, which outlines the birth or "joining together" of the parts from which the soul is said to consist, shows the ratio by which the soul and body are joined. Indeed, the body of living beings, which is inspired by the vigor of the soul, certainly has a surface and also has solidity. Therefore, the soul, which was to penetrate both the surface and the solidity with vital vigor, required powers similar to solidity and similar to the surface.

<summary>Chalcidius explains the composition of the World Soul as a "mean" between indivisible intellect and divisible matter, using the Platonic "Lambda" ratios to illustrate its mathematical harmony. He argues that the soul's structure mirrors the geometric dimensions of the physical bodies it is meant to animate.</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, Timaeus, Chalcidius, Psychogonia, Lambda diagram, Geometry, Arithmetic, Harmony, Demiurge, Intelligible World, Sensible World</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the mathematical division of the World Soul into harmonic proportions.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus

<margin>Portion</margin>ought to have. Since like is gathered to like. "First, therefore," he says, "he took one portion from the whole." From which whole? That which was reduced from three into one form, as demonstrated above—namely, from Essence, the Same, and the Different—he began to divide. The phrasing of "portion" is also to be noted. For he did not say a "part," as if of a simple and incorporeal thing, but a "portion"—that is, he said it was taken up in the manner of a part, which is similar to a part of a geometric sign <note>original: "geometricae illius notae pars." Likely referring to a point or unit in a diagram.</note>. Afterward, he took a double of that which he had first taken: that is, two, which are placed below the one on the left side of the formula. Third, he took a sesquialter <note>A ratio of 3:2 (one and a half)</note> of the second, but a triple of the first taken. Justly so. For three excels two by the sesquialter ratio. For three consists of two, and likewise one, which is the half part of two. Fourth, he took a double of the second, because twice two is four. Fifth, a triple of the third, which is nine. For three times three are nine. The sixth taking was by seven parts more inclined than the first, that is, in the number eight, which is the last on that side which is ordered from even numbers. The seventh was greater than the first by twenty-six parts: that is, twenty-seven, which number is worth as much as the number eight, as I shall show. Therefore, the apex of numbers is singularity <note>The number one</note> without any parts, like a geometric point; its double is a line, length without width; the double of a line is a surface, which is extension with width; the double of which is a cube. A body divided through length, width, and depth: twice two, twice [two], which is eight. And in the same way, according to the triple ratio, the brotherhood of numbers is found on the other side. And the cube which is the sum of twenty-seven is the first cube of an odd number. For three times three, three times, produces the aforementioned sum. Therefore, according to the ratio of a fitting continuum, which is a geometric ratio: as eight is to four, so is four to two, and two to one.

<margin>Question</margin> ¶ At this point, it is first asked why he extended the division so far as to establish seven limits, with three intervals on both sides—both in that side ordered from double and even numbers, and in that from triple and odd numbers. Next, what numbers ought to be assigned to the parts. For if we always place singularity as the first part, and the rest following the description, we will by no means find between these numbers the two others which complete the entire continuation of intervals as he commands to be done. Third, what the form of the diagram <note>original: "descriptionis"</note> ought to be. Therefore, regarding what we said is usually asked in the first of the three places, this must first be demonstrated: that the seven limits are reasonably arranged, containing six intervals. Moreover, the proof will rely on authorities. 

<margin>On the number seven</margin> For the number seven <note>original: "septenarius"</note> is praised by the Pythagoreans as the best, most natural, and most sufficient. For the perfect number is indeed ten, because starting from singularity we count up to the number ten; but the remaining counting is not so much counting as a replication of those same numbers we used before in counting. Eleven and twelve and other such numbers are born from the replication of the preceding ones. This "decuman" number <note>The number ten (the Tetractys)</note> 

<margin>The first squaring</margin> the Pythagoreans call the "first squaring," because it consists of the first four numbers: one, two, three, and four. The ratio of harmony <note>original: "Symphoniae"</note> also flows from these same numbers that complete the decuman number, as if from a certain fountain. Indeed, from these are born the epitritic, sesquialter, double, triple, and quadruple numbers and sounds. The epitritic <note>A ratio of 4:3</note> indeed, such as four against three, contains the whole number three and its third part, which is one. The sesquialter <note>A ratio of 3:2</note> such as three against two, contains the whole dual number and its half part, which is one. The double, as two against one. The triple, as three against one. And the quadruple, as four to one. Moreover, the epitritus in calculating is the same as what is called the *diatessaron* <note>A musical fourth</note> in singing; the sesquialter is the same as the *dia* [pente] <note>A musical fifth; the text cuts off at "dya"</note>

<summary>This page explains the mathematical construction of the World Soul in Plato's Timaeus, using the Pythagorean Lambda to arrange numbers in even (double) and odd (triple) series. It justifies the use of the number seven and explains how the first four numbers (1, 2, 3, 4) form the basis of all musical and geometric proportions.</summary>

# XIX. Chalcidius’s Interpretation
->m 3<-

<note>The text discusses musical intervals: the "fifth" (diapente) and the "octave" (diapason).</note> is called the fifth in singing. The double ratio is the octave. The quadruple ratio is the double octave.
Next, another property of the number seven is considered, which other numbers 
<margin><unclear>entirely</unclear></margin>
do not have. For while other numbers contained within the limit of ten either produce other numbers, are produced by others, or both produce and are produced; 
the number seven alone neither begets another number from itself within the limit of ten, nor is it born from any other. For two doubled begets the number four; three is born from no doubled number, but when doubled it begets the number six. The 
number four is both born and begets: it is born from twice two, and when doubled it begets the number eight. Again, the number five is born from no number counted twice, but when counted twice it begets ten. Likewise, the number six is born from a doubled three, but begets no one within the limit of ten. Eight is born from four counted twice, but begets no one. Nine is born from three times three, but begets no one. Ten is born from the doubled number five, and it further begets no one. Thus, while all others are either being born, or begetting, or both; the number seven alone is neither born from the doubling of another, nor does it beget any within the limit of ten.
<margin>The excellence of the number seven</margin>
For this reason, it was named **Minerva** <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom, born fully grown from the head of Jupiter without a mother, mirroring seven's "unbegotten" nature.</note> by the ancients. Like her, it is without a mother and is a perpetual virgin. It is considered natural and best for this reason: because many things which come forth by the law of nature are noted to happen according to this number. First, 
<margin><unclear>entirely</unclear></margin>
births in the seventh month are the first legitimate ones among the offspring of the human race. Next, after birth, teeth are driven out in the seventh month, and are changed in the seventh year. Likewise, the second week of years <note>Age 14</note> brings puberty to both sexes, the maturity for begetting and bearing. In the third week of years, the bloom and down appear around the cheeks. In the fourth week, the increases in height are finished. In the fifth, the full perfection of youthful age. In illnesses also, experience and practice have taught that changes occur according to this same number. Hippocrates <note>The famous Greek physician.</note> mentions this often in many of his books, and clearly in those he wrote *On Weeks*; he also counts the seven passages of all the senses in the head: of the eyes, ears, nostrils, and mouth. The vital organs also equal this number: the tongue, lung, heart, spleen, liver, and two kidneys. The number of vowel letters is the same, by which the harshness of consonants is smoothed. That multiform transformation of the waxing and waning Moon is also placed in this same number. For from being dark, it becomes crescent-shaped as the light grows; then halved; then more than half; then full; and back again to more than half, halved, and crescent. What more? In the world there are seven planets distant from each other by a musical melody, as he himself testifies when he says God cut the circle of a diverse nature six times and fabricated seven unequal orbs, which are turned by contrary motions and agitations according to the spaces of the double and triple ratios, in which he places the Sun, the Moon, and the other wandering fires. Such is the reason for the excellence of the seven limits.
<margin>The excellence of the number three</margin>
Regarding the three intervals, they say this is the reason: Again, the number three is the best in another kind. For it is the first and perfect before all others, having a beginning, a middle, and an end, by which bodies grow and according to which bodily increases proceed. Since one interval makes a line, two a surface, and three a body—divided and divisible—than which nothing is more perfect. For these three intervals complete a body: length, breadth, and solidity. Certainly, all things were first said to be "three," which cannot be said of "two" <note>Referring to the grammatical distinction where "both" is used for two, but "all" begins at three.</note>. For "both" is said of two. These three intervals, therefore, on both sides of the description...

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato’s Timaeus, specifically focusing on the mathematical and harmonic construction of the World Soul (Anima Mundi). It follows the discussion of the initial division of the soul's substance into geometric and arithmetic progressions.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus

->**The Soul's Composition**<-

...thus: they create six intervals, and the number six, being full and perfect, rightly holds this position. For it is equal to the parts of which it is composed: it has a half in three, a third part in two, and a sixth in one; which, when gathered together into one, complete the number itself. <note>A "perfect number" in ancient mathematics is one that equals the sum of its divisors (1+2+3=6).</note> Finally, the square that is born from it—that is, the number thirty-six—is called the "Second Quadrature," consisting of four odd numbers (one, three, five, and seven) and likewise of four even numbers (two, four, six, and eight). Again, the number fifty-four, which arises from the triangle described above, is named the "Third Quadrature" because it contains four limits on the double side—that is, one, two, four, and eight—and four others on the side composed of triples: one, three, nine, and twenty-seven. The "singularity" <note>The number one (monad).</note> is accepted as common to both, being the head of each side. Therefore, the singularity is considered both even and odd. Rightly, then, seven limits are established, while three intervals are perceived through each side. For the singularity is the beginning of both even and odd numbers; because it is held to be both even and odd, it is believed to contain within itself all forms of numbers: <term>epipedam</term> <note>original: "epipedam" — a plane or two-dimensional figure.</note> (plane), triangular, and cubic.

It would be tedious to pursue each of these individually. Let it suffice, therefore, to have demonstrated the logic of the nascent soul which was destined for incorporation: that, beginning from the individual and incorporeal singularity, it grew step by step through the line and the surface until it reached a perfect body, through the intervals of length, breadth, and depth. Because of this, it penetrates both the subtle and the solid bodies of the sensible world. Now we must provide the reason for that triangular form in which there are seven limits and six intervals of double and triple quantity. I say that no figure is more fit than this one, in which the singularity, placed upon the peak, is considered to hold the summit and the citadel. Through it, as if through a small opening, a great river might flow from the bosom of the perennial fount of provident intelligence; and the singularity itself may be understood to be Mind, or Intelligence, or God the Creator himself. For since it is the origin of numbers and provides substance to all things from itself, it contains within itself their ratios, both simple and multiplied. While other numbers change through increases and decreases and depart from their own nature, the singularity alone remains by unshakable right and persists in its own state. Always the same, always immutable, and always a singularity—just as all divine things, which are changed by no progression of time, exist forever in unattainable happiness. Next, Plato continues:

*When these were thus divided, he consequently filled up the intervals of double and triple quantity, still cutting portions from the whole. And from these, filling the spaces of the intervals so that each individual interval was supported by two means. Furthermore, one of the means exceeded the lowest limit by the same fraction of the limit that it was exceeded by the other furthest limit. The other mean exceeded and was exceeded by the limits by a sum that was equal in number.*

Just as those who play musical instruments harmoniously, between the two limits of the outermost strings—the lowest <term>hypates</term> <note>The lowest note in the Greek tetrachord.</note> and the highest <term>netes</term> <note>The highest note in the Greek tetrachord.</note>—weave other middle strings with varying sound and ringing; these middle strings are higher than the first limit but lower than the last. Thus, through the accent, one reaches the final sound of the peak, where the stroke of the fingers and the plectrum wanders. So God, tuning the Soul of the World, introduced those six intervals of double and triple quantity, crowding each with two means: so that one of the two means is greater than the smaller limit by a third part of it, but lower than the larger limit by that same third part of the larger limit.

Therefore, a figure similar to the one written a little further above must be described.

<margin>c iii</margin>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

distant from it by its own numbers, so that the larger numbers are contained: the intervals of which receive an insertion of two means <note>The "means" refer to the arithmetic and harmonic means used to bridge the gaps between the base numbers of the Platonic scale.</note>.

->[Diagram of the Platonic Lambda]<-
<note>The diagram illustrates the progression of numbers (2, 3, 4, 8, 9, 27) and their intermediate harmonic values. The apex starts at 6, branching into sequences that define musical and geometric ratios.</note>

Because the number 6 creates one limit and likewise 12 creates a second limit according to the ratio of double quantity <note>a 1:2 ratio, or an octave</note>, and they are distant from each other: two means are interposed, one of the number 8, the other of the number 9. Therefore, when the number 8 is compared against the limit of 6: it exceeds it by a third part of that same limit of six, that is, by two. It is itself exceeded by a third part of the limit of 12, that is, by four. Thus, by whatever proportion the one mean of 8 exceeds the limit of six, by that same proportion it is exceeded by the limit of 12. Again, the other mean of 9 exceeds the limit of 6 by three numbers: and it is exceeded by the limit of 12 by those same three: and not without reason. For 8 compared against 6 is greater by an *epitritus* <term>epitritus: a ratio of 4:3, known in music as a perfect fourth</term>. They call an *epitritus* an interval whenever, in a comparison of numbers, the larger contains the whole of the smaller within itself plus its third part. Equally, the limit of 12 compared against 9, one of the means, is found to be its *epitritus*. For it contains the whole of 9 and its third part <note>9 + 3 = 12</note>. And again, the interval between the two means is an *epogdous* <term>epogdous: a ratio of 9:8, representing a whole tone in music</term>. For the number 9 contains the whole of 8 within itself and its eighth part, which is one. Thus the two means exceed the limits, and are exceeded by the limits, by a double and diverse ratio: one by parts, the other by numbers, as has been shown. This same ratio is also found in the triple numbers. For the limit of 6, being distant from the other limit of 18, demonstrates that the interval lying between is triple <note>a 1:3 ratio</note> on the other side of the formula. Here also are two means: one in the number 9, the other in 12. The mean of 9, compared against the limit of 6: surpasses it by a *sescuplus* <term>sescuplus: a ratio of 3:2, known as a perfect fifth</term>. For it has the whole of 6 and its half part, that is, 3. The same 9, compared against the limit of 18, is exceeded by double. For twice nine is eighteen. Again, the other mean of 12, if compared against the limit of 6, exceeds it by double. But if compared against the other which is larger, that is 18, it is found to be smaller by six numbers: which was equally greater than the limit of 6 by six numbers. This same ratio is found among other limits distant by a double ratio, that is 12 and 24. For here also the mean of 16 exceeds the limit of 12 by the ratio of an *epitritus*. The limit of 24 also exceeds the mean of 18 by the ratio of an *epitritus*: and again, the limit of 24 is distant from 48 by a double ratio: and it has means, one of 32, the other of 36. The mean of 32 compared against the limit of 24 makes an interval of an *epitritus* quantity. For it has the whole of 24 and its third part, that is, eight. The limit of 48 is the *epitritus* of the number 36, which is the other mean. For it contains in itself the whole number 36 and its third part, that is, 12. For 36 and 12 complete the number 48. This same ratio is found also on that side which is arranged from triple numbers. Therefore, because 9 and 8 create the space of an *epogdous*: and from these the two limits distant by a double ratio, that is 6 and 12, are filled, he rightly said that the intervals of all *epitriti* are filled by the space of an *epogdous*. For again, the number 18 holds the ratio of an *epogdous* against the number 16: from which two means the *epogdous*...

<summary>Chalcidius explains the mathematical ratios (4:3, 3:2, and 9:8) used to bridge the gaps between the primary numbers of the World Soul's composition. He demonstrates how these "means" create harmonic balance between the double and triple intervals described in Plato's Timaeus.</summary>
<keywords>Chalcidius, Timaeus, harmonic mean, arithmetic mean, epitritus, epogdous, musical ratios, Platonic lambda, World Soul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the mathematical and musical ratios used by the Demiurge to construct the World Soul.</meta>

# On Plato's Timaeus. Folio XXI.

<column-break/>

...having the space of an *epogdous* <note>original: "epogdous"; a ratio of 9:8</note>, the interval is filled by a limit of 12 and an equal limit of 24. Again, the space between the limits of 24 and 48 is filled by two *epogdous* means, that is, 36 and 32. Thus, it is evident that the intervals of the *epitrites* <note>original: "epitritus"; a ratio of 4:3, corresponding to a musical fourth</note> are filled by the space of the *epogdous*. Next, he proceeds: so that for a perfect and accumulated perfection, something was lacking from the *epitrite*: namely, as much as is lacking when comparing 243 against 256. What is it that he wishes to be understood? I shall say. But first I will explain those things which must be said beforehand.

For just as the principal and greatest parts of articulate speech are nouns and verbs—and of these, the syllables, and of syllables, the letters (which are the first individual and elementary voices, for from these the continuity of the whole oration is constituted, and the dissolution of the oration reaches back to these same final letters)—so it is also with the tuneful voice, which is called <term>emmelēs</term> <note>original: "εμμελής"; meaning "in tune" or "melodic"</note> by the Greeks, and is composed of measures and numbers. Its principal parts are those which are called <term>systēmata</term> <note>original: "συστήματα"; systems or scales</note> by musicians. These themselves consist of a certain stretch of pronunciation, which are called <term>diastēmata</term> <note>original: "διαστήματα"; intervals</note>. Furthermore, the parts of these intervals are <term>phthongoi</term> <note>original: "φθόγγοι"; notes or tones</note>, which are called "sounds" by us; these sounds are the first foundations of song.

Now, there is a difference in sounds according to the tension of the strings. Indeed, high sounds are excited by air struck more vehemently and quickly, while lower sounds occur whenever the pulses are gentler and slower. An "accent" <note>high pitch</note> exists from a very rapid pulse, while a "succent" <note>low pitch</note> comes from a gentle and slower one. Furthermore, from accents and succents varied by the reasoning of music, a melody is called a "symphony" <note>concordance or harmony</note>.

Therefore, the first symphony is found in the first four modules, which is called the *diatessaron* <note>a perfect fourth</note>. The second, which consists of the first five modules, is named the *diapente* <note>a perfect fifth</note>. When these are composed in order, there arises that melody which is called the *epogdous* and the *diapason* <note>an octave</note>. It is called *epogdous* because the ancient musicians used only eight strings, of which the first was the *hypate*, emitting the lowest sound, and the last was the *nete*, emitting the high sound. These two diverse sounds have between them a concord and consonance of a certain wondrous kind.

Later, music was increased by adding more strings to each part; however, the earlier symphonies persevered in their names, and from these, the additions were composed. Thus, the *diapason* joined to the *diatessaron*, and also joined to another *diapente*, received a double perfection, and for that reason is called the *disdiapason* <note>a double octave</note>.

Pythagoras is said to have been the first to note these sounds harmonizing with each other and to have understood that they have a certain kinship with numbers. Thus, he said the *diatessaron* melody has the same ratio as the *epitritus* (4:3) in numbers. Again, the *diapente* symphony has a ratio similar to that quantity which is *sescuplaris* (3:2) in numbers. The *diapason*, however, obtains the power of a double quantity (2:1). Thus he made a comparison of this kind: that the *diapason* and *diatessaron* symphonies have as much difference as there is between eight and three. For eight is both a double and a double-*epitrite* of the number three. Again, he said the *diapason* and *diapente* have a triple ratio (3:1). But the *disdiapason* has a quadruple ratio (4:1). A "tone" has the ratio of an *epogdous* (9:8). That which is called a "hemitone" <note>half-tone</note>—but was called a *diesis* by the ancients—he recognized as a *limma* <note>a "remnant" or small interval</note>. A *limma* is when something is lacking from integrity. This very thing which is lacking, that is the hemitone or *limma*, he said has the ratio which these numbers have between themselves: that is, the sum of 256 against the sum of 243.

He was called to the discipline and understanding of these things by experimentation. For he suspended, I believe, certain weights from equal strings, equal both in length and thickness, and found that what he calls a "tone" was equal to eight moments <note>units of weight</note>. Thus he found the symphony of music to harmonize with the symphony of numbers...

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

<header>Chalcidius’s Interpretation</header>

...is defined. Therefore, because the *diapason* <note>the octave</note> consists of two primary melodies, the *diatessaron* <note>the perfect fourth</note> and the *diapente* <note>the perfect fifth</note>: the ratio of the *diapason* is found to be placed in a double quantity <note>2:1</note>, while the *diatessaron* is in the *epitrite* <note>4:3 ratio</note>, and the *diapente* in the *sescuplus* <note>3:2 ratio</note>: it is necessary that this number, which we said is found in the double quantity, also consists of the *epitrite* and the *sescuplus*. For the number eight is the *epitrite* of the number six <note>8 is 4/3 of 6</note>, and the number twelve is the *sescuplar* of the number eight <note>12 is 3/2 of 8</note>. Therefore, the number twelve is the double of the number six. This is divided into an *epitrite* ratio, namely twelve against nine, and another *sescuplar* ratio, namely nine against six. Again, the *diapente* symphony exceeds the *diatessaron* by one tone. Indeed, the *diapente* consists of three tones and a semitone: while the *diatessaron* consists of two tones and a semitone. From this it appears that a tone is found in the *epogdoan* ratio <note>9:8 ratio</note>: and the *sescuplus* measure is more powerful than the *epitrite* measure by the *epogdoan* ratio. Since from the *sescuplus*, for example nine against six, if the *epitrite* of eight against six is removed, the *epogdoan* measure remains, that is, nine against eight. Likewise, because the *diapason* symphony is found placed in the double measure: and the *diatessaron* in the *epitrite*: that which consists of these is recognized as being placed in that ratio in which the number eight is against the number three. For the *epitrite* of three is four, and the double of these is eight. The *diapason* and *diapente* together <note>the octave plus a fifth, or a twelfth</note> are in a triple ratio. For the *sescuplar* and the double composed together produce this same result, that is, eighteen. Indeed, nine compared against six is found to be *sescuplar*: of which the number eighteen is the double. But compared against six, it is triple. Similarly, even that symphony which is called the *disdiapason* <note>the double octave</note> obtains a quadruple ratio. For a triple number added to an *epitrite* number completes the quadruple quantity. And the *diapente* symphony added to the *diapason* symphony produces a triple quantity. But the *diatessaron* symphony is an *epitrite* ratio: as we have already often said. From these, however, the *disdiapason* symphony consists, for the number eighteen exceeds the number six by three times. Moreover, the number twenty-four exceeds this same number eighteen by an *epitrite* ratio. But the number twenty-four compared against six is four times as much. And again, the *epitrite* of the number six is the number eight; and the triple of this very number is that which is found in twenty-four units. Thus the same number twenty-four is found to be the quadruple of the number six.

Having traversed these matters in this manner, let us return to the consideration of the arrangement itself. Therefore, the limits of the *sescuplus* ratios having been born: and likewise those to which a third part of itself is added (which kind is called *epitrite* by the Greeks) and likewise those to which an eighth part of itself is added: which number is called *epogdoas* <note>9:8</note> by the same people: from these connections those first spaces, that is, the intervals of all the *epitrites*, were being filled by the spaces of the *epogdoans*. What, therefore, are those first spaces? Those, namely, which are contained in the form described at the beginning: which was arranged from double numbers: in which the *sescuplars* and *epitrites* are born. But in this one which was described in the second place, the *epogdoan* numbers, which are tones, are found to be born. Then he says that all the *epitrites* are filled by the *epogdoan* spaces: that is, even those *epitrites* which are in the *sescuplar* numbers. For in these also the *epitrite* ratio is contained: because the *sescuplar* number is greater than the *epitrite* number by the *epogdoan* calculation, that is, by one tone. It is necessary that the same *epitrite* ratio also be contained in the double and triple quantity: and not without merit. For the first symphony is this very one which is called the *diatessaron*, placed in the *epitrite* measure. And because the *epitrite* consists not only of two *epogdoans*: but also of another small remainder: just as the *diatessaron* does not consist of only two tones: but of a semitone, which the ancients [called] the *limma*...

<summary>Chalcidius explains the mathematical ratios underlying musical intervals (octaves, fourths, and fifths) and how they combine to form the harmonic structure of the universe. He demonstrates how the 9:8 ratio (a whole tone) fits into larger intervals like the 4:3 perfect fourth.</summary>

# On Plato's Timaeus. Page XXII.

...they used to call it. In the treatment of this ratio, you have what he said: that there is a certain small remaining portion of each of the <term>sesquitertians</term> <note>original: "epitritorum"; a ratio of 4:3, corresponding to the musical interval of a fourth</note>. He was designating this very ratio as the <term>semitone</term> <note>original: "hemitonii"</note>, which he says is as much as is lacking for 243 to reach 256, so that the <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>original: "epogdous"; a ratio of 9:8, corresponding to a whole tone</note> number is not full. Therefore, a third diagram must be made, which is placed below; so that we may attain not only by understanding, but also by seeing, what that remainder is and how much it is that is missing from the sesquitertians when measured by the sesquioctave ratio. Larger numbers are therefore chosen, so that the density of the whole voice and a kind of total compression may proceed, the capacity of larger sums allowing this to happen.

### ¶ Third Diagram: which is the harmonic modulation according to the sesquioctave ratio for both the symphonies of the fourth and the fifth.

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| Tone | 192 | 24 |
| Tone | 216 | 27 |
| Semitone | 243 | 13 |
| Tone | 256 | 32 |
| Tone | 288 | 36 |
| Tone | 324 | 40.5 |
| Semitone | 384 | 128 |

What number of the first portion, then, will suit the established reasoning? Surely one hundred and ninety-two, which I find in this way: I take the number which, before all others, consists of three parts—that is, the number three. I multiply this by itself eight times <note>The author likely means 3 multiplied by 64, or 8x8</note>; the number 24 is born. I calculate this same number 24 eight times. The sought number is found to be 192, of which the third part is 64, and the half is 96. But because we have set out to discuss it by the <term>sesquioctave</term> ratio <note>9:8 ratio</note>, I apply another limit distant from it by double the amount which is in the sum 384. I say the number 256 is the <term>sesquitertian</term> <note>4:3 ratio</note> of that number which we established as the highest limit, that is, 192; for it contains this very 192 and its third part, 64. Of this limit 192, the <term>sesquioctave</term> number is 216; for it contains in itself the whole limit 192 and its eighth part, 24. Of this very number 216, the sesquioctave is that which is in the sum 243. For it contains 216 and their eighth part: that is, 27. But indeed 256, which we said was the sesquitertian of the number 192, is the sesquioctave to that which completes the sum 288. For it contains the same and its eighth part, which is 32. Of this very number which is in 288, the sesquioctave number is 324, having itself and its eighth part, which is 36. Again, of the number 324 itself, the sesquioctave number is that which has the sum 364 and a half; for it contains itself and its eighth portion, 40 and a half. Two sums remain: one which is in 256, the other which is in 243. I cannot say that 256 is the sesquioctave of two hundred and forty-three. For the number 243 is not contained entirely within the number 256 along with its eighth part. For the eighth part of two hundred and forty-three is thirty and nearly a half. But between 216 and 243, there are only 27 numbers between them. Therefore, the sesquioctave ratio cannot be preserved between these numbers. And since the sesquioctave in numbers is the same as the tone in music, it is clear how much is lacking to complete a tone when the aforementioned numbers are compared with each other. Nor can a whole <term>semitone</term> <note>original: "hemitonium"</note> be preserved, whence he rightly adds: "So that for a perfect and heaped-up perfection, something was lacking from the sesquitertian," namely, as much as is lacking when a comparison is made of 243 against 256. Which ratio is found also in the other numbers that are in the formula. Certainly, the speech will suit the disputant. For that <term>Timaeus</term> <note>The speaker in Plato's dialogue</note> who treats of this in this book was from the mastership of <term>Pythagoras</term>; whom <term>Plato</term> reasonably introduces using proofs domestic and familiar to himself, to teach that the nature of the soul agrees with numbers, and harmonizes also with the modulations of music. Now, since the virtues of which the <term>World Soul</term> <note>original: "anima mundi"</note> consists, joined together as if from parts and members, by these same virtues and those which...

# XXII. Interpretation of Chalcidius

<margin>Sextus Empiricus cites Empedocles here at locations 134, 129, and 52.</margin>
It has been made quite clear how much the nature of the soul accords with numbers and proportions. Now it must be demonstrated why Plato, in pursuing his purpose, descended to this specific treatise: so that by a rational guide we might arrive by this path of reasoning at the intended effect of the work. He wishes, therefore, for the soul of the sensible world <note>The physical world perceived by the senses</note> to be born as if granted the freedom to take possession, yet remaining the knower of all things: those which are intelligible <note>grasped by the mind</note> as well as those which are sensible. Furthermore, it is a Pythagorean doctrine that "like is only understood by its like." Following this, Empedocles also says in his verses:

> By the earth we perceive earth, by the flames the ether,
> By moisture the liquid, by our breath the air,
> By peace we see peace, and by strife, mournful strife.
> <note>original: "Terram terreno comprehendimus..." Chalcidius is quoting Empedocles' theory that we perceive elements because we are composed of them.</note>

Indeed, he established these as the elements and beginnings of the universe: from which he also judged the substance of the soul to consist. For this reason, he believed a full knowledge of all things resided within the soul, as it comprehends things having a similarity to its own likeness. And Plato himself, asserting this very point, fashions the soul out of all the "beginnings" <note>the primary principles or elements</note>: so that it might possess knowledge of the beginnings themselves, of that which follows the beginnings, and indeed of all existing things, and might judge concerning them all. Finally, after he mixed the soul from those virtues <note>powers or properties</note> from which he thought it should be composed, and divided the composition into parts according to certain geometric, arithmetic, and musical ratios, he added this: He cut this very thing, and its single series, lengthwise; and from one he made two; and he bound them together middle-to-middle in the shape of the Greek letter **χ** <note>the letter Chi</note> and bent them into circles until their ends met; and he inserted one circle into the other so that one of them rotated in a direct path and the other in an oblique circuit. He added this: Therefore, since it was joined together from the nature of the "Same" and the "Different" mixed with "Essence," and being possessed of motion and returning into itself in a circular revolution, whenever it encounters either a dissoluble substance or an indivisible one, it easily recognizes what is of an identical and indivisible nature, and what is of a different and dissolvable nature; it sees the causes of all things that happen, and from those things that occur, it measures what shall be in the future. From this it is clear: since the most ancient beginnings of things are Essence or Substance—and this is twofold: one part indivisible, the other divisible—and since the twin diversity of nature is by far the oldest, the fashioned soul consists of both substances, and likewise of the "Same" and "Different" natures from all origins. Therefore, its nature most closely accords with the nature of numbers: which it is established are even older than geometric forms themselves. Geometric forms must necessarily be found within some number: such as figures of three, four, or even more sides. Again, the "beginnings" are called hexahedrons <note>cubes</note> and octahedrons <note>eight-sided solids</note>. These certainly cannot exist without numbers. Yet nothing prevents numbers from existing without these forms. Thus, the most ancient beginnings of numbers are found in all ratios. Furthermore, the very beginnings and principles of numbers are Unity <note>the Monad</note> and likewise Duality <note>the Dyad</note>. For it is established that these two are the origins of all other numbers. By this reasoning, it is concluded that the soul—composed of a twofold substance and harmonizing with the twin nature of the power of numbers, and giving life to the celestial bodies and to living beings in which there is reason and learning—possesses a consciousness of all things from whose powers it is itself composed. ¶ This is that rational **World Soul**: which, according to its higher nature, provides protection and veneration to inferior things, obeying divine dispositions, imparting providence to created things, blessed by its likeness to eternal things because of its kinship with them, and acting as the helper and patron of dissolvable things. The marks of its virtue in deliberating and reasoning appear in the character of man: who, as an eminent worshiper of God, has imparted care to gentle animals. Indeed, by these same virtues of the soul...

<summary>This page explains the Platonic and Pythagorean theory that the World Soul is composed of mathematical ratios and primary elements so that it may perceive and govern all things. It describes the soul's structural formation into the shape of a Greek letter Chi (X) to represent celestial motions.</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, Timaeus, Chalcidius, Empedocles, Pythagoreanism, sacred geometry, Monad and Dyad, celestial mechanics, Chi, macrocosm and microcosm</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
On Plato’s Timaeus. Page XXIII.

<margin>The generation of man. Hebrews and Christians.</margin>
The sensible world is nourished: when, in addition to the other vital powers common to humans, beasts, and even plants—namely growing, moving oneself, desiring, and imagining—reason arrives to perfect the soul proper to man. Thus, he is not only possessed of life, but also does not lack the choice of living well. And so, human life is tempered by the companionship of the natural and the rational soul. An eminent doctrine of a holier sect <note>likely referring to the Judeo-Christian tradition, specifically Philo or Genesis</note>, more prudent in the understanding of divine matters, testifies that this is true. It claims that God, having completed and illuminated the sensible world, established the human race by building and forming a body from a portion of earth taken from the ground according to this likeness; but for its life, He summoned it from the celestial vaults. Afterward, He imparted inspiration into its innermost parts with His own breath—this "inspiration" signifying the counsel of God and the counsel of the soul. And the reason of God is that which provides for human affairs; it is the cause for men living well and blessedly, provided they do not neglect the gift granted to them by the highest God.

<margin>Epilogue</margin>
¶ Now, enough has been said regarding the Soul of the World, which is forged from the blending of two essences—the Same and the Different—and regarding its division according to harmonic, arithmetic, and geometric ratios, and in what way its nature accords with numbers and sounds. Now we shall follow how the division of the soul corresponds to the division of the celestial members and the bending of the circles, by whose motions the revolution of the soul is met. Indeed, he <note>Plato</note> acts with sufficient skill to show that the soul, established by the same reason as the celestial bodies, easily recognizes its own kinship; or because his dogma is of this kind: that the beginning of the motion of the universe rests with that which is moved by itself, and there is nothing except the soul which moves from itself.

<margin>Where motion begins.</margin>
From this, it is clear that the origin and cause of the world's motion is the same. Furthermore, the world is carried in a gyre <note>a spiral or circular motion</note>. Therefore, it is moved first by the circuits of the gyres, so that the entire body of the celestial vault is carried around in these same orbits. That the soul is moved by itself is declared when, making a distinction between opinions and science, he says these things: Thus, as it is joined together from the nature of the Same and the Different mixed with Essence, needing motion and returning into itself in a circular circuit, when it encounters either a dissolvable substance or an indivisible one, it easily recognizes what is of the Same and indivisible nature, and what is of the Different and dissolvable nature. It sees the causes of all things that happen, and from those things that occur, it measures what will be in the future. Its motion is rational, without voice or sound; when it looks upon something sensible, the circle of the Different kind is carried without error by truthful sense; and with a certain messenger, all the right opinions and those worthy of belief are born in the soul.

Furthermore, when it beholds the indivisible genus which is always the Same, with the internal motion faithfully reporting, intellect and science grow strong. It is clear that all these things happen in the soul and are marked within it. He signifies this same thing somewhat more clearly in the *Phaedrus*, saying thus:

<margin>The immortal Soul is marked.</margin>
> Every soul is immortal: because that which is always moved is always in life.

But truly, that which moves another thing, having taken its power of motion from elsewhere, must necessarily suffer a pause in moving, and thus suffer a pause in life. Therefore, only that which moves itself—inasmuch as it never deserts its own motion—never ceases to move itself; rather, it is the source and beginning of motion for all other things which do not have motion from their own nature. A beginning, moreover, is without an origin: for when things are born, they are created from a beginning; but the beginning itself is from no origin. For if it were from something else, the beginning would not be the beginning, since it is necessary that all things which are born come from a beginning. And because it lacks generation <note>birth/origin</note>, it also lacks death. Indeed, if the beginning were buried, it would neither be repaired from anything else, nor would anything be created from it. For surely all things that are in a state of generation subsist from a beginning.

Thus, therefore, the beginning of motion is that which is moved by an internal and genuine motion; this, moreover, it is forbidden to either be born or to die. Or the whole heaven...

<meta>catchword: Or the whole heaven</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the divine creation of the human soul as a blend of physical life and divine reason, drawing parallels between the human soul and the Soul of the World. It argues for the immortality of the soul based on its status as a self-moving "beginning" of motion, citing Plato's Phaedrus.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Timaeus, Phaedrus, immortality of the soul, self-motion, creation of man, divine inspiration, celestial circles, Same and Different</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary and translation of Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the mathematical construction of the World Soul and the spherical nature of the universe.</meta>

# XXIV Chalcidius's Interpretation

...and that every created thing, once weakened, would collapse, and there would be no remedy by which those things—which must be moved for the sake of preserving the universe—might be restored. Therefore, by demonstrating the immortality of that which is moved by itself, the immortality of the soul and the mind has been revealed. For every body that possesses the vigor of motion within itself is endowed with a soul; whereas that which receives motion from without is without a soul: clearly, this is the very nature of the soul. Since this is so, it appears that the soul alone is that which is moved by itself; and for that reason, it is placed beyond the necessity of birth and death.

This constitution, he says, God split lengthwise like a ribbon; and from one, he made two: and he joined them at their centers in the shape of the Greek letter **χ** <note>The letter Chi, representing the intersection of the celestial equator and the ecliptic</note>; and he bent them into circles until their ends met; and he inserted one circle into the other in such a way that one of them would rotate in a direct circuit, and the other in an oblique circuit. And the motion of the outer circle, which was of the same nature, he named "the Same"; but that of the inner circle, "the Different." And the outer circuit, which we said was named "the Same," he turned from the right side through the left toward the right; but the "Different" he rotated diagonally toward the left, giving it power and skill in its revolution. One of them, indeed, he left as it was, undivided. But the inner one he split six times: and he fashioned seven unequal circles, according to double and triple intervals <note>Referring to the Pythagorean intervals of 1, 2, 4, 8 and 1, 3, 9, 27</note>: and he commanded these circles to be carried in contrary motion. Of these seven, three move with equal velocity, but four move with a speed that is unequal and dissimilar both to themselves and in comparison to the others, yet moving according to reason.

<margin>
The shape of the world is round.
</margin>

Again, so that the tradition of the parts of astronomy may also be handed down, those matters which pertain to the present treatise will be explained briefly and clearly, according to their nature. Plato says that the shape of the world is round and spherical: likewise, that the earth is spherical and situated in the middle of the world, and that it occupies the position of a mere point; as for its smallness, it is like a mark compared to the magnitude of the entire universe. But I think it is not enough for him to have said this, unless it be shown to be so by evident reasons. To what extent, then, is the shape of the world smooth and spherical? First, the rising of the celestial fires as if emerging from the depths, their journeys with fixed elevations, and then their descents until they are submerged in their settings, and then their rising again from the same place: and likewise their setting in the western sea, testify that they encompass the outermost surface of the heaven by day and night. Next, that from any region of the earth, half of the world is seen by us, situated at the summit, while the lower part is not seen unless it is returned to the upper position by the turns and succession of days and nights, because the earth is placed in the way and resists our sight. Then, that from every point of view, all lines stretched out in every direction to the outermost boundaries of the heaven appear equal: just as the radii of a circle proceeding from a point to the outermost circumference are of equal measure.

<margin>
The astrologers call this "Hadir" <note>A term likely derived from Arabic or a corruption of a technical Greek term for the whirling of the spheres</note> or "the whirling."
</margin>

Furthermore, the zodiac circle, lifting the signs in an oblique whirling at their rising, hides other signs at their setting—signs which stand directly opposite and are distant by the diameter. Likewise, as in the turning of a wheel: when a certain part of it ascends, the opposite part descends; which does not occur in any other shape except in roundness alone. Moreover, the likeness of a sphere in the form of the earth is understood in many ways: either because the day does not dawn everywhere at the same moments of time, and the succeeding night creates shadows; but both rising and setting happen earlier in eastern regions, and later in western regions: so that it is clear there is some higher prominence between each region of the heaven which, being placed in the way, holds the shadows of night before the sun becomes higher. Or because an eclipse of the moon, occurring everywhere at the same moment, is noted at different times. To the regions near the East it is noted later: but to others according to the diversity of places, on account of the bends, boundaries, and natural prominences of the earth; for the sun does not illuminate the circuits of the earth everywhere at the same time.

<summary>This page explains Plato's cosmological model, describing the mathematical division of the World Soul into the "Same" and "Different" (the celestial equator and ecliptic) and providing proofs for the spherical shape of the Earth and the universe.</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, Chi, celestial spheres, planetary motion, spherical earth, astronomy, Chalcidius, Timaeus, eclipses, zodiac</keywords>

# On Plato’s Timaeus

Page XXIIII.

regions: and therefore, its light is obstructed from the opposite side by terrestrial shadows still standing in the way, preventing the illumination of the moon; for the "labor" <note>original: "labor" - a classical term for an eclipse</note> of the moon occurs during the night season. Furthermore, the roundness of the earth appears clearly: likewise it is subject to the north and the north wind, and similarly to the south and the south wind: for when people travel back and forth from either region on nightly journeys, they behold stars unknown to them with the wonder of novelty. It is evident that these stars do not belong only to those specific regions; rather, humans are hindered from seeing and knowing them all because the massive bulk and elevations of the earth are cast in the way of their sight. From this it is concluded that the earth is like the globe of a sphere; its growth into a mass is understood by looking up from all regions. This same point can also be understood and grasped through natural laws. Indeed, all things that possess any weight, descending from everywhere and from any part in a direct course, travel toward a single central point. All things make a rigid path of descent in the manner and appearance of lines starting from a wider beginning and always reaching toward the seat of the point by a narrower path, with equal angles standing around that same seat of the point from every side: which is the very image of roundness. Furthermore, that all things of any weight hasten toward the center is proved thus: even many weights to which an external force has been applied are carried downward toward the earth according to nature as soon as that force has withdrawn. This is seen in the engines that hurl javelins: as long as the impulse flourishes, they seem to fly suspended and cut the air; but when that force which was impelling them fails, they descend with the point turned toward the earth, hastening to what is the center of the world. ¶ Even the form of the sea and of all water, when the sea is in stable tranquility, appears in a rounded shape and appearance. For those things which we see while standing on the shore across a strait, such as a tree or a tower, or even a ship in that same strait: these same things, if we are lying down with our face and sight level with the "back" <note>The "back" or surface of the sea</note> and top of the sea, we either do not see at all, or certainly see as much smaller than before, because the curvature of the sea stands in the way of the observers. And also in sailing, quite often when the land was not yet visible from the ship, sailors have seen it after climbing the mast; evidently, once the obstacle of the intervening mass has been passed, the things lying below on every side are observed freely from a higher vantage point. By artificial consideration, we also find the roundness of calm and resting water. Indeed, it is granted by nature that liquid flows down from higher places into lower ones. Furthermore, those things are higher which are furthest from the center point of the earth, and those are lower which are closest. Therefore, if we suppose the surface of the water to be flat and placed in a straight line **A B G** <note>original: "A B Γ" - using Greek letters for geometric points</note>, then from the point of the earth, for instance **k** <note>original: "κ" (kappa)</note>, let a line **k B** be drawn upward: and if the outermost parts of the surface are joined by these two lines **k A** and **k G**, both lines will be longer compared to line **k B**. If we wish to make these equal, the increase will reach as far as **B H**, and the true surface will be at **A H G**. Therefore, from

<image-desc>A geometric diagram demonstrating the sphericity of water. It shows a circular arc labeled A H Γ at the top. Below the arc is a straight horizontal line A B Γ. A central point at the bottom is labeled κ (kappa). From κ, radial lines extend to points A and Γ, and a central vertical line κ B continues upward to point H on the arc. This visualizes why water on a supposedly flat surface A B Γ would be further from the center at the edges than at the middle, thus naturally forming the curve A H Γ.</image-desc>

the higher parts, the water will flow from both **A & G** into the lower **B**, until the vessel is covered from the bottom **k** by the increase of water even at **B**, and it reaches as far as **H**, until the water pours itself equally through all parts with a rounded circumference. From this it is clear that the surface of the sea and of all water is also spherical. Indeed, the logic of the whole and of the parts is one and the same. ¶ But if anyone, looking down at the peaks of mountains and their length and rocky roughness, says that it is not like a ball smoothed on a lathe—that is, because of the deformity of the rugged mountains—he does not perceive correctly. For we do not say the earth is a globe, but that it is *globose* <note>shaped like a globe but not a perfect geometric sphere</note>; nor that it is a ball, but *similar* to a ball. Certainly, of the whole...

# XVIII Chalcidius’s Interpretation

Conceiving the magnitude of the earth rationally in the mind, we investigate the truth not by sight and the senses, but rather we lead our examination toward the remedies of reason and intelligence. Now, we shall consider in what manner the earth occupies the central seat of the world. I believe that if it were not in the middle, and if it deviated from the strictness of that central point, it would not have one half of the heavens above it and the other half below it from every and any part of itself. No one can show that lines extended from any given point all the way to the extreme circumference of the world would be equal to one another if the spaces of those lines were not equal <note>Chalcidius is arguing that if the Earth weren't central, the sky wouldn't look symmetrical from every horizon</note>. Therefore, it deviates from the center not at all. 

Indeed, that the Earth holds the status of a mere point when compared to the vastness of the entire universe is declared by the sharpness of the pointers which are called <term>gnomons</term> <note>The vertical rod of a sundial</note> by engineers. <margin>†Solar</margin> Setting these up throughout all provinces and even all habitable regions, they take for themselves—indiscriminately and without distinction—the tips of these very gnomons as the center-point of the <term>solstitial sphere</term> <note>The celestial sphere as measured during the solstice</note>. And they are not in error. Therefore, if there is one true center of the solstitial sphere, and yet all markers and all points from every region of the earth align with that true center of the sun, it is clear that the entire earth acts as a single point when compared against the globe of the sun. 

But concerning the form of the whole world and also of the earth—and further concerning its central position and its comparison against the immensity of the universal body—enough has been said. However, as the celestial sphere turns itself around the fixed and ever-abiding poles, and as the axis to which the central earth adheres connects those same poles, the earth attends the rotation of the celestial sphere of all the stars. These celestial signs, scattered about, trace circles through their own equal revolutions at distances opposite and meeting the axis. The multitude of these circles surpasses counting. Yet a few are celebrated by noble names, which are useful to know for the spectacle of the "celestial dance." 

One, which is near the high pole known and visible to us, is called the **Northern** circle because of the constellation of the "Seven Oxen" <note>original: *septentrionum*; the Big Dipper/Ursa Major</note> situated there. The other, opposite and equal to this one, is near the submerged and always hidden pole; it is itself hidden, the **Antarctic**. The middle and greatest of all, dividing the sphere in two, is the **Equinoctial** <note>The Equator</note>; which the Greeks call *isēmerinos* <note>original: ἰσημερινὸς; "equal day"</note> because it provides nights equal to all days for the regions of the earth situated directly beneath it. Nevertheless, even in other regions not directly beneath it that see the rising and setting of the Sun: when the Sun applies itself to this circle, it measures out equal hours of day and night. 

Furthermore, between this and the arctic regions on either side are the two **Tropics**, the Summer and the Winter. The one in our region, near the North, is the Summer tropic; the Winter tropic is near the South. Through these the **Zodiac** <note>original: *signifer*; "the sign-bearer"</note> curves obliquely, itself a great circle touching the tropics at individual signs: the Summer at Cancer, and the Winter at Capricorn. This same circle cuts the Equinoctial circle twice, and is itself cut by it just as many times at the "Claws" <note>Libra</note> and the Ram <note>Aries</note>. Beneath this circle the moon and sun are carried, as well as the other fires which are called **planets**: *Phaenon*, which is **Saturn**; and *Phaeton*, which is **Jupiter**; also *Pyroïs*, which is <margin>†Indian</margin> **Mars**. Likewise *Lucifer*, which is **Venus** (who is also known by the name *Hesperus*). Furthermore, *Stilbon*, the star of **Mercury**. 

There is also said to be a "final circle," which our sight imagines as the **Horizon** (a Greek name), limiting the world and dividing it into two parts according to human sight. Since the mass of the earth is in the way, only that hemisphere which is above is seen; the other meanwhile lies hidden beneath the southern pole, where, as the poet says: *Black Styx is seen beneath our feet, and the spirits of the deep.* <note>A quote from Virgil's Georgics</note> This too is a great and maximal circle, cutting both the Equinoctial and the Zodiac in a twin section. From this it happens that of the signs distant from each other by the diameter: one appears in the east, the other in the west. This horizon is itself divided in two by the **Meridian**, which is called *mesēmbrinos* <note>original: μεσημβρινὸς; "midday"</note>. This too is a great circle...

<summary>Chalcidius explains the Earth's central position in the universe using the geometry of sundials (gnomons) and defines the major celestial circles including the Equator, Tropics, and Zodiac.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, gnomon, celestial spheres, planets, Zodiac, Horizon, Chalcidius, Greek terminology, geometry of the universe</keywords>

# On Plato’s Timaeus

Folio XXV.

curved through both poles. But that which is understood as straight against the boundary <note>The horizon</note> which it presses upon, he calls the **meridian**; <margin>Meridian</margin> because at midday the Sun, higher on its lofty path, looks down directly upon the earth with a straight gaze. But the **equator** <margin>Equator</margin> and those circles which stand at its sides are fixed and established both in size and position. The **Zodiac**, <margin>Zodiac</margin> however, is fixed in size and on a firm axis, and its position is also fixed relative to the heavens; yet relative to us, it tips forward, nodding variously because of its slant, appearing one way to us and another way to others. The **meridian** and likewise the **horizon** <note>original: "finalis"</note> are powerful in terms of size, for they are the greatest circles; but they shift in position across different regions of the earth, applying themselves to some people in one way and to others in another. But indeed, those circles near the poles—that is, the **arctic** and its opposite, the **antarctic**—are solidified neither in size nor position; rather, according to the difference of the Northern and Southern regions, they are thought larger by some and smaller by others. However, at the center of the earth, where the zone stretches under the meridian circle, parched by flaming heat, it is uninhabitable; there, both poles are visible and clear, through which the boundary line passes, determining the parts of the world. It must also be added that the other circles are "circles" according to the definition which teaches that a circle is a flat figure contained by a single drawn line. Thus, each is contained by its own single drawn line. But the **Zodiac** <margin>Zodiac/Sign-bearer</margin> <note>original: "Signifer"</note> consists of many parts in the appearance of a drum-cylinder <note>original: "tympano"</note>, in the width of which the figures of the signs are sketched. Furthermore, a circle which is called the **median** <margin>Median</margin> cuts through its width; this is a "great circle," touching both tropics and intersecting the equator twice. The remaining two outermost circles of the Zodiac's width are shorter than this median circle itself.

<note>The diagram illustrates the celestial sphere. The North Pole (Polus arcticus) is at the top, the South Pole (Polus antarcticus) at the bottom. The horizontal bands show the Summer Tropic, the Equator, and the Winter Tropic. The broad diagonal band represents the Zodiac or Ecliptic, labeled as the "Sign-bearer."</note>

<margin>Fixed stars</margin>
¶ Therefore, the **fixed stars**, which do not wander, and likewise the poles, are carried around with the greatest sphere that embraces all things in its own hug—which is called the **Aplanes** <note>The "unwandering" or outermost sphere of the fixed stars</note>—clinging to it in one simple motion. Wherever it revolves, they are revolved with it, always holding one and the same position and preserving their order with an unchangeable tenor, never admitting any change in their configuration, their rising, their stability, their size, or even their color.

<margin>Planets</margin>
¶ But the **Sun**, the **Moon**, and the other five which are called **planets**, are indeed swept along by the single revolution of the greater globe from east to west every single day, just like those fixed and unerring stars. Yet they are seen to make various other motions: either when they migrate into the place of a passing sign as if acting out motions contrary to the motion of the whole globe, or when they wander from the Northern to the Southern regions and back with a wider deflection; and from the summer to the winter tropic, and from there finally return to the summer one. The cause of this is judged to be the width of the **Zodiac** <note>original: "signiferi orbis"</note>. And also returning within the slanted width of the Zodiac itself, now toward the North, now toward the South, when indeed they apply themselves to the North...
