# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

higher up: but when they are toward the South they are called low, and this is not the same for all of them, but some more so, others less. There also appears to be a certain diversity in size to those observing them. For it is necessary that they seem smaller from a distance, and larger from nearby when they tend toward the lowest point: for this reason, their speed in motion is also not equal: because equal measurements of the <term>signs</term><note>The twelve divisions of the Zodiac.</note> do not seem to be completed in equal times: but rather more quickly at the time when they appear larger due to proximity; but more slowly when, being further removed from the earth, they appear smaller. Nevertheless, the wandering in breadth <note>Lat. *evagatio in latum*; this refers to planetary latitude, or how far a planet moves north or south of the ecliptic.</note> which occurs in the Zodiac is not equal for all. Indeed, that of the Sun is smaller than the other fires: for its entire wandering falls under the estimation of a single degree. But for the Moon and <term>Lucifer</term><note>The "Light-Bringer," the planet Venus.</note>, according to the opinion of the men of old, it is far greater, reaching almost twelve degrees. For <term>Mercurius</term><note>The planet Mercury.</note> it is nearly eight parts; for <term>Pyrois</term><note>The "Fiery One," the planet Mars.</note> and <term>Phaeton</term><note>The "Shining One," the planet Jupiter.</note> it is five; for Saturn it is three degrees.

<margin>The courses of the Planets</margin>
Furthermore, they traverse the circle of the <term>sign-bearing orb</term><note>The Zodiac.</note> in length—that is, where the boundary of the beginning and end meets—the Moon indeed in the space of twenty-seven days and a third part of a day-and-night measure. Likewise the Sun in a year, that is, 365 days and nearly a quarter of a day-and-night space. <term>Lucifer</term> and <term>Stilbon</term><note>The "Gleaming One," the planet Mercury.</note>, though with unequal steps, nevertheless complete their courses in almost the same times as the Sun, sometimes overtaking it with a swift flight, sometimes finally being overtaken by it with a sluggish pace. <term>Pyrois</term> in almost two years. <term>Phaeton</term> in nearly twelve years. <term>Phaenon</term><note>The "Shining One," the planet Saturn.</note> in thirty years, a little less. For this reason, they by no means coincide with the Sun’s traversals, brightenings, and darkenings, which we call rising and setting. Indeed, the Moon, after its meeting and union with the Sun: because it arrives sooner at the destination, always appears first in the evening: and rising in the morning, it is hidden in the west. On the contrary, <term>Phaenon</term>, <term>Phaeton</term>, and <term>Pyrois</term>, because they traverse the spaces of the signs more slowly than the Sun, as if overtaken and passed by it, always setting in the evening, they rise at the omen of the morning light. Furthermore, <term>Lucifer</term> and <term>Stilbon</term>, running together with the Sun, and for the most part accompanying it, sometimes overtaking it and in turn being overtaken by it: if indeed they rise in the evening, they will be hidden in another evening. If they shine forth in the morning, they will sink in another morning. For while the other wandering stars recede from the Sun by a long interval—so that they are often diametrically opposite—<term>Stilbon</term> and <term>Lucifer</term> are always seen around the Sun. <term>Stilbon</term> indeed for no more than 20 <term>moments</term><note>A unit of time or arc; here likely referring to degrees of elongation.</note>, that is, two parts of one sign, leaning either toward the north or sometimes toward the south.

<margin>Rising is said in various ways.</margin>
But <term>Venus</term><note>Lucifer.</note> departs nearly 50 moments toward the east and west. "Rising" is spoken of in various ways. Properly, it is the first brightening of the stars and the Sun: and the lifting above the boundary circle <note>The horizon.</note>. Loosely, it is the first splendor of the Sun bringing in the day; this the poets call the *jubar* <note>A beam of light or radiance.</note>, and again the *aurora* <note>The dawn.</note>. The third type is called <term>achronicos</term><note>Achronychal rising: when a star rises in the east just as the sun sets in the west.</note>, when, as the Sun sets, a star diametrically opposite is seen in the eastern part. Similarly, "setting" is indeed

<margin>Setting properly defined</margin>
the first descent below the limiting circle. In another way, it is also the first darkening of any star when the Sun overshadows it. The third is <term>achroicos</term> <note>Achronychal setting.</note>, when, as the Sun sets, a star diametrically opposite sinks.

<margin>Differences of risings and settings</margin>
Of the risings and settings of which the Sun is the author—that is, the brightenings and hidings—some are morning and some are evening. It is called a morning rising whenever, the splendor of the Sun having been anticipated, some star appears before its rising, such as the <term>Dog Star</term><note>Sirius.</note>. It is an evening rising when a star is first seen rising after the setting of the Sun, as we say the New Moon rises.

<margin>Differences of settings</margin>
Similarly, a morning setting occurs whenever that star which had risen before the Sun the day before is overshadowed and hidden by the splendor of the Sun approaching it: as we see with the Moon. But an evening setting is when the Sun, having caught up to some star positioned in the west, first hides it with its flashing splendor. Regarding the position and order of the placement of the spheres

<margin>Order of the planets.</margin>
or even the orbs in which the Planets are said to be placed: some of the <term>Pythagoreans</term><note>Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras.</note> have said it is thus: That the sphere of the Moon is especially closest to the earth. After which, in the second place, is that of Mercury; above these, that of <term>Lucifer</term>; and above that, the Sun; beyond these the sphere of Mars; further still, Jupiter.

Folio XXVI.

### On Plato’s Timaeus.

The last and closest to the *Aplanes* <note>original: "aplanī"; the sphere of fixed stars which does not "wander" like the planets</note> and the stars adhering to it is the Saturnian star. It is understood that the Sun is located in the middle among the planets, just as the heart holds its place within the vital organs with its excellence. Alexander of Miletus <note>A 1st-century BC polymath and historian</note> agrees with these views, speaking thus:

<margin>Alexander of Miletus.</margin>
> They repeat their allotted orbits in lofty windings.
> The golden Moon flies in her chariot close to the lands.
> Above her is carried the Cyllenian <note>Mercury, born on Mount Cyllene</note>: the nurturing Cytherean <note>Venus</note>
> Laughing with the nectar of the heavens shines far and wide.
> Borne by a four-horse team, the fiery Sun circles
> Fourth among the ethereal goals, whom then from above
> The one powerful in arms <note>Mars</note> looks down upon: the sixth, the Phaetontic glow <note>Jupiter</note>
> Looks up at the high star in the winter cold <note>Saturn</note>.
> These seven distinctions of the plectrum of the white lyre
> You, O Father, have joined in a harmonious examination, just as you resemble them.

<margin>Pythagoras</margin>
It is a Pythagorean dogma that the world consists of harmonic proportion, and that the celestial distances, with intervals congruent and consonant to one another, produce musical sounds by their immense force and the speed of their rotation. Eratosthenes <note>The Greek mathematician and geographer</note>, following him, agrees that musical sounds are produced by the motion of the stars; but Eratosthenes says the order of their placement is not the same. For immediately after the Moon, he gives the second height from the Earth to the Sun, recalling the fable that Mercury, having recently invented the lyre, as he first ascended to heaven, passed through those things which resounded in an organic mode by the motion of the planets, and marveled at the similarity to the lyre he had invented: that the image of the work he invented was found also in heaven in the arrangement of the stars. This was the cause of the harmony: he recounted that he first passed the globe of the Moon from the Earth, after which was that of the Sun; then Mercury (of *Stilbon* <note>The "Gleaming One," a Greek name for Mercury</note>); and the others up to the highest and loftiest *Aplanes*. The mathematicians, however, do not all set this or any single arrangement of the

<margin>Mathematicians</margin>
wandering stars: but they place the Sun indeed after the Moon: beyond this, however, some place Mercury; while others place some other of the remaining stars.

-> [Diagram showing the Earth at the center of concentric circles. The outermost circle is labeled "i. Aplanes" (Fixed Stars), followed by "ii. Saturn," "iii. Jupiter," "iv. Mars," "v. Mercury," "vi. Venus," "vii. Sun," and "viii. Moon" closest to the Earth. Text within the circles notes the "Velocity" and "Magnitude" of each sphere, noting the Sun's velocity is "Equal" and the Fixed Stars have "Maximum" velocity.] <-

<margin>Plato</margin>
Plato also, in this very *Timaeus*, clearly measured the first height from the Earth up to the Lunar circle: and the second up to the Sun: also in the *Republic* <note>original: "politia"</note> noting not only the arrangement of the planets, but the magnitudes of each, their velocities, and also their colors—that is, their brightness and clarity—comparing the celestial axis to a spindle and the circles departing from the axis, both the outermost ones near the poles and the middle ones, to three whorls.

<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the motion of the planets and the relationship between celestial order and earthly change.</meta>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

¶ It remains to add to the discussion held concerning the wandering stars <note>The planets</note> this point: that some of them are "followers," which the mathematicians call *hypoliptical* <note>original: "hypolipticas"; from Greek *hypoleiptikos*, meaning trailing or falling behind</note>: such as the Sun and the Moon. For these fires never make a passage toward the preceding signs <note>westward relative to the stars</note>, but always toward those that follow; for that reason, they neither come to a standstill in their motion nor ever move backward. Other stars, however, sometimes lead the way and sometimes are left behind: as the rest <note>the other five known planets</note> which at times seem to stand still and at times to return by a thick <note>slow or dense</note> motion. 

<margin>The following-nature of planets.</margin> Indeed, "following" is the sight and appearance of a star proceeding as if toward the following signs and passing toward the east: for example, from Cancer to Leo. 

<margin>Station.</margin> "Station" is the sight and appearance of a star remaining for a long time in the same place next to another fixed and non-wandering star. 

<margin>Retrogradation.</margin> Furthermore, "retrogradation" <note>moving backward</note> is the sight and appearance of a star after a station, as if it were a projection onto a path contrary to its prior journey. All these things seem to us to happen in this way: but in reality, they do not happen as they appear. The cause of the error in contemplating them is this: that since these same planets are carried through their own circles or spheres, and below the heights of the starless sphere <note>original: "aplanos"; the sphere of fixed stars</note>, they seem to us—viewing from the region of the earth with the obstruction of the airy body hindering our sight—to traverse the outer circle of the Zodiac. It must be done, therefore: that once the causes of the errors are explained and demonstrated, the false opinion may rest. 

The constitution of the whole world is thus directed from whole bodies and materials by the rudders of providence toward a motion suitable for eternity: and displaying a globe-like form similar to its own motion. But for the numbering and revolutions of times, and for the alterations and diversity of earthly things, the varied motion of the wandering stars has given authority to all the events that now happen. For the simple and uniform starless sphere, being of the same nature, is a fixed and always the same revolution, equal and ordered: but that of the wandering stars is indeed circular itself, yet neither equal nor unordered, but varied: not one, but multiform. 

But truly, from beneath the Moon down to us, there is every kind of motion, and every kind of change, exactly as it is in the old verse of Naevius: 

<margin>†Exquisite.</margin> †*Out of the spoils, a thousand swarms of the madness of thieves.* <note>original: "Exuuiis rabies furarum examina mille." This is a fragmented or corrupt quote from the early Roman poet Naevius, used here to illustrate earthly chaos.</note>

For generation, and likewise death, are in this place, as well as increases and decreases: and every kind of change, and transition from place to place. All these passions <note>undergoings or experiences</note> draw their origin from the motion of the planets. Yet this is said for this reason: lest anyone think those things concerning the eternal and blessed cause of things born and dying were so arranged <note>i.e., that the planets are themselves chaotic</note>; but rather that those celestial things have a course consistent with their own happiness, while these earthly things, from a certain accidental and somewhat consenting fellowship with them, imitate divine happiness as far as they can. 

For so that the motion of the entire thing might be similar, and always rotate in a circle—this very agitation being the life, act, and operation of the world and the divine life—it was necessary for the earth to remain in the middle, around which those things carried through the middle might go. Furthermore, with the earth remaining, it was necessary for fire to take up a different region, light and high-wandering, which followed its material. Since these were widely distant, reason demanded that the other materials of air and likewise water be placed in the middle: for the sake of continuing the whole body. Furthermore, with these continued into a single series, as it were, it was finally necessary that there be some transfusion from one element to another: since their "forest" <note>original: "sylua"; used here as a translation of the Greek *hyle*, meaning matter or raw material</note> is changeable on both sides, while the force and nature of the materials themselves are contrary. 

Therefore, the cause of changes is from the varied motion of the planets. For if there were one and the same revolution, just like that of the fixed stars, with all stars observing one and the same mode, not even this earthly diversity would occur. But as it is, the revolutions and equinoxes, the progressions and retrogradations, through latitude and through height—especially of the Sun and Moon, but then of the other stars as well—change the turns of the seasons: and they bring in all this mutation and inconstancy. Which inconstancy, indeed, does not happen exactly as it appears to us. Nothing...

<summary>Chalcidius explains the astronomical concepts of planetary station and retrogradation as optical illusions caused by our perspective from Earth. He argues that while the heavens are orderly, the complex movements of the planets govern the constant change, birth, and decay found in the terrestrial realm.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, planetary motion, retrogradation, station, elements, Chalcidius, Naevius, cosmology, Zodiac, providence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically addressing the apparent irregularities in planetary motion compared to their actual divine, uniform movement.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus

For there is no inconstancy in divine acts; rather, it only appears so because, while those bodies remain fixed and immovably attached to their own circles—through which they are carried with a constant and ordered pace—our sight imagines them to be carried through the higher curves of the zodiacal circle. Thus, from an orderly logic and uniform motion, there arises a seemingly contrary motion according to the sight of men.

<margin>**Uniform motion.**</margin>
Uniform motion is that which covers equal spaces in equal cycles of time without slowing down or speeding up.

<margin>**Ordered motion.**</margin>
Ordered motion, however, is that in which the body neither meets resistance nor moves backward, but arrives at each successive point with a moderate pace. Yet, all the planets seem to us to move with a non-uniform motion; indeed, some seem to be carried along by a disordered agitation. What cause, therefore, shall we give for this error and perceived "getting ahead"?

<margin>**The cause of the error.**</margin>
It is the cause previously mentioned and understood by Pythagoras <note>The text attributes the mathematical understanding of planetary orbits to the Pythagorean tradition, which heavily influenced Plato.</note>: that while they are carried along staying within their own spheres, they are thought to be carried through the Zodiac because the sharpness of our sight fails. This may be considered in one of the wandering stars—indeed, in the one through which all things are seen: the Sun. Let there be a Zodiac circle marked **A B C D** <note>original: "Α Β Γ Δ"</note> revolving around; and let the letter **θ** <note>Theta</note> represent the center of this circle and the entire universe, where we say the Earth remains packed tight. Through this center, let two lines be drawn, bisecting the circle into four equal dimensions: **A-C** and **B-D**. Let each be understood as a diameter: **A** located at the beginning of Aries, **B** at the beginning of Cancer, **C** in the region of Libra, and **D** in Capricorn.

->[Diagram of the Zodiac showing the four seasons and the Earth at the center θ. The quadrants are labeled with the number of days the Sun appears to spend in each: Spring (94.5 days), Summer (92.5 days), Autumn (88.125 days), and Winter (90.125 days).]<-

Therefore, the Sun, approaching **A**, is seen to create the vernal equinox; at **B**, the summer solstice <note>original: "aestivam conversionem" - the turning point of the sun</note>. Likewise at **C**, the autumnal equinox, and at **D**, the winter solstice. It is seen to travel through these four equal parts of the world—**A-B**, **B-C**, **C-D**, and **D-A**—in unequal and disparate times. Indeed, from the vernal equinox to the summer solstice, it completes the intervening space of heaven in almost 94 and a half days and nights. From the summer solstice to the autumnal equinox, it arrives in 92 and a half days and nights. Proceeding further from the autumnal equinox to the winter solstice, it arrives in 88 days and the eighth part of one day. The remaining space lying between the winter solstice and the vernal equinox it completes in almost 90 days and the eighth part of a day. Because of this, it is believed by the consensus of all that it completes the entire circuit of the circle in nearly 365 days and nights and a fourth part of a day. In the region of Gemini, it is slowest; in the borders of Sagittarius, it is swift. It is at a medium pace in the transition of both Virgo and Pisces.

Yet, it is impossible that beings endowed with divinity should do or suffer anything disordered against their own nature. From this, it appears that while the Sun and the other stars travel through their own circles uniformly and in an ordered fashion, to us looking from the region of the Earth, they seem to travel through the circle **A B C D**, which is not the Solstitial circle but the Zodiac. Because the Sun does not proceed through this Zodiac circle, but through another Solstitial circle which is its own: if indeed the center point of both circles were one and the same at **θ**, the Solstitial circle, divided by the same logic as the Zodiac, would be found with the same faults and inequalities. And in the same way **A B C D**...

<margin>d iii</margin>

<summary>This page explains that the apparent irregular speed of the Sun and planets is an optical illusion caused by the observer's position on Earth. It uses a geometric diagram of the Zodiac to show how the Sun appears to spend different amounts of time in each quadrant of the year despite its actual uniform motion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the mathematical models used to explain the apparent irregularity of the Sun's movement. It follows previous discussions on planetary motion and the structure of the cosmos.</meta>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

...the four parts of the circle, he would traverse some in more days and others in fewer. But if reason demonstrates that men falsely believe this to be contrary to the nature of the stars, it will certainly be clear that the center point of that sphere through which the Sun moves is not located in the Earth <note>original: ⊕, the symbol for Earth</note>. Therefore, the circle of the Sun will either contain the Earth within itself—but not as its center point or midpoint—or it will pass through the Earth itself, or it will exclude the Earth entirely from its embrace. However, it is impossible for the Sun to go through the Earth. For earthly things would be consumed by the Sun’s heat; and since the globe of the Sun would always be above, there would be perpetual day, and no night would follow, as the Sun would never set. It remains, therefore, that the Earth is either within the circuit of the Solstitial circle <note>The "Solstitial circle" here refers to the Sun's own orbital path</note> or outside of it. It will be shown that both assumptions are reasonable: that is, either the Earth is within the circuit of the Solstitial circle, or outside it in the manner of an epicycle <term>epicycle: a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one</term>. This matter has indeed created a dispute among mathematicians. For some assert that the planets are carried by eccentric spheres—that is, those which contain the Earth within them, but not as their own center point—while others prefer epicycles, which are separate from the Earth and do not hang directly over it.

Let the eccentric Solstitial circle be **E Z H K**, and let it have a point immediately below the circuit **E Z**, namely where **M** is. This circle being divided into three hundred sixty-five days and a quarter of one portion, to balance these parts, the circuit **E Z** will contain ninety-four and a half portions <note>original: "semis", meaning a half</note>. **Z H** will truly be ninety-two and a half parts. Likewise, **H K** will have eighty-eight parts and one-eighth of a part. The remaining circuit **K E** will consist of ninety parts and one-eighth of a part.

->[Image: An astronomical diagram illustrating an eccentric orbit. A central point M is the center of an outer circle labeled with points B, N, and A at the top, and Γ and Δ on the left and right respectively. Offset from point M is an inner circle labeled E, Z, H, K, which has its own center marked with the symbol for Earth (a circle containing a cross). Radial lines extend from center M through the inner circle to the outer Zodiac circle. Within the four quadrants of the inner circle E Z H K are inscribed the values: "94 1/2", "92 1/2", "88 1/8", and "90 1/8". A star-shaped compass rose marks the center point M.]<-

It is necessary, therefore, that when the Sun approaches that part in which **E** is—while we are positioned at the center point of the whole world, the Earth, and looking out from there as far as we can—it then appears to be carried over **A**. At that time, that region is not of the Solstitial circle but of the Zodiac, which is of a much higher altitude, which the sight cannot reach. And so, passing uniformly through the circuit **E Z** (which is larger than the other three), completing the larger circuit in more days as is necessary, when it reaches **Z**, it will appear to have reached **B**. Thus, having traversed the circuit **A B**, it is believed to have completed an equal fourth part of the Zodiac circle in more days than the principle of uniformity requires.

Again, having traversed the circuit **Z H** of its own circle (the second in magnitude) uniformly in ninety-two and a half days (for the circuit is of that many parts), when it reaches **H**, it appears to us to have reached **Γ** <note>Greek letter Gamma</note>, having measured out the circuit **B Γ** as if it were equal to the first, but in fewer days. In the same way, traversing the smallest circuit **H K**, which is eighty-eight and an eighth parts, when it has traveled for that many days and reached **K**, to those looking from the region of the Earth it will indeed appear to be carried above **Δ** <note>Greek letter Delta</note>; it will be thought to have traversed the circuit **Γ Δ** (equal to the others) in fewer days. By a similar persistence of error, having measured the circuit **K E** in ninety days and an eighth part according to the number of portions, and being finally restored to **E**, finishing its annual winding path <note>original: "anfractu"</note>, it is thought to have measured the circuit **Δ A** equal to the others with an unequal and uneven pace; and it is not represented as being at **E**, the beginning of its own circle, but at **A**, the summit of another circle, namely the Zodiac. Because if of the two circles...

<summary>Chalcidius explains the eccentric model of the Sun's orbit, demonstrating how a uniform circular motion appears irregular to an observer on Earth because the Earth is not at the center of the Sun's path.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, eccentric orbit, epicycle, Sun, Earth, Zodiac, geometry, Chalcidius, Timaeus commentary</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the astronomical commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically addressing the apparent irregularities of solar motion using geometric models.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus
<note>original: "In Timeum Platonis"</note>

-> d iiii <-

...of the circles, that is, the two points of the greater Zodiac and the smaller Solstitial eccentric <note>An eccentric circle is one whose center does not coincide with the Earth's center</note> are joined: and let there be a line **m o**, then let the line **n z** be drawn through this, because the center of the circle **ε ζ η κ** is **m**, the lines **n m** and **m z** will be equal. Therefore, the line **n m** is greater than **z o**. Consequently, **h o** is much greater than **o z**. Therefore, when the Sun is carried through **n**, further removed from the earth (that is, from **o**), it will appear smaller to us from a distance, and also slower: which occurs near the twenty-fifth degree <note>literally: "fifth half part," a common way to denote 25 degrees</note> of **Gemini**. But when it is carried through **z**, being nearest to the earth, it will be thought larger and faster to those watching. This finally occurs near the twenty-fifth degree of **Sagittarius**. It is of medium size and speed when it passes through either **Pisces** or **Virgo**. And since we have demonstrated that the irregularity of the Sun's motion is falsely perceived in eccentric circles: now, if it is carried by **epicycle** <term>Epicycle: a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one</term> spheres as some believe, we shall nonetheless teach by the displayed form of the epicycles that it completes its annual courses with moderated and tempered steps.

<margin>Epicycle</margin> An **epicycle** is the name given to a sphere which is carried along some circle. Let there be even now the circle of the **Zodiac** which the marks **a b γ** limit. But let the Solstitial circle, excluding the center of the Zodiac circle (that is, **ε ζ η κ**), have its own center **m**. And from the point **o**, with the interval **m**, let it describe the circle **m o n z**: and let the epicycle **ε ζ η κ** be understood to be swept along with the rotation of the entire universe <note>original: "universi mundi vertigine"</note> similarly to the other fires <note>The stars and planets were often described as celestial fires</note> from the east to the west in a daily whirling: yet to be carried naturally against the flight of the whole world: and the Sun, again placed in the same epicycle, moves according to the turning of the whole world.

<image-desc>A geometrical diagram illustrating astronomical theory, specifically the motion of an epicycle. It features a large outer circle (representing the Zodiac) with points labeled A, B, Γ, and Δ. Inside this, a circular path (the deferent) is marked with points M, O, N, and Z. Four small circles (epicycles) are drawn at these points along the path. These epicycles are labeled with Greek letters ε, ζ, η, and κ. Lines radiate from the center point H to the various positions of the epicycle, showing angles of sight and geometric relationships.</image-desc>

<column-break/>

Therefore, the epicycle **ε ζ η κ**, traveling through the described circle **m o n z** by its natural motion against the direction in which the rotation of the whole world is moved, completes this course moderately and uniformly in the space of a year. And the Sun, likewise placed in this same epicycle, undertakes a motion contrary to the motion of its own epicycle, but follows the company of the whole world. There, therefore, the epicycle **ε ζ η κ** walking through the circle **m o n z**: and when it arrives at the letter **o**, it will have traversed a fourth part of the world. Then the Sun will proceed from **ε** to **κ**. The Sun will therefore be where the letter **o** is. Yet to us looking from the earth (that is, **o** <note>The text uses 'o' for both a point on the path and the observer's position; context suggests the central earth</note>) with a direct line of sight, it seems to be at the mark **B**. Thus, when it has completed a fourth part of the World, it will seem to have completed a smaller fourth. And again, the same epicycle having set out from the region of the letter **o** arrives at **n**, and the Sun will proceed from **κ** to **η**, it will be where **n** is. Yet to us looking with a direct sight, it will seem to be where the letter **γ** is. Thus, when it has completed an equal fourth part of the world from letter **B** to letter **γ**, it will be seen to have traversed a greater fourth more quickly and rapidly. Again, let the same epicycle complete another equal part of the World, that is, **n z**. Let the Sun also, as in the previous ones, proceed from **η** to **ζ**: it will be where **z** is: but to us it will seem to be where the letter **Δ** is. Thus, when it has traversed an equal fourth part of the world, it will be thought to have traversed a greater part of the Zodiac circle **γ Δ**. Finally, let the same epicycle traverse the remaining quadrant **z m**: and when it arrives at **ε**, it is restored to its place after a year.

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the astronomical discussion of planetary motion, specifically focusing on the Sun's apparent movement through the Zodiac using the epicycle model, and transitioning into a critique by Aristotle.</meta>

# XVIII Chalcidius’s Interpretation

The Sun will then appear to be where **A** is, and it will be thought to have reached the boundary **AA** of the zodiac circle. By this reasoning, it is clear that even according to the motion of the epicycle <note>An epicycle is a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one; used in ancient astronomy to explain complex planetary paths.</note>, those things which appear to us happen differently than they do in reality. For the Sun appears slower and smaller to our sight when it is, for instance, in Gemini. But it appears largest and most rapid when it is in Sagittarius, and not without reason. Indeed, while the epicycle **θ ζ η κ** moves through the circuit **μ ο η ρ**, the Sun, proceeding from **α β α** to **κ**, makes a delay by moving contrary to the direction its own epicycle is carried; thus it is carried more slowly toward **ο**, and traverses the circuit **μ θ ο** more slowly, and the zodiac circle is thought to have passed through the region **α β** much more slowly. And again, when the aforementioned epicycle moves toward the circuit **ο ν**, the Sun, finally proceeding from **κ α** to **π**, will appear to run into the world’s rotation, and aided by it, to pass through the quadrant of the zodiac quickly and more rapidly. In the same way, when the epicycle moves through the circuit **π ρ**, the Sun, finally proceeding from **π** to **ζ** as if outrunning the motion of its own epicycle, seems to precipitate its course through the Zodiac. And again, when the same epicycle moves through the circuit **ζ m**, the Sun, proceeding from **ζ** to **ε** against the direction its epicycle is carried, makes a delay and is carried more slowly to **m**, and traverses the quadrant **ζ m** more slowly, and is thought to have traveled through the quadrant **Δ Α** of the zodiac circle much more slowly. Thus, the epicycle will complete its course in a turning year, and the Sun its natural course. And in the circuit of the Sun, the greatest distance is **Α θ** to **ζ**, that is, from the Earth to the highest limit of the solstitial epicycle; the smallest distance, however, is at its lowest limit.

In the same way, although the other planets <term>planetes: wandering stars</term> are carried while remaining fixed in their own spheres, they appear to us to be carried through the Zodiac for the reasons demonstrated above. The same understanding of slowness and speed will achieve the explanation for all the wandering stars, although the others complete their courses in different times and not in the same ones as the Sun—to such an extent that some of them, on account of excessive slowness, sometimes seem to stand still <note>Stationary points in planetary motion.</note> and sometimes to move backward <note>Retrograde motion.</note>. For the Sun, traversing a smaller annual circle, seems to us to run into smaller deviations whether when it is exalted, or when it is brought low, or when it departs from the middle toward different regions of the sky; and it closes all that irregularity within the limit of a turning year. Because the return to the starting points for the other planets happens individually for each of them according to the size of the circles they traverse and the length of time in which they return to the beginning after completing their course, they are thought, not without reason, to undergo various and multiform conditions of motion. Indeed, one completes its orbit in two years, another in twelve years, and a third in nearly thirty. From this it happens that our sight, noting the motion of the stars without the understanding and memory of the shortcut of one star resulting from the brevity of its circle, or likewise the slowness of others resulting from the traversing of vast orbits, imagines that some planets go ahead, others stop, and others "regrade"—that is, are carried backward. This appears least of all in the circuit of the Sun, of Lucifer <note>The planet Venus as the Morning Star.</note>, and of Stilbon <note>The planet Mercury.</note> on account of the brevity of their circles and the narrowness of the time within which they complete their courses; and it is even much more so in the motions of the Moon. Indeed, in the motions of these, no station or backward movement is discerned, but only in those whose larger circuits are traversed in a corresponding length of time.

Aristotle, however, contrary to the opinion of everyone, teaches that the stars are neither carried while the ethereal body is at rest (as if by a loose and free motion), nor moved with or against the motion of the whole universe; but rather that they are all drawn by one and the same natural rotation of the world. He likewise does away with the opinion of both eccentrics and epicycles, because he says that the true and solid bodies of the stars cannot be carried by circles—that is, by painted lines lacking a body. For how far can a body be bound by the connection of an incorporeal thing? Rather, he says there are spheres...

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically addressing the celestial mechanics of planetary motion and the phenomenon of retrograde motion.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus. Folio XXIX.

...certain properties consistent with the nature of that fifth body <note>The "fifth body" refers to the ether or quintessence, the celestial element distinct from the four terrestrial elements.</note>: and that these are carried through the whole heaven with various and diverse motions. Some of these are the largest, others the smallest; some are moved from on high, while others are pressed down to the lowest depths. And now, some are solid houses <note>original: "casas solidas" - likely referring to the crystalline spheres or fixed positions</note>, others containing the bodies of the stars; and all these, just like the other fixed stars, are embedded in the heaven. They travel from diverse regions and from lower and higher places toward the west. This is the cause for the depiction of the shapes of the circles: because the convergence toward one place from diverse quarters of the heaven outlines, as it were, the tracks of various paths—just as if ships from all regions of the sea were brought to one and the same port at different times. And enough has been said regarding the inconsistent wandering of the straying stars <note>term: "stellae errantes" - the planets, from the Greek 'planētēs' meaning wanderer</note>.

¶ It follows that, since some of them seem to us at times to stand still in their progress, at times to move backward, and sometimes to run through certain signs of the Zodiac circle, and at other times to remain behind and be left, the cause of this appearance should be considered.

<margin>The cause of retrograde motion.</margin>
Let the Zodiac, therefore, be **A B C D**, the center point of which is **$\theta$** <note>representing the Earth or the observer's position</note>. Let the epicycle <note>term: "epicyclus" - a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one, used in ancient astronomy to explain planetary paths</note>, or the wandering path of any star, be **E F G**, the center point of which is the letter **M**. Through this center, as if through its own axis, let the same epicycle be carried with the star located within it, with a motion equal to the Zodiac circle—that is, from east to west. Let there also be drawn from the region of **$\theta$** two oblique lines touching both outer edges of the epicycle, **$\theta$ F B** and **$\theta$ G D**; and through the center point of the epicycle **M**, let the line **$\theta$ M A** be drawn upward.

->[Geometric Diagram of an Epicycle within the Zodiac]<-

Therefore, when the star is at **F**, to us looking with a direct gaze from the earth, it will seem to stand in the region of the Zodiac where **B** is. And when it has traversed the arc **F E**, it will be thought to have passed through the Zodiac arc **B A**, and to have progressed toward the preceding signs. Similarly, when the arc **E G** has been traversed, it will seem to have passed through the Zodiac arc **A D**, and even now to have hastened toward the preceding signs. Conversely, when the region **G F** has been traveled, it will seem to us to have finally stepped back through **A D** to **B**, having encountered the following signs; and for that reason, it is said to be retrograde <note>term: "regradata" - moving backward against the usual order of the zodiacal signs</note>.

<margin>The cause of station.</margin>
And when it does not depart far from **F**, it will even now be thought to linger as if in the same place <note>This refers to the "stationary point" where a planet appears to stop before changing direction</note>. But truly, when it has departed further, and the transition has reached **G** by the limit of **E**, it is thought to stand again at **D**, and to have run through the preceding signs; and departing further from **G**, to have stepped back through **G F** to **B**.

<margin>The opinion of the Mathematicians.</margin>
However, the opinion of the mathematicians is not the same as that of the philosophers. The mathematicians do not pay attention to those things which are seen, but to the natural motion of the stars. Indeed, they assert that no star is moved by a diverse or contrary motion against the motion and revolution of the universe, but that all are carried uniformly according to their own nature. I shall therefore demonstrate how, even according to the Mathematicians, the epicycle **E F G** may be carried against the motion of the whole world. Therefore, when the star is at **G**, it will seem to be near **D**, and when it has traversed the arc **G E**, it will be believed to have passed through the arc **A D** of the Zodiac circle, proceeding according to the order of the following signs. Moreover, when the arc **E F** has been traversed, it will even now be thought to progress toward the following signs. Furthermore, having measured the arc **F G**, it will be thought to stand again at **D**, having returned backward through the arc **B A** to **C D**; and for that reason, it is retrograde. And thus, by the reasonings of both the physicists and the mathematicians...

d v

<meta>catchword: reasonings</meta>

<summary>This page explains the astronomical phenomenon of retrograde motion and planetary stations using a geometric model of epicycles. It contrasts the observational perspective of "philosophers" with the theoretical models of "mathematicians" regarding uniform celestial motion.</summary>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the astronomical explanations for planetary movements and eclipses. The previous section likely established the order of the celestial spheres.</meta>

# Chalcidius's Interpretation

...so that the appearances of their stations, progressions, and retrogradations <note>The apparent stopping, forward, and backward movement of planets as seen from Earth.</note> may be revealed. It would be a long task to follow through by demonstration what the maximum, middle, and minimum altitudes of the wandering stars <note>planets</note> are, and to what extent they occur, whether they are carried along eccentric circles or epicycles. Therefore, we shall come to the demonstration of their "meetings," which we are also accustomed to call by another name, "conjunctions"; likewise of their obstructions, outpourings, and sudden darkenings. Furthermore, the beginning of the discussion shall be as follows. Because our sight is naturally extended in a straight line, and there is moreover a far-off, high, and eminent sphere which is called the *aplanes* <note>The sphere of the fixed stars, from the Greek *aplanes* meaning "unwandering."</note>; beneath which are placed the spheres of the planets, arranged as we demonstrated above: it is clear that the globe of the Moon, which is the lowest and closest to the Earth, stands in the way of the other stars located beyond it. It overshadows all things, both fixed and wandering, whenever it casts itself in a straight line between our sight and any of the stars.

<margin>The Sun.</margin>
The Sun, however, which the Moon sometimes obstructs, hides the other higher stars in a different manner: either when, having become close, it covers the lights of the others with the brightness of its own light; or when it strikes back against our sight by its own intervention.

<margin>Stilbon <note>The Greek name for Mercury, meaning "The Gleaming One."</note>.</margin>
Indeed, Stilbon and Lucifer <note>Venus, the "Light-Bringer."</note> and the higher stars, when interposed, remove other stars from our sight; and they frequently obstruct one another because of unequal measurements, the slant of their orbits, and diversities of position, as they are made higher or lower than one another in turn. For a pure perception is difficult in their case, because of their nearly inseparable meeting and companionship. Mercury alone, also, with its very small body and tiny fire, adheres most closely to the Sun and is overshadowed by the Sun's own inflammation.

<margin>Mars.</margin>
Mars frequently hides the two planets above itself. Phaenon <note>Saturn, "The Shining One."</note> also, the highest, is hidden by Phaeton <note>Jupiter, "The Radiant One."</note> obstructing it; and all the planets overshadow the fixed stars that loom over them in a straight line. Likewise the Moon, when distant from the Sun by the diameter <note>at 180 degrees, or Full Moon</note> and running into the shadow of the Earth, grows dark; yet this does not happen in every month, just as the darkening of the Sun does not intervene in every meeting (that is, every New Moon), nor does the "labor" <note>A poetic term for an eclipse.</note> and darkening of the Moon occur at every full and perfect moon, because their circles differ from one another by an excessive slant. Indeed, the Sun is said to be carried beneath the very Balance <note>Libra</note> of the middle of the zodiac, turning aside only slightly to about half a degree, declining either toward the South or toward the North.

<margin>†Hipparchus <note>The famous Greek astronomer (c. 190–120 BCE).</note>.</margin>
But truly the Moon, as Hipparchus had asserted, is found to wander by ten degrees on either side. Therefore, if we consider both orbits as *epipedos*—that is, flat and without any thickness—both the Sun's and the Moon's, placed opposite each other in such a direct position that a single line drawn through the middle of the orbits marks a "spine" for the two flat circles: the diameter of both will be that same line. And its highest part, cutting the nearest circle, is called *katabainon* <note>original: καταβαίνον; "descending node."</note>, that is, "sub-dividing." The lowest part, cutting the larger circle equally upward, is called *anabibazon* <note>original: ἀναβίβαζον; "ascending node."</note>, that is, "supra-dividing."

<margin>Eclipse of the Sun.</margin>
->[A diagram showing the Sun, Moon, and Earth aligned to demonstrate a solar eclipse.]<-
Therefore, if the meeting of the Sun and Moon becomes direct, as if by the aim of a plumb-line, since their bodies are made close to each other by the conjunction, the Moon will cast itself before our sight and obstruct the narrow path of seeing, and keep the light of the Sun from us: although smaller, it blocks the much larger body because of the direct and therefore narrow path of our eyes. If, however, their meeting is not made according to the plumb-line, but there is a breadth of interval—and furthermore the sides decline somewhat from the straight line, so that one of the fires verges toward the North and the other toward the South—no darkening will occur.

<margin>Eclipse of the Moon.</margin>
The "labor" or failure of the Moon is demonstrated thus: because indeed when it runs into the shadow of the Earth, it appears to fail; this is well known among all people.

<summary>This page explains the mechanics of celestial occultations and eclipses, identifying how planets block one another and why solar and lunar eclipses only occur when the Sun and Moon align at their orbital nodes.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, solar eclipse, lunar eclipse, planetary conjunction, Hipparchus, nodes, zodiac, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the geometric properties of shadows and their application to celestial bodies and eclipses.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus

But why this does not happen always or in every month must now be explained.
The direct rays of light-providing fires are carried along, forming the shadows of obstructing bodies into various shapes. Therefore, when the fire providing the light is equal in size to the body from which the shadows spring: if both the fire and the body are spherical in shape, the shadows will be produced in the manner of a cylinder. For example, let the light-bringer and illuminator be **A B**, and that which is illuminated be **Γ Δ** <note>The Greek letters Gamma and Delta</note>. Let these be equal and both spherical. It is clear that the rays—one **A Γ**, the other **B Δ**—extended straight forward, are positioned alongside each other at an equal distance. Rightly so: because the diameter of circle **A B** is equal to the diameter of circle **Γ Δ**. Therefore, as the rays grow in height, ray **A Γ Ε** will be distant from ray **B Δ Ζ** with equal stiffness—that is, without any inclination <note>meaning the lines are parallel and do not lean toward or away from each other</note>. And thus **Γ Ε** will stand apart from ray **Δ Ζ** without inclination, and each ray, though increased to infinity, will grow with equal stiffness; nor will their peaks ever be joined or touch one another. Since this is the case, there is no doubt that the shadow of sphere **Γ Δ** is formed into the likeness of a cylinder, even if it increases through infinity.

<margin>Cylindrical shadow. This figure is called a cylinder.</margin>
<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing two equal-sized circles (A B and Γ Δ) representing a light source and an object. Parallel lines (AΓE and BΔZ) extend from the circles, illustrating a cylindrical shadow of constant width.</image-desc>

¶ If, however, that which illuminates is smaller, such as **Η Θ** <note>Eta Theta</note>, and that which is illuminated is larger, such as **Κ Λ** <note>Kappa Lambda</note>: it is manifest that the shadow of the orb **Κ Λ**, which is **Χ Μ** and **Λ Ν**, is shaped in the form of a basket <note>original: "calathi", referring to a lily-shaped or flared wicker basket</note>, yet this appearance does not change even if the shadow increases to infinity. For since the diameter of **Κ Λ** is greater than the diameter of **Η Θ**, surely the rays **Η Χ Μ** and **Θ Λ Ν**, stretched out to infinity, form a wider opacity the more they grow.

<margin>Basket-shaped shadow. This figure is called a calathoid.</margin>
<image-desc>A geometric diagram featuring a smaller circle (Η Θ) and a larger circle (Κ Λ). Lines (ΗΧΜ and ΘΛΝ) diverge as they extend past the larger circle, depicting a basket-shaped (calathoid) shadow that widens as it recedes.</image-desc>

Therefore, whether the shadow be cylindrical or basket-shaped, it would necessarily happen that many stars which hang above us would not be seen by us during the night, due to one or another of these obstructing shadows. But this does not stand to reason. It is necessary, therefore, that the illuminating globe always be larger than those which are illuminated.
¶ Thus, if the light-bringer excels in size, such as **Ξ Ο** <note>Xi Omicron</note>, and that which is illuminated is smaller, such as **Π Ρ** <note>Pi Rho</note>, and both are spherical: the shadow of circle **Π Ρ**, which is **Π Ρ Ζ**, is produced in the manner of a cone ending in a point, and is finished at that same place where rays **Ξ Π** and **Ο Ρ**, stretched upward, touch each other at point **Ζ**. This happens for the same reason: because the diameter of **Π Ρ** is smaller than the diameter of **Ξ Ο**, and for that reason the appearance of the shadow will be cone-like.

<margin>Cone-like shadow. This figure is called a cone.</margin>
<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a large circle (SUN) and a smaller circle (EARTH). Converging rays (ΞΠ and ΟΡ) meet at a point (Ζ). A third, even smaller circle (MOON) is shown within the resulting conical shadow, illustrating an astronomical eclipse model.</image-desc>

<margin>Hipparchus.</margin>
Because, therefore, Hipparchus <note>A famous Greek astronomer (c. 190–120 BCE) known for his work on the sizes and distances of the Sun and Moon</note> in that work written *On the Sizes and Distances of the Sun and Moon*, teaches that the magnitude of the Sun is one thousand eight hundred and eighty parts greater than the Earth, and that the Earth is twenty-seven parts greater than the Moon, and that the Sun is much higher than the Moon: it appears that the shadow of the Earth is made like a cone. Indeed, the rays of the Sun **Ξ Π** and likewise **Ο Ρ** narrow themselves near the diameter of the Earth **Π Ρ**, and illuminate everything to the right and left.
<margin>† extended.</margin>
The Earth, however, placed in the way of the Sun's light, with the light flowing around it, makes the shadow of the Earth † thin, † extended from the width of its diameter into a narrowness, and † thinned out even to the ultimate end of the narrowings. When a nocturnal eclipse occurs, the Moon, being distant in diameter from the Earth and Sun: as that which [lacks] its own light in the Moon... <note>The sentence breaks off, likely continuing on the next page to explain how the Moon enters this conical shadow.</note>

<summary>This page uses geometric principles to explain why the Earth's shadow must be a cone (conoid) rather than a cylinder or a flared basket shape. By citing the astronomer Hipparchus, the author demonstrates that because the Sun is larger than the Earth, the Earth's shadow tapers to a point, which explains the mechanics of lunar eclipses.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, astronomy, shadows, Hipparchus, Sun, Earth, Moon, lunar eclipse, cone, cylinder, Plato, Timaeus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's 4th-century Latin commentary and translation of Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the astronomical and mathematical construction of the World Soul.</meta>

# Chalcidius's Interpretation

<margin>The moon</margin> needs [light], and always borrows it from the Sun; and since it is smaller than the earth, it is hidden in darkness when the earth stands in its way. Furthermore, when the position of the sun, earth, and moon does not align in a direct line—so that a diameter line passes through the center points—but the Sun turns toward the north and the Moon toward the south (or vice versa), the Moon escapes the earthly shadow and suffers no obscuration <note>This describes why lunar eclipses do not happen every month.</note>.

And so much for these matters. Now, having traversed all this, let us come to the discourse itself. And by now, almost that entire mixed kind of essence <note>original: *essentiae*; referring to the substance of the World Soul</note> had been consumed by these kinds of divisions of parts. Then, [the Creator] cut this very sequence lengthwise and made two from one; and he joined them middle-to-middle in the likeness of the Greek letter **χ** <note>Chi</note>, and curved them into orbits until their ends met one another. Out of these three—namely, **Essence**, the **Same**, and the **Different**—he had mixed the soul. He had divided it into numbers, partly doubles up to the cube which is the first born of doubles <note>The number 8 (2³)</note>, and partly triples up to the cube which is the first born of triples <note>The number 27 (3³)</note>. He willed the intervals of these numbers to be woven together with other numbers, so that there might be a likeness of the body within the texture of the soul. Therefore, with the limits established—one at six, the other at twelve, which is double—he continued the interval between the limits of six and twelve with two means, eight and nine, using the power of the *epitrite* <note>a 4:3 ratio</note> and the *sesquialter* <note>a 3:2 ratio</note>. Just as the body of the world was made continuous by inserting the materials of air and water between the limits of fire and earth, so the intelligible members of the soul were connected by inserting the powers of numbers as if they were elements and materials, so that there might be some likeness between the soul and the body. Therefore, God did not cut this sequence from matter or a body, he says: as if one were to split a straight line **A B** lengthwise and make a **χ** <note>Chi</note> from the two segments, **Γ Δ Z**; then curve that very thing and make two circles intertwined with each other:

<image-desc>Two geometric diagrams illustrating Chalcidius's commentary on the Timaeus. On the left, two lines cross to form an 'X' shape, representing the world-soul divided into the form of the Greek letter Chi. The vertices are labeled A, B, Δ, Γ, and Z. On the right, two concentric circles represent the celestial spheres (the Same and the Different). Points on the diameter and circumferences are labeled H (top), K (bottom), and Θ, M, Λ, N along the horizontal axis.</image-desc>

And these very circles he bound around with another outer circle, whose motion and revolution is always the same and uniform—that is, the circle of the **fixed stars** <note>original: *aplani*; the "unwandering" sphere</note>. Indeed, its circular agitation is always the same, with always equal velocity, requiring no place outside the path of its accustomed circuit. And the outer circle, which he calls the **Same**, is that which is highest in the globe of the fixed stars; which we call the **meridian**, and the Greeks call *mesembrinon* <note>original: μεσημβρινὸν</note>, embracing both poles; and with these, it revolves to delineate the fixed stars in the likeness of a globe. But the inner circle must be understood as that in which the order of the signs <note>the Zodiac</note> is carried around, under which the **planets** <note>original: *planetes*; "wanderers"</note> are borne. And for this reason, he named the outer motion the **Same**, because the flight of the fixed stars is always the same and least differing; but the inner motion he called the **Different**. For all the planets that wander beneath the Zodiac, although they are brought back in a circle, nevertheless perform diverse movements for various reasons and conflicting occurrences.

<summary>Chalcidius explains Plato's mathematical construction of the World Soul using harmonic ratios and the geometric shape of the Greek letter Chi to represent the intersection of the celestial equator and the ecliptic.</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, Timaeus, Chalcidius, harmonic ratios, astronomy, fixed stars, planets, Chi, celestial mechanics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato’s Timaeus, specifically discussing the astronomical motions of the celestial spheres and the soul of the world.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus

Fo. XXXI.

...they seem to make rotations, or rather to perform them: while they exalt and humble <note>referring to the rising and setting or the varying altitudes of planets</note>, they nevertheless proceed more slowly and sometimes more rapidly. For that reason, they are carried along by the motion of the entire universe: and yet they resist as much as they can, sometimes even wandering more widely. They are often thought to precede the signs <note>of the Zodiac</note>, often to remain behind, often to stand still, and often to move backward <note>retrograde motion</note>. However, that he <note>Plato</note> perceives and speaks of no others but these very ones, he himself makes manifest when he says: The outer circle indeed—which we have said was named the **Same**—he turned from the right side through the left side back to the right; the **Different**, moreover, he turned across the diameter toward the left side. For that same and constant revolution, that is the *aplanes* <note>original: "aplanes"; the sphere of the fixed stars which does not wander</note>, through which the meridian circle revolves, is moved by the right part of the world. 

<margin>The Right of the World.</margin> Furthermore, the right part of the world will be shown to be the East. "The Different," he says, "moreover across the diameter," that is the interior motion of the World, by which the planets are moved beneath the obliquity of the zodiac belt. 

<margin>The Interior of the World.</margin> Truly, regarding the "sides," since the body of the world is like a sphere and globular—and similar in every direction and not differing—likewise the right and left parts of the world do not seem rightly spoken of to those thinking of a sphere and looking only at its outward appearance. Yet because the world itself is a living being <note>an "animal" in the classical sense of a thing with a soul</note>, and an intelligent living being, it will have right-hand parts: certainly those in which is the beginning of motion, and from which part they are first moved, just like other living beings. Furthermore, this part of the world is in the East; for that reason I think Homer erred when, auguring by a bird, he said: *Flying to the right toward the dawn and the sun and the day.* <note>A reference to the Iliad; the translator notes that "right" in ancient augury depended on the direction the observer faced.</note> Nor indeed will there be a "top" or a "bottom" in the image of a ball: but in a living and animate ball, nothing prevents these things from being truly said. For let us grant to our body certain additions from the outside, made of mud or wax, until it swells into a globe: certainly the form will be changed, and with all members made similar in every direction and reduced to an un-sloping roundness, no right or left side is seen; neither feet at the bottom, nor a head at the top, since the members are hidden in coverings. But the principle of the vital substance makes the bottom, top, right, and left parts lie hidden within. Thus the diverse interface of the parts of the world is hidden on the surface by the indifferent similarity of the members: yet it is betrayed by motion and breathable <term>co-vegetation</term> <note>the shared life-force or "convegetatio" that binds the cosmos</note>, like a formed body and living being. 

Then he proceeds: *And he gave a rotation to that similar revolution in power and craftsmanship.* Indeed, he left that one as it was, undivided, and rightly so: for one sphere, which is called the *aplanes* <note>the fixed stars</note>, rotates many and innumerable stars, while many spheres carry around the individual planets. Yet its power, which it holds against the motion of all the planets, can be understood in another way: that its same and indifferent conversion carries around both the planets themselves and the other stars clinging to it in the course of night and day from East to West. 

<margin>From West to East</margin> From which they say the planets are participants in that changeable nature, which is named the **Different**, inasmuch as they are snatched by the whirling of the whole universe from the East to the West, and are finally brought back from the West to the East: as in sailing, when the ship is flying toward its destination by the pulse of the wind from the region of the prow, some of the sailors run back toward the stern. 

Then he says: *But he cut the interior six times.* The interior, that is, the circle of the **Different**. *And he fashioned seven unequal orbs according to the intervals of the double and the triple.* We hold in memory that the first description of the <term>Psychogony</term> <note>the "birth of the soul"</note> was so formed that one side was ordered from double numbers, and the other from triple numbers: and seven limits were made on each side, through which there are three intervals on each side. Therefore, upon this 

<margin>The Music of the Stars</margin> sketch by which he depicted the Soul, he outlines the image of a rival likeness and the appearance of the world. And he established seven circles of the planets: and he makes them stand apart from one another by musical intervals: so that according to Pythagoras, the stars rotated by harmonic motion [produce] musical sounds...

<summary>This page explains Plato's cosmology, specifically how the "Same" (fixed stars) and "Different" (planets) move in relation to one another. It uses the metaphor of a living animal to explain why a spherical universe can still have a "right" and "left" side, and introduces the musical-mathematical ratios used to space the planetary orbits.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Timaeus, celestial spheres, planetary motion, Psychogony, Pythagoras, harmony of the spheres, East and West, aplanes, Different and Same</keywords>
