<meta>This page continues the commentary by Chalcidius on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the mathematical and musical proportions of the World Soul and the arrangement of the planetary spheres.</meta>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

<note>Chalcidius was a 4th-century philosopher whose Latin translation and commentary on Plato's 'Timaeus' was the primary source of Platonic thought in Western Europe for eight hundred years.</note>

...produce melodies in their whirling: similarly as in the *Republic* <note>original: "politia"</note> he describes Sirens standing upon each of the circles. These, being rotated with the circles, evoke a single honey-sweet song, and from eight unequal sounds, one harmonious concord is stirred. Therefore, the "Aplanos" <note>The sphere of fixed stars; from Greek 'aplanes' meaning 'unwandering'.</note> will be the Reason of the soul. The "Planetes" <note>The wandering stars or planets.</note> represent motions such as anger, desire, and other movements of this kind. By their concord, the modified harmony of the entire life of the world is created. The arrangement of the planets also seems to agree with the division of the parts from which he constituted the soul: since he makes the first portion taken from the whole as one, and the smallest, from the Earth to the Moon; the second is double that, that is, what lies between the Moon and the Sun; the third is triple the first, namely that of Venus. The fourth is double the second, that is, four times the first, belonging to Mercury. The fifth section is eight times the first, belonging to Mars. He placed the sixth as triple the third <note>i.e., nine times the first</note>, the region or circle of Jupiter. Finally, the last section of twenty-seven parts belongs to Saturn.

<margin>
The ratio of the Fixed Stars.
The motion of the soul of the Planets.
</margin>

<image-desc>
A geocentric cosmological diagram consisting of concentric circles. At the center is a circle labeled "Earth." followed by rings labeled, from inner to outer: "Moon," "Sun," "Venus," "Mercury," "Mars," "Jupiter," and "Saturn." Between the rings are the following numerical markers, starting from the center: 1, 2, 3, 4, 8, 9, 27.
</image-desc>

As for his statement that God commanded those seven circles to be carried in a contrary motion, many understand it thus: that all are moved against the motion of the whole world; since indeed the revolution of that same motion always begins to move from the right side, so that these seven revolutions are understood to run against the one that carries them along. Others think not so, but that there is a diverse and contrary revolution among the planets themselves, and that they encounter one another in opposition, because a varied and multiform diversity is found in their circuits. Indeed, the revolutions of the Sun and Moon, companions to the motion of the universe, are delayed in their progress from their natural and established course by their eccentric or even epicycle <term>epicycle: a small circle whose center moves around the circumference of a larger one, used to explain retrograde motion</term> as much as they can be. Of the other five, the natural motion is understood to be that which resists the revolution of the whole world, even though that most powerful whirling seizes them. From this it happens that they seem to stop, and sometimes even to move backward. Furthermore, some of the wandering stars always set in the evening, lying hidden during the day in the brightness of the Sun, and appearing only when the night is spent before the rising of the Sun, such as the stars of Saturn, Mars, and Jupiter. On the contrary, while the Moon appears before all stars in the evening, in the morning it is hidden in the west. Lucifer <note>The morning star, Venus.</note> and Stilbon <note>The "Gleaming One," Mercury.</note> both rise and set in both ways. What of the fact that the other planets depart further from the Sun, even to the point that at certain times they are separated by the diameter <note>i.e., in opposition, 180 degrees apart</note>? Stilbon and Lucifer, however, are never separated by the diameter from the orbits of the Sun, being made quite close to it in either region of the world, whether eastern or western; but their courses are always closest to the Sun...

<summary>This page explains the harmonic and mathematical structure of the cosmos, linking the proportions of the World Soul to the distances of the planetary spheres and discussing their varying orbital motions.</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, celestial harmony, planetary spheres, Chalcidius, Timaeus, astronomy, retrograde motion, epicycles</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the motion of the planets and the nature of the World Soul as established by the Creator.</meta>

# On Plato's Timaeus. Folio XXXII.

they complete their courses; for that reason, Plato said that three move with equal and the same speed, while those other four move differently both to themselves and to these three. Yet he says all these diverse motions and contrary agitations of the planets are supported by reason: namely, that which he took care to manifest through continuous treatment. He then proceeds: Therefore, when the entire substance of the rational soul was born according to the will of the father, a little while later he fashioned all that is bodily within its enclosure; and applying the middle to the middle, he joined them with a suitable measure. But she <note>The Soul</note>, embracing the furthest reaches of heaven and poured around it in an outer embrace, covering it with her circuit and turning back into herself, inaugurated a divine origin of a tireless and wise life without intermission.

<margin>Heaven.</margin>
"Heaven" is both spoken of and understood in different ways. Partly as the surface of the world, which the Greeks call *Ouranos* <note>original: οὐρανόν</note>: as if it were the limit of our sight beyond which it cannot extend, whence it is called *ouranos* <note>original: οὐρανός; etymologically linked to "limit of seeing"</note>. Partly it is the sphere which is called *aplanes* <note>The sphere of fixed stars, literally "unwandering"</note>. Properly, however, it is all that rises from the lunar globe; but commonly it is whatever is above us: in which region the clouds gather, and somewhat higher where the stars are. For we say both that rains flow down from heaven, and that stars appear in heaven which are called comets, and other things which appear below the globe of the Moon. Using the term "Heaven," we also call out to the whole world. Therefore, he says the Soul of the world began from the midpoint even to the extremity of the mundane body: and from there even to the other extremity, poured around the whole globe of the body, she covered its entire circuit. From this it appears that the embrace was surrounded by the vital parts of the world through the outermost parts: that is, so that she might cherish it with vital vigor both within and without. For the universal body did not stand in need of the help or embrace of another body (since none remained), but the whole was filled with vital substance by the powers of an incorporeal nature.

<margin>P. says / O. says</margin>
<margin>Opinion of the ancients concerning the Soul of the World.</margin>
But as for that which is said, that the soul is stretched out from the center: some think it is said thus, not as if it were stretched out by a measurement made from the midpoint of the whole body, but from that part of the vital members in which the "High Priest" <note>term: Pontificium; here used metaphorically for the governing or sovereign part of the living being</note> of living is situated; and therefore they are called the vitals. Therefore, they pronounce that it should rather be understood that the vigor of the soul was infused into the mundane body not from the midpoint of the body, which is the earth, but from the region of the vitals, that is, from the Sun. Since the earth is immobile, but the Sun is always in motion. Likewise, the midpoint of the womb is immobile, while that of the heart is always in motion: since even the hearts of recently deceased animals may still carry on surviving motions. And for that reason, they say the Sun holds the principle of the heart, and the vitals of the whole world are placed in this fire.

<margin>The Sun holds the principle of the heart.</margin>
That the soul itself turns back into itself is certainly not to be understood as a bodily conversion being made: but by the gyres and windings of thought and memory, with the body being obedient to it; just as the body obeys the motions of our souls. And that was the beginning for the world, the inauguration of a tireless life without intermission. Excellently so, since a tireless life without intermission is propagated by time and its circuits.

<margin>Parts of Time.</margin>
<margin>Eternity.</margin>
Yet there is another certain perseverance of Eternity <note>term: Aevum; refers to timeless eternity or a duration beyond human time</note> in a more sublime majesty. For the parts of Time are years, months, days, and hours. But of Eternity there is neither any beginning nor any end, and for that reason it is indeterminate and perpetual. Therefore, when he introduced the generated world, he consequently devised the generation of Time as coeval with the world. And these things he himself will soon explain more broadly.

<margin>The Soul is incorporeal.</margin>
Now, however, he teaches that the soul is incorporeal, when he says: "And the body of heaven or the world was indeed made visible; but she herself is invisible, yet a partaker of reason and likewise of harmony." For when he calls her invisible, he pronounces that she is not at all subject to the senses. And that which is perceived neither by sight nor by the other senses cannot possibly be a body. Yet she is a partaker of reason: as...

<summary>This page discusses the creation and diffusion of the World Soul throughout the universe, debating whether its "center" is the Earth or the Sun (likened to the heart). It also distinguishes between the cyclical nature of Time and the perpetual nature of Eternity (Aevum).</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, Timaeus, Plato, Sun as heart of the world, incorporeality, Eternity vs Time, celestial motion</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's Latin commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the composition of the World Soul and the nature of time versus eternity.</meta>

# Chalcidius's Interpretation

It is clear that the rational nature lacks a body: which is indeed the proper and fitting sketch of the rational soul. Furthermore, he asserts that this same soul is modulated <note>arranged according to musical proportions</note>, and he recalls and repeats its origin and, as it were, certain elements from which he established it among the beginnings. He does this so that, being identical to those external origins—the indivisible and divisible substance, and likewise the same and the diverse natures joined together—it may easily recognize in things themselves the likeness and unlikeness of matters, the diversity of goodness and evil, and the disparity of the nature to be desired or cursed. For the soul, being diverse in its numbers, composed of proportions, packed with means <note>mathematical medians</note>, ordered by musical ratios, and even split six times and bound again with immortal bonds, is adapted to the diverse and varied motion of the entire mundane body: so that it may know all things and attain all things according to its own nature.

Finally, he adds: Therefore, since it was joined together from the nature of the Same and the Diverse with Essence mixed in, needing motion, and returning into itself in a circular revolution: when it encounters any substance, whether dissipatable <note>material/perishable</note> or indivisible, it easily recognizes what belongs to the Same and the Indivisible, and likewise what belongs to the Diverse and dissoluble nature. It sees the causes of all things that come forth, and from those things which happen, it measures what will be in the future. In these things, he clearly manifests that there is nothing that lies hidden from the soul; and he clearly reveals its intelligence when he says its motion is rational, without voice, without sound. When it looks upon something sensible, and the circle of the Diverse kind is carried without error, then true senses and certain reports give rise to right opinions and beliefs worthy of credit throughout the whole soul. Furthermore, when it beholds the indivisible kind, which is always the same, and the inner motion faithfully reports those things which are, then intellect and knowledge grow strong. Without voice or sound, the motion is Reason <margin>Reason. Speech.</margin> residing in the innermost shrines of the mind. This, however, differs from speech. For speech is the interpreter of the reason conceived in the soul. When this reason exerts itself in those things which are born and die, or are made by hand, and in short all things which are sensed—and yet this same motion of the inner mind is moved rightly—true opinions and those worthy of credit are born. Yet, a sufficiently certain and firm <margin>Opinion. True knowledge.</margin> knowledge does not result; for indeed there is a wide distance between knowledge and opinion. But when the soul establishes itself as a spectator of immutable things, which are always the same, and looks up to the intelligible nature, and in those same spectacles invigorates the sharp contemplation of the mind: then a motion similar to the motion of that globe which is called *aplanes* <note>original: "aplanes" - the sphere of the fixed stars</note>, being turned around without error, creates a true knowledge of the divine kind in the mind. Now he comes to the distinction between that <note>eternity</note> and time: and he teaches that because the intelligible world is the model for the sensible world—inasmuch as it is the living being holding primacy among the other living beings of the intelligible divinity—it remains through all eternity in unshaken stability. Its image here, the sensible world, will also be joined to a likeness of eternity that has been made and established. For time is, after all, the image of eternity <margin>Time is the image of eternity.</margin> remaining in its own state. Furthermore, time is not at all remaining, but rather always progressing and repeatable. Thus, just as the intelligible world exists through eternity, so the sensible world exists through all time. For the life of the models is one thing, and the life of the images is another. Rightly, at one and the same moment, the sensible world was built, and the successions of days and nights were established, the elements and sequences of time: from which months and years, its parts, are divided by reason and calculation. And when we assign these things to eternity, he says—that is, to the solitary nature—we do not rightly fashion parts of an indivisible thing. For we say, "it was," "it is," "it will be"; but to that <note>eternity</note>, only "to be" is appropriate. This assertion proceeded on account of those who doubt whether time, which is said to exist, is actually nothing. For they say that all past things have already ceased to be. If anything is imminent, it is not yet; and present things are neither plainly existing, nor entirely non-existing.

<summary>Chalcidius explains how the World Soul's composition allows it to perceive both the physical and divine realms, and defines time as a moving image of static eternity.</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, Timaeus, Chalcidius, eternity, time, reason vs speech, opinion vs knowledge, Sameness and Difference</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the nature of time and the motion of the celestial bodies (the Sun, Moon, Venus, and Mercury).</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus. Folio XXXIII.

...to be [unstable] because of the unstable and unbridled march of moments. For he contends that all things flow and pass away. Therefore, he says that the past part of time exists in such a way that it is understood to *have been*, not as existing in the present: as when we say that **Homer** *is* a divine poet <note>original: "Homerum esse divinum poetam" — meaning he exists in reputation/history, though not physically present</note>. Truly, he says the future exists in such a way that it does not yet exist or hold to the present: as when we say that by next year there *will be* the conclusion of a five-year period <term>lustrum</term> or an Olympic contest. Furthermore, regarding what happens in the present, it exists not as if it were seen to remain through any long duration, but as if it flows and passes by: as when we say the **Compitalia** <note>A Roman festival in honor of the Laes (household gods) celebrated at crossroads</note> is today, or any other public or private celebration; for indeed, certain measured moments of that same day have already passed, while some truly still remain. In the same way, we say the month is now January, or any other; or that a sacred contest is being held when, the boys' contest having finished, the adolescents are summoned, and after them in succession the young men of full age join the struggle.

What of the fact that we even say those things *are* which are not, when we wish to show that those same things are not [equal]? As when we say the side of a square is unequal to the other sides, or that the diameter is greater than the sides: for in saying this, we prove it is by no means equal to the sides, because it is dissonant. Or when the same **Plato** says that "matter" <term>sylva</term> <note>original: "sylvam" — used here as a Latin translation of the Greek 'hyle' (wood/matter)</note> exists in no substance: because no "woody" things have perfection; for while they are still raw matter, they are unformed and without order or appearance, like stones; yet in them is a natural possibility so that, with the addition of craftsmanship, an image may be made, or something else of that kind. But that which is seen to exist only by possibility and without effect is the least of things, as it lacks perfection. But this discussion, since it pertains nothing to the natural treatise but is rational [logic], shall be set aside.

<margin>The birth of time</margin>
Truly, he says the birth of time was necessarily established so that those same times might come under measurement, and the spaces of days, months, and years could be numbered. For this reason, the illuminations and necessary settings of the **Sun** and **Moon** were required, and the other wandering stars <note>planets</note> were placed upon the gyrations of the circles, commanded to perform a sevenfold motion in various and dissimilar windings <term>meanders</term> in that region which lies beneath the curvature of the Zodiac circle: the **Moon** in the first circuit nearest the Earth; the **Sun** truly in the second, distant from the Moon by the diameter. Then he places the fires of **Lucifer** <note>The morning star, Venus</note> and **Mercury**; he says they concur in that motion with the sun’s circuit, yet it is carried around by an agitation contrary to it. <margin>Lucifer</margin> <margin>Mercury</margin> Wherefore it happens that these stars overtake one another, and are in turn overtaken by each other. <margin>which motion</margin>

Why he says these stars are of equal speed he manifests himself, when he asserts that the courses are completed by all in the space of a turning year. But in such a way that, moving now more slowly, now more swiftly, they repeatedly overtake the Sun, and are repeatedly overtaken by the Sun. He says, however, that these fires also have a contrary power. This matter different people understand differently. For some think this contrariety arises from the fact that while the Sun is naturally carried always from the east to the west, just as the whole world is moved, it nevertheless completes its **epicycle** in the space of a year, the turning of which epicycle is contrary to the turning of the world. **Lucifer** and **Mercury**, however, always exert motions contrary to the world's revolution. But some think there is a contrary power in these stars because **Mercury** and **Lucifer** overtake the Sun's progress, and sometimes the Sun, slowing them down, overtakes them; since they undergo risings and likewise settings, flashings-forth and overshadowings, sometimes in the morning, sometimes as evening falls: now preceding, now left behind. For in this way they almost always seem to accompany the Sun. This happens to them because one half-way point and one single point belongs to the sun’s circle as well as to any other of these stars. Finally, <margin>Heraclides of Pontus on the circle of the Sun</margin> **Heraclides of Pontus**, when he described the circle of **Lucifer** and likewise of the **Sun**, and gave one point and one middle to the two circles, demonstrated how sometimes **Lucifer** becomes higher and sometimes lower than the **Sun**. For he says that both the Sun and Moon and Lucifer and all the planets, where their...

<meta>This page continues the astronomical commentary by Chalcidius on Plato’s Timaeus, specifically focusing on the movements of the planet Venus (referred to here as Lucifer and Hesperus) relative to the Sun.</meta>

-># Chalcidius’s Interpretation<-

whatever it may be: it is demonstrated by a single line extending from the point of the Earth through the point of the star. There will therefore be one straight line from the center of the Earth demonstrating the Sun; but two other straight line, no less straight, on the right and left, distant from the Sun by 50 moments <note>original: "L. momentis"; a 'moment' here refers to a specific unit of angular measurement or time used in ancient astronomy</note>, and 100 from each other. One of these lines, closest to the East, demonstrates **Lucifer** <note>The Morning Star</note> when Lucifer is at its greatest distance from the Sun, having been made near to the eastern regions. For this reason, it receives the name **Hesperus** <note>The Evening Star</note>, because it appears in the east in the evening after the setting of the Sun. The other line is closest to the West: when the same Lucifer is at its greatest distance from the Sun, having been made near to the western regions; for this reason, it is called **Lucifer**. Indeed, it is clear that it is called Hesperus when it is seen in the eastern parts following the setting of the Sun, but Lucifer when it sets before the Sun: and again, when the night is almost over, it rises before the Sun.

<note>The diagram shows Earth at the bottom (x) with two lines of sight tangent to the orbit of Venus. The Sun (SOL) is on the central axis (B). The distance between the Sun and the maximum elongation points (A and r) is marked as 50 moments.</note>

Therefore, let the point of the Earth and the sky be where the letter **x** is: and let the circle of the Zodiac be that upon which are the marks **A B r**, and let the circuit **A B** be 50 moments. Likewise, let the circuit **B r** be of just as many moments: and let the point of the Sun be on the line **x B** at the letter **x**. There will therefore be a line **x K B** which demonstrates the Sun, that is, the letter **B**: but let this same line move as much as the Sun moves through nearly every single daily moment. Similarly, let the other lines **x A** and **x r** be divided into 50 moments. Furthermore, let the line **x A** be in the Eastern part, and the line **x r** be in the Western part. This one, that is the line **x r**, sets earlier and rises earlier than the Sun. But that other one, **x A**, sets later and rises later. It is necessary, therefore, that these lines, **x A** and **x r**, demonstrate Lucifer: the letter **A** as Hesperus, namely at that time when the same star has departed furthest from the Sun. But that other line, **x r**, demonstrates the same star to be Lucifer in the morning times at the sign of the letter **r**. This, however, will become clearer if a circle is drawn through the line **x K B** which touches the two lines distant from each other, namely **x A** and **x r**, which demonstrate the measure of Lucifer's departure from the Sun.

But indeed Plato, and those who have held a more diligent examination of this investigation, affirm that the globe of Lucifer is somewhat higher than that of the Sun: which is bounded by the marks **Δ E Z H**, touching the line **x A** at letter **E**, and the line **x r** at **H**.

<note>This complex diagram illustrates the nested spheres of Venus and the Sun. The Earth (x) is the viewpoint. The sphere of Venus (Veneris) is shown with its points of maximum elongation (E and H) where the lines of sight from Earth are tangent to its orbit. The Sun's path is also indicated.</note>

Wherefore, when Lucifer, traversing its own circle, arrives at **E**, it will be seen located at **A**, at its furthest from the Sun, as if separated by all fifty moments, and turning toward the East and the day. For the Sun is seen only where the letter **B** is. Furthermore, when Lucifer is at **H**, it will be seen to stand at the height of **r**, likewise at 50 moments from the Sun, and further removed toward the West. When it stands either near **Δ** or near **Z**: there is no doubt it is closest to the Sun.

Chalcidius uses a diagrammatic approach to show how the planet's position in its sphere determines its visibility relative to sunrise and sunset.</summary>

# On Plato's Timaeus.

<margin>A precept.</margin>
it would seem to have happened: and to have made a conjunction, one more excellent far from the region of the earth at point A: the other nearest and closest to the earth at point Z. Now this must be weighed beforehand with more diligent observation: that the star itself, in its greatest withdrawal—whether that withdrawal be eastern or western—returns to the position in which it previously was, either Saturn <note>original: ♄</note> or Jupiter <note>original: ♃</note>, in approximately five hundred and eighty-four days. Thus it is clear that it traverses its entire circle, which is Δ Z H, in the aforementioned number of days, so that it travels the larger arc of the withdrawal, which is from point I to the west (that is, H A B), in four hundred and forty-eight days: but the smaller and lower arc, that is Z E A, in the remaining one hundred and thirty-six days. Indeed, the gliding from the greatest withdrawal from the west to the east is restored in this number of days: as the frequent observation of ancient men has made clear.

Again he adds these things below. Therefore, when each and all had been located with a motion fit and decent for themselves: namely those things which were consequent to come forth in time by vital bonds: when the bodies were constrained and made into living creatures <note>The author refers to the stars as "animals" or "living beings" (animalia) in the Platonic sense, possessing soul and intelligence.</note>, and learned what was commanded: he asserts that the celestial bodies, constrained by vital bonds—that is, the stars—became living creatures, and recognized what was commanded by God. Specifically, that each of the planets, reduced into their own spheres, should not only enjoy soul and life: but that the world and the soul should also use all these same things and be a participant in reason. For how could anything lacking reason understand what is commanded? Those things which the oblique motion of the "Different" nature <note>Plato's concept of the "Circle of the Different" (the ecliptic) vs. the "Circle of the Same" (the celestial equator).</note>, turning itself through the direct motion of the "Same" nature as if constrained, was carrying around: they were rotated partly in a larger and partly in a smaller circle: those passing through the measured space more quickly which were in a smaller circle: but more slowly those in a larger one, as they were driven around in a more extensive circuit.

<margin>The course of the Moon.</margin>
What the "Different" nature is has already been shown often in the preceding sections: that its motion is carried from the west to the east. Therefore, since this is interior, it must necessarily be contained by the exterior sphere: which is called the *aplanes* <term>aplanes: the sphere of the fixed stars, from the Greek for 'unwandering'</term>: and being slow itself in proceeding, it is snatched by the swifter *aplanes* into a contrary motion, and being caught, it seems to follow the one following it, and even to overtake the one overtaking it. Indeed, some of the planets complete their courses more quickly: others more slowly. Deservedly so. For the shortcuts of the smaller circles, compared against the windings of the larger ones, complete the spaces of the circles with modest expenditures of time: as the Moon, which traverses its circle in twenty-seven days (near the cubic number) <note>27 is 3 cubed (3x3x3), a significant number in Platonic numerology.</note>: while the Saturnian star returns to its original starting point in nearly thirty years.

<margin>Of Saturn. The course of the Planets.</margin>
For which cause, he says, it happened: that from the uniform conversion of the "Same" nature: those which were circling more quickly seemed to be overtaken by those which were being carried around more slowly, though they were actually overtaking them. The uniform conversion of the "Same" nature snatches the stars—which are striving with a contrary motion—into its own flight in the daily cycles. They, however, proceed naturally through their own circles: the motion and agitation of which disagrees with the motion of the one snatching them away. And so, because the planets traverse partly smaller and partly larger circles: those which traverse smaller circles, in that rotation of day and night, catch up to those which proceed more sluggishly: because they are carried through more extensive orbits: and therefore, while overtaking and passing them, they are thought to be overtaken by those whom they have passed. For example, the Moon, having accompanied the Sun at the first moment of Aries, passes the whole circle in twenty-nine days: and catching the Sun at the twenty-eighth moment of the same sign: because it passes him and goes forward, it seems as if it were fleeing him and being caught by him. This same reasoning is found also in the other stars whose course is unequal. Then he continues.

Indeed, that uniform and unerring conversion, turning all their circles into a spiral and like a winding volume of acanthus, with the center of the compass fixed by chance or even by our will, oppo <note>The text cuts off mid-word; "oppo" likely leads to "oppositum" (opposite).</note>

It provides specific orbital periods for the Moon and Saturn and discusses the soul-like nature of celestial bodies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the complex motions of the planets and the definition of time through the Sun and Moon.</meta>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

...or circles are described as if by a loosened pair of compasses: so that the end of the drawn line not only fails to reach its starting point, but by deviating from its proper stiffness, it creates tighter or looser circles above or below the previous line. This type of circle they are accustomed to call <term>helical</term> <note>original: "ελιχωδεσ" (helikōdes); referring to a spiral shape</note> or the "scroll of the acanthus" <note>The acanthus leaf was a common architectural motif in antiquity, often shown in curling, spiral forms on Corinthian columns</note>. Therefore, because the fixed sphere <note>The "aplanes" or the outermost sphere of the stars</note> carries the planets along in its daily rotation, it does not allow them to be restored to the exact same place or seat from which they set out. Instead, it either forces them to pass beyond it or, by a slower progression, prevents them from reaching their destination. He rightly said that the wandering stars rotate in a spiral and like a winding acanthus scroll because of their inconsistent and unequal revolution. 

For example, if the star of Venus were in the sign of Aries, and then the turning of the world carries it away so that it is pulled further along than its previous day's progress, there will certainly be some departure from Aries. The more revolutions that occur, the further it will depart from Aries toward the preceding signs, eventually being carried into Pisces and from there into Aquarius. Conversely, if the pulling force is more relaxed, it will recede from Aries toward Taurus, and toward Gemini and Cancer, always in uneven gyrations that deviate from their starting points and wander from their proper stiffness. The Greeks call these gyrations <term>helices</term> <note>original: "ελιάκας" (heliakas); spirals</note>, whose increases are marked by their decreases, and their decreases by their increases. They are similar to the diagram which is written below.

<column-break/>

->[Diagram: A circular zodiac showing the twelve signs (Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Leo, Virgo, Libra, Scorpius, Sagittarius, Capricorn, Aquarius, Pisces) with a spiral line winding outward from a central star, illustrating the "helical" path described in the text.]<-

<column-break/>

Then he says: And so that this rational and deliberate variety and moderation of movements might also be noted by sight, and the dance of all eight motions might be clear, God the creator of things kindled a most bright light in the region of the second sphere from the earth, which light we call the Sun. This was done both for the other ineffable benefits which the Sun brings to the whole world, and also because a birth of this kind of fire is said to have existed so that such a great design of things would be covered by no obscurity; so that while it gave color to things, the convenience of number, by which the spaces of time are noted, might also come forth, <margin>Month. Year.</margin> and so that the change of days and nights might follow. Indeed, he designated the birth of the **Month** to be when the Moon, having completed its circle, meets the Sun. Likewise, the **Year** occurs when the Sun returns to the same sign from which it had set out, in the number of three hundred sixty-five days and a fourth part. For this reason, it happened that by the wisest men, most practiced in the observation of celestial things, that fourth portion was collected into one after the fourth year and, being incorporated, completed the space of a day and a night. It is easy to understand, however, that the perfect number of time completes the perfect year. Then at last, <margin>The Perfect Year.</margin> when the courses of all eight revolutions are finished and return as if to the origin and beginning of another revolution, which the same and uniform motion will always measure. <margin>The Perfect Number</margin> He calls that the **Perfect Number of Time**, which completes the perfect year, in which both the seven planets and the other stars, which are called fixed, are restored to their original seats and produce the same arrangement that existed in the beginning of things and the start of the world's constitution, so that length corresponds to length, and the breadth of the former intervals...

It introduces the concept of the "Perfect Year," where all celestial bodies return to their original positions at the moment of creation.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, planetary motion, spiral, zodiac, Sun, Moon, measurement of time, Perfect Year, Chalcidius, Plato's Timaeus</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato’s Timaeus, specifically discussing the "Great Year" or the cosmic cycle of the stars, and the classification of living beings within the universe.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus. Folio XXXV.

...so that the breadth squares with the length, and the depth with the depth. This time, however, contains an innumerable series of years. Indeed, since the circuits of the wandering stars <note>The planets</note> are unequal, they must necessarily complete their courses at different times. Furthermore, some wander more widely from the center of the world, while others stay more narrowly toward the curves of the south or the north. Some are higher from the earth, while others are not separated from the terrestrial region by such a long interval of height. They also perform diverse movements among themselves: so that the swift meet the slower, those moving forward meet those receding backward, the low meet the high, and those on the right meet those on the left, and those further left meet those further right, all coming together into one. There is nothing at all that differs in designation from the <margin>condition.</margin> condition, appearance, and figures of the other stars. 

And so, when all are distant from all by equal diameters, they produce one nod and one single conformation of all the stars. For it is necessary that if any one of the fires <note>The stars/planets</note> is restored to the state of its ancient constellation according to the ratio of its height, it might not yet be restored to its ancient latitude. Or, if one is perfectly recalled to its ancient state through all moments, yet the perfect representation of the others—whose condition is different—will by no means have arrived. It is necessary that even for that star found in perfect representation, another change must occur until that opportunity arrives which represents one and the same face as existed from the beginning of the world. One should not think that this movement and designation brings decay or dissolution to the world; rather, it brings recreation and a kind of new greenness <note>original: "novellam viriditatem"</note> placed at the beginning of a new motion. I do not know whether in certain regions of the earth any loss will arise from this innovation. Thus far he has treated the constitution of the sensible world.

We have set apart the perfection of the whole world, completed by God the maker, in the text of the preceding work, adhering to Platonic dogmas as far as the mediocrity of our talent allowed, according to the contemplation of nature and the ingenious ratios of generation. Next, he treats before all things the first generation of the celestial beings which are fixed upon the back of the fixed and unerring revolution <note>The "Aplanes" or the sphere of fixed stars</note>, and also those things which the world embraces. Thus the whole thing may be full and perfect, and the sensible animal <note>The world itself, viewed as a living being</note> of the world may obtain the closest likeness to the perfect, intelligible, and exemplary world born from itself. 

Four species of sensible animals, both celestial and terrestrial, are enumerated by natural contemplation: of the celestial, the stars; of the terrestrial, the winged creatures, and likewise the swimming creatures, and those which are moved across the land, rightly called terrestrial. Indeed, these are nourished by the earth and rest in the bosom of the same earth; since their bodies are for the most part earthly, they are named after the prevailing matter. Just so the celestial beings, which are composed for the most part of pure and liquid fire, are called eternal fires. Not content with the demonstration of the aforementioned animals, he extends his diligence even to the explanation of the angelic nature (which he calls "daemons" <note>original: "demonas"; in the Platonic sense, these are intermediary spirits, not "demons" in the modern sense</note>). The purest of these has its seat in the ether, another in the air, and a third in that region named the moist essence. Thus the internal parts of the world are crowded with animals using reason, and no region of it is deserted. 

Because this treatise is more elevated and beyond the contemplation of nature, he necessarily defers it. He says that after the birth of the fixed stars, since each of them was a divine animal using a body mixed from the four purest materials—and for the greater part of serene fire—he fixed them as a certain shimmering animal formed in the fashion of a sphere upon the highest globe of the fixed heaven <note>original: "aplanis globo"</note> with the greatest harmony. He adorned the whole of it with lights of this kind. But where the use of moving was necessary for sight, he granted these two principal motions out of all motions: one by accident, by which the fixed heaven carries the stars toward the west (which motion he now calls "antecedent"); the other truly principal, which is a turning around, and rotates around itself, similar to the motion of a deliberative soul, with the other five motions ceasing. For the local motions are seven, I believe: two indeed according to...

<sig>e iii</sig>

<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the motions of the heavens and the central position of the Earth within the cosmos.</meta>

# From the Commentary of Chalcidius

->lengthwise, that is, forward and backward; likewise two others through width to the right and to the left; and two others according to depth, upward and downward; and the last, similar to the rotation mentioned above, which flies around a fixed axis. Since, therefore, out of those seven motions, God assigned the use of only two to the individual fixed stars—the primary rotation, and by accident that which is carried to the right—he rightly said that the five other motions besides these are vacant. Then he concludes by mentioning that he has discussed the motions of the wandering stars <note>The planets</note>. And the heaven was thus adorned with wise and eternal indwelling living beings.<-

Because it seemed a natural consequence that the birth of mortal animals, subject to passions, should also be demonstrated—these are furthermore the earthly ones—he rightly and deservedly first sets forth what he thought should be said about the Earth itself. He says, moreover, that God established this Earth, constrained by the limits of the pole that goes through all things and contains all things, as the guardian of night and day. But he calls the "pole" that axis which pervades the entire body of the world. "Constrained" is to be understood in two ways: either according to Pythagoras, <margin>The place of fire</margin> turning itself around the axis. For it pleases the Pythagoreans that fire, as the principle of all materials, occupies the middle of the world, which they call the "Guard of Jupiter." They say that through this, moreover, the stars, the Earth, and the <term>antichthona</term> <note>original: ἀντίχθονα; the "Counter-Earth," a hypothetical planet in Pythagorean cosmology</note> are moved, being carried around in a circle. Therefore, it must be understood either in this way, or, somewhat more likely, <margin>Vesta</margin> that the Earth rests, adhering to the middle of the world; and for that reason, it is named Vesta by Plato and many others. Finally, in the *Phaedrus*, he says the same: "For Vesta alone remains in the divine dwelling." Moreover, he says she is the guardian and maker of day and night for this reason: because as the Sun is carried through the same—that is, the Earth—illuminating the parts of it subjected to him, he makes the day. But when he has moved away from some part, the Earth itself is overshadowed by the Sun's turnings. Thus, from the shadows, a thick darkness follows. At the same time, because the Earth is immobile, he significantly named her the guardian of night and day. For the day does not perish for the night to be born, nor does the day arise from the loss of the night; but each succeeds the other while remaining safe. Because this happens through her, while she herself remains untiringly always in her own seat, she is, I believe, the spectator of those things which are carried out as if before her sight; and deservedly the guardian. I believe she is called the most ancient of the gods either because she is a place capable of holding living beings—for it is necessary that a region and place be conceived before those things which are in the place—or because she holds the logic of a point. Furthermore, it is known by a certain natural conception of the mind that this is more ancient than all magnitude and all quantities, just as Hesiod says:
<margin>Hesiod</margin>
> First this was Chaos, then afterwards the Earth was created,
> The firmest seat of breathing things within her vast breast.

For after Chaos, which the Greeks call <term>hyle</term> <note>original: ὕλην; matter or raw material</note> and we call "the woods" <note>Latin: *sylva*; used here as a technical term for primordial matter</note>, he teaches that the Earth stood firm in the middle of the world's circuit: fixed like a foundation, and immobile. To us, however, it is granted by nature to precede in the mind that which stands before those things which move. For every motion takes its beginning after a state of rest.

<margin>The Dance</margin> The works of the wandering stars, which are celebrated on account of their modulated and harmonious agitation—which the same people call the <term>choreia</term> <note>original: χορείαν; a choral dance</note>—are clearly seen in the progressions and turnings of those same stars: as when they appear to have progressed further by some excessive and rapid motion; or when they are thought to stand for a long time in one and the same place; and likewise when they are thought to be carried backward; then also in their meetings and their flashings-forth and hidings, either in the east or in the west; also in their turnings and equinoxes and all their transformations. Furthermore, in their eclipses and restored illuminations, and concerning the rest...

<summary>Chalcidius explains the Earth's central, immobile position in the universe as the "Guardian of Day and Night," contrasting Pythagorean theories of a moving Earth with the Platonic view of Earth as Vesta. He also introduces the concept of the "choral dance" of the planets to describe their complex celestial motions.</summary>
<keywords>cosmology, Pythagoras, Vesta, Hesiod, planetary motion, Counter-Earth, primordial matter, celestial mechanics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the movements of the planets and the significance of celestial omens, transitioning from physical astronomy to the theological implications of the stars.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus
### Page 36
#### e iiii

If anyone should dispute these matters with dedicated effort, Plato says he would waste his labor in vain on a superfluous task. For this reason, he excuses himself, believing that this particular treatise pertains more to Astronomy than to <term>Physiology</term> <note>In this context, the study of the laws of nature or physics.</note>. For he names certain "parables"—which are the companions of the stars—such as Mercury and Lucifer <note>The Morning Star, or Venus.</note>, which are always inseparable from the Sun and follow reciprocal circuits, which Astrologers call "retrogradations"; likewise, they make progress toward preceding positions, advancing and even forming "conjunctions."

<margin>Twofold Conjunction</margin>
Indeed, there is a twofold conjunction. One is by *Catoecum* <note>original: "per Catœcum," likely referring to a longitudinal alignment or "dwelling together."</note>, the other is by diameter. That which occurs by *Catoecum* signifies what kind of stars are coming together with others, and what the significance of their meeting might be. By diameter, however, he wishes us to understand the position of stars distant from one another, when a straight line connects and joins the center of one star to the center of another star placed directly opposite. 

<margin>Eclipses of the stars.</margin>
Those events which Mathematicians call "eclipses," Plato calls "obstacles," more properly and somewhat more significantly. It is not that the stars actually block one another, but that for us looking toward them, our sight is barred from the higher star by the interposition of the lower one; therefore, the higher star is thought to "fail" or "suffer" because of the object below it. Likewise, he says that those stars which appear again after a long interval signify fears and certain portents, either about to happen soon or later on. He believes the interpretation of these signs belongs to those who are able to reason about such matters. 

<margin>Stars do not cause the future.</margin>
From this, it is given to be understood that the stars do not *cause* the things that happen, but rather *foretell* the future. Following this reasoning, even the prophet Homer calls the rising of the Dog Star "Orion’s Dog." 

<margin>Homer</margin>
Some call this same star <term>Aspokyon</term> <note>original: "ἀσπόκυνον," likely a variant of "the star that follows the dog."</note>, while the Egyptians call it <term>Solecho</term> <note>original: "σολεχῶ."</note>. Its year is completed—which is called the <term>Cynic Year</term> <note>original: "κυνικὸς," the Sothic cycle of approximately 1,460 years.</note>—in one thousand four hundred and sixty years. Homer speaks of this star thus far: "That one is indeed bright, but it threatens grim fates." This occurs, I believe, when it is seen after an interval of time. Furthermore, it is seen after a four-year period has passed, but not in the same locations. After four times three hundred and sixty-five four-year periods, the Cynic Year is completed. 

<margin>Cynic Year.</margin>
The prophets of the Egyptians also fear a certain star not seen for several years, which they call *Alic*. Furthermore, a rising star announces diseases of the people and the deaths of many nobles. 

<margin>Homer the Egyptian.</margin>
Finally, Homer—who was himself an Egyptian, and is clearly said to be a Theban (which is the most noble city in Egypt)—secretly follows this very idea in the opening of the *Iliad*. He says that because of the anger of Achilles (whose father was Peleus and mother a sea goddess), there arose disease and the destruction not only of famous men, but also of other animals and cattle necessary for war. Having taken this opening, he fashioned the rest with poetic license. 

<margin>The star at the birth of Christ. The Magi.</margin>
There is also another more holy and venerable history which relates that by the rising of a certain star, not diseases and deaths were announced, but the descent of the venerable God for the sake of human preservation and mortal affairs. When the wise men of the Chaldeans—who were certainly men of wisdom and sufficiently practiced in the observation of celestial things—had looked upon this star during their nightly journey, they are said to have sought the recent birth of God. Having found that "childly majesty," they venerated him and took vows fitting for so great a God—matters which are much better known to you than to others.

<margin>The excellence of demons.</margin>
But truly, to provide an account of the nature of <term>demons</term> <note>In the Platonic sense: intermediary spirits between gods and humans.</note>, Plato says, is a greater work than human genius can bear. Not because this discussion is foreign to philosophers—for whom else would it be more suitable?—but because the inquiry into this matter belongs to primary and elevated contemplation, which is called <term>Epoptia</term> <note>original: "ἐποπτεία," the highest stage of initiation or "beholding" in mystery traditions.</note>, being somewhat higher than Physics. Therefore, it does not seem at all appropriate for us who are now disputing the nature of things. Nevertheless, he spoke briefly and strictly of those powers which are considered gods, I believe, so that the constitution of the world might not [be left incomplete].

<summary>This page discusses the astronomical movements of planets like Mercury and Venus, the nature of eclipses as visual obstructions, and the role of stars as omens rather than causes. It links classical Greek poetry (Homer) and Egyptian cycles to the Star of Bethlehem and the birth of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Astronomy, Plato, Timaeus, Homer, Sothic cycle, Cynic Year, Star of Bethlehem, Magi, demons, Epoptia, Achilles, astrology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the necessity of tradition and the classification of living beings within the five regions of the cosmos.</meta>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

<margin>
He himself said: One ought to believe the authorities of Pythagoras.
</margin>
...would be left incomplete in whatever part if he remained silent about such matters; he shows, in a manner more through faith than through persuasive or probable proof, that belief ought to precede all doctrines, especially since this is the assertion not of just anyone, but of great and nearly divine men. Finally, it was not said in vain of Pythagoras that "he himself said it" <note>original: "ipse dixit" — a famous phrase implying that the master's authority is sufficient proof</note>: and for that reason, one ought not to seek further. Therefore, he says, neither are proofs always to be applied—

<margin>
Orpheus, Linus, Musaeus, Li[ber], Philo.
</margin>
—nor is a persuasive assertion [always necessary] for those things said by the ancients who were endowed with a certain divine wisdom. At the same time, he sets forth those things which Orpheus, Linus, and Musaeus <note>Legendary figures of early Greek poetry and myth</note> prophesied concerning the divine powers, not because he took delight in them or believed them [literally], but because the authority of those prophesying was so great that it was not right to believe them too sparingly when they made such assertions. But indeed, in that book entitled *The Philosopher*, he pursues all such questions with the greatest diligence and special care: [noting that] the race of ancient men [believed] that all things which flow down from divine counsel and providence toward the management of human life—both through powers and through reasons that help and work—were themselves gods. This was because the knowledge of the true God had not yet settled in their unrefined minds.

<margin>
The Poets.
</margin>
For they were shepherds and forest-dwellers, and others of that kind without the study of the humanities, whom a favorable dwelling-place had made survivors of the public destruction caused by the inconvenience of storms and floods. Later, the poets, flattering human passions for the sake of greed, strengthened these ideas in their verses; they fashioned them limb by limb and adorned them with grand and hidden names, to such an extent that they even named the seductive vices and most shameful acts of men as "Gods" subject to passion. Thus it happened that instead of the gratitude owed by men to divine providence, a sacrilegious account of their origin and birth was spread. The opinion of this error grew through the vanity of thoughtless men.

<margin>
The five regions of the world.
</margin>
For the present, Plato has discussed these things concerning the race of demons <note>term: "demons" (daemones) — in this context, intermediary spirits between gods and humans, not necessarily evil</note>. However, it behooves us to briefly explain their logic, even if it is not everywhere true; it is as follows. The same Plato says there are five regions or places in the world capable of supporting living beings, having a certain difference of position among themselves due to the difference of the bodies <note>i.e., the physical elements</note> which inhabit those same places. For he says the highest place is that of clear fire; next to this is the ethereal, the body of which is likewise fire, but somewhat thicker than that higher celestial fire. After that is the region of air. Then of the moist substance which the Greeks call *hygroion* <note>original: ὑγροῖον — "the moist element"</note>, which moist substance is thicker than air, so that it is this air which men breathe. The lowest and last place is that of earth. Furthermore, the difference found in the places is the same as that found in their magnitudes. The celestial is the largest, since it brings all things within its embrace. The earth is the smallest, because it is surrounded by all the other bodies. And according to the logic of a fitting continuum, the others are in the middle. Therefore, since the outermost limits—that is, the highest and the lowest—are inhabited by living beings suited to their nature (namely, those using reason): the celestial by the stars, and the terrestrial by men: it follows that the other intermediate places and regions should also be thought full of rational living beings, lest any place in the world be left deserted. Indeed, it is absurd that men, inhabiting one region of the world with a fragile body and a mind full of folly and without sincerity—full of regret because of the inconstancy of their emotions, pleasing now some and then others—

<margin>
The stars are living beings.
</margin>
—should be considered rational beings, while the stars, which are of a wise nature because of the eternal constancy of their acts and subject to no regret, having a pure and least-dissoluble body (inasmuch as they inhabit the outermost regions of the fire that surrounds all things), should be without a soul.

<summary>Chalcidius discusses the authority of ancient sages like Pythagoras and the poets, then explains Plato's theory of the five cosmic regions, arguing that every part of the universe, including the stars and the air, must be inhabited by rational beings.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Plato, Orpheus, cosmology, five elements, demons, rational souls, stars, providence, hygroion</keywords>

# On Plato's Timaeus. Folio XXXVII.

<margin>The Work of Moses</margin>to think that they even lack life. To this matter the opinion of the Hebrews also harmonizes: they claim that God, the decorator of the world, mandated the duty to the Sun indeed that it should rule the day; to the Moon, however, that it should protect the night. He also arranged the other stars as boundaries of time and signs of the years: and also as indicators of future events. All these things certainly could not be conducted so moderately, so prudently, and so continually without a rational—or rather, without a most wise—governor. Therefore, since the celestial and starry race of living beings is divine and immortal, while the earthly race is temporary, fading, and subject to suffering: it is necessary that there be some middle ground <note>Latin: medietas</note> between these two, connecting the outermost limits: just as we see in harmony, and in the world itself. For just as there are middle points in the elements themselves which, being interposed, keep the body of the whole world together, and there are between fire and earth two middle points of air and water—which, being in the middle, touch and connect the outermost limits—so since there is an immortal and impassive <note>incapable of suffering or change</note> living being, which is also rational and called celestial, and there exists another mortal race of ours subject to passions: it is necessary that there be some middle race which partakes of both the celestial and earthly nature: and that it be both immortal and subject to passion.

<margin>The nature of demons.</margin>Such, furthermore, is the nature of demons <note>In this context, "demons" refers to the classical Greek 'daimon'—intermediary spirits—rather than purely evil entities.</note> I believe, having a partnership with divinity on account of their immortality: yet also having a kinship with the fading things of earth: because it is capable of feeling: nor is it by nature immune to passions: whose affection also looks out for us. Furthermore, of this race is that ethereal being which we mentioned in the second place: whom the Hebrews call holy angels, and they say they stand before the contemplation of God with venerable, supreme, and sharp intelligence, and also with a wonderful tenacity of memory, performing service to divine matters with supreme wisdom, and prudently helping human affairs; these same observers and executors are called demons, I believe, as if they were called *daēmones* <note>original Greek: δαήμονες</note>. Furthermore, the Greeks call *daēmones* those who are "knowers of all things."

<margin>Angels</margin>It must be thought that those who are prefects over the sensible world indeed imitate a certain rank. For as God is to an angel: so an angel is to a man. Hence they are of use to us, interpreting and announcing our prayers to God, and likewise informing men of the will of God: they bring our needs to Him, and further bring divine help to us. For which reason they are called angels <note>From the Greek 'angelos', meaning messenger.</note> because of their constant duty of announcing. All Greece, all Latium <note>Italy/Rome</note>, and all the Barbarian lands are witnesses to this benefit: as are the thanksgivings of peoples preserved in books for perpetual memory. For the nature of the human race, being too weak, requires the support of a better and more excellent nature.

<margin>Angels</margin>For which reason the Creator and Preserver of all, God, wishing the race of men to exist, placed over them those by whom they might be rightly ruled, whether angels or demons. Nor should the name terrify us, being applied to both the good and the wicked: since it does not even terrify the angels: for angels are partly ministers of God—and those who are so are called holy—and partly attendants of the opposing power: as you know best. Therefore, according to the custom of speaking used among the Greeks, there are holy demons as well as polluted and infected ones: about which there will soon be a more fit place for disputing. Now let our discourse be of that race which Plato says is of a certain admirable prudence, memory, and happy docility: which knows all things: and looks into the thoughts of men: and is exceedingly delighted by the good, but hates the wicked with a resulting sadness: which arises from the displeasure of hatred: for God alone, as being of full and perfect divinity, is touched by neither sadness nor pleasure. Therefore, all regions of heaven having been allotted demons as inhabitants, mutual passages are conducted by the powers inhabiting the middle seat of the world, offering service to heaven and also caring for earthly things: which powers are the ethereal and aerial demons, removed from our sight no...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary on the nature of the universe and the hierarchy of beings, specifically focusing on the nature of "demons" (mediating spirits) as described by Plato and other ancient authorities.</meta>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

...by our sight and other senses: because their bodies do not possess so much fire as to be transparent, nor so much earth that their solidity resists touch. Their entire framework is joined from the serenity of the ether and the liquid of the air, forming an indissoluble surface. <margin>Hesiod</margin> For this reason, some think this region of ours is rightly called <gloss>Hades</gloss> <insert>an addition</insert> <note>The translator suggests the word "Hades" is linked to the Greek word for "unseen" or "dark."</note> because it is obscure. Furthermore, **Hesiod** is pleased to suggest that there are many demons. <margin>The protection of demons</margin> He says likewise that there are ten thousand of them, and that they are as much in the service of God as they are in the <margin>number.</margin> protection of mortals; he does not establish a fixed sum for their number, but according to the power of a full number, he multiplies ten thousand by the number three. <note>A symbolic way of expressing an infinite or vast multitude.</note>

<margin>Definition of a demon.</margin> Therefore, the definition of a demon shall be as follows: **A demon is a rational animal, immortal, capable of feeling, ethereal, and bestowing care upon human beings.** It is an "animal" because it is a soul living in a body. It is "rational" because it is <margin>using</margin> wise. It is "immortal" because it does not change from one body to another, but always uses the same one. It is "capable of feeling" <note>original: *patibile*</note> because it takes counsel; for care cannot be had without the experience of emotion. It is "ethereal" likewise from its location, or named from the quality of its body. <margin>An aerial demon.</margin> Finally, it "bestows care upon humans" because of the will of God, who gave them as guardians. This same definition will also apply to the aerial demon, except that this one manifests in the air; and the closer it is to the earth, the more it is adapted to the experience of emotion.

<margin>The shapes and duties of demons.</margin> The remaining demons are neither so virtuous nor so convenient; nor are they always invisible, but are sometimes visible when they transform into various figures. They take on the shadowy forms of bloodless phantoms, dragging the filth of a heavy body; often they are also the avengers of crimes and impiety, according to the sanction of divine justice; they even frequently cause harm of their own accord. <margin>Matter, which is the malignant soul.</margin> For they are touched by earthly lust from their proximity to the earth, and they have too much communion with *silva* <note>original: *sylua* (forest/matter). In Platonic thought, "silva" refers to raw, chaotic matter.</note>, which the ancients called the "malignant soul." Some properly call these and similar demons "deserting angels," about whose name no question need be raised.

Yet many, following the teaching of **Plato**, think that demons are souls freed from the duty of the body: that the ethereal demons are the souls of praiseworthy men, while the harmful ones are those of the wicked; and that these same souls resume an earthly body only in the thousandth year. <margin>Empedocles.</margin> **Empedocles** likewise thinks these souls become long-lived demons in no other way. <margin>Pythagoras.</margin> **Pythagoras** also says in his *Golden Verses*:

> When you leave the body behind and go free into the open ether,
> You shall escape being human and become a God of the nourishing ether.

Plato seems to agree with these very little, since in the *Republic* <note>original: *Politia*</note> he describes the soul of a tyrant being tortured after death by avengers. From this, it appears that the soul is one thing and the demon another. Indeed, that which tortures and that which is tortured must necessarily be different things. Moreover, the Creator God established demons before He created our souls; and He willed that these souls should need the help of demons, and that demons should provide protection to them. He thinks, however, that certain souls who have led a life of exceptional virtue through three incarnations are consecrated to the aerial or even ethereal regions, immune from the necessity of further embodiment.

-> Thus far concerning the nature of demons; next he discusses the race of mortals. And first <-

<margin>The race of man.</margin> concerning human beings, and the male sex of them specifically, he teaches that the rational part is endowed with a double virtue. One part contemplates the same and always immutable nature; <margin>by intention</margin> from which intention of the mind wisdom grows strong. The other part is the observer of changeable and generated things, to which the name "prudence" is appropriate. He says that both of these powers of the soul were given by the supreme and intelligible God to the soul of the entire body of the sensible world; the other portions of the soul, lacking reason and subject to decay—that is, the appetite of the senses, the localized motion by which each body is nourished, and the whole body...

It also contrasts the views of Pythagoras and Empedocles with Plato regarding whether human souls can become demons.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the speech of the Demiurge (the Creator) to the lesser gods regarding the creation of mortal beings and the human soul.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus. Folio XXXVIII.

...the body was assigned to mortals in a just and orderly arrangement by the Architect God, through the powers generated by Him. This was done lest, if these [mortal beings] were also created directly by the Artificer and Intelligible God, all things would share a single fortune. If all things were immortal and no mortal seed existed, the perfection of the universe would be incomplete <note>literally "would limp"</note>; for in the Exemplar—that is, the Intelligible World—the seeds of lower nature exist in an intelligible manner.

<margin>† Gods: The charm of the discourse</margin>
Let us now look at the text of the discourse. † *Gods of gods, of whom I am the artificer and father.* This is a dramatic sketch of the dialogue pertaining to its arrangement, but the speech is of a more august character. Indeed, a mythical interpretation of mysteries is fitting for this kind of oration. Therefore, the author does nothing inappropriate by introducing the Artificer God delivering a speech and decreeing to those He generated what He wished to be observed, for many reasons. First, for the sake of the readers' recreation. Indeed, a difficult matter seasoned with charming speech is more easily admitted into the depths of the mind. Second, the variety of unexpected speech mixed with religion prevents the mind, rapt by the present image of the speaker, from feeling the labor of the study. Then, the change in mastery at the beginning of a new part of the work excites new strength in the listener. For every listener fails against the boredom of uniform eloquence; furthermore, one becomes more attentive when the hope of a new arrangement is shown. In this place, he also zealously asserts his own dogmas, so that they seem not so much invented by himself as foretold by God. Moreover, they are "foretold" not in that speech which consists of the sound of the voice to declare inner movements because of the wrappings of the human mind. For God can be hindered by no obstacle from the understanding and knowledge of all things by divine law, which Plato calls an "inevitable proclamation." What, therefore, does God say? *Gods of gods, of whom I am the artificer and father.* Excellently put. For he makes a King decreeing to his nobles, so that the law given to them might also pass down to the other powers and souls, reminding them that He is their Artificer and Parent. He is the Artificer <term>Opifex: the craftsman or builder</term> insofar as they were made by Him; but He is the Father 
<margin>God is Artificer and Father</margin>
insofar as He provides for them so that they may be eternal and blessed. For you are "my works," because He is the Father and Artificer not of their substance, but of their generation. For those "nobles"—that is, the stars—are not intelligible but sensible <note>perceivable by the senses rather than just the mind</note>. But their Fabricator is supremely Intelligible. Next, He teaches them of what nature they are, and that they subsist from a composition. Furthermore, things that subsist from a composition are dissolvable by the same principles by which they subsist; yet they themselves are indissoluble. Rightly so. For they are not from any temporary birth, but from the will of the Supreme God, which surpasses all antiquity of time. He who confesses them to be indissoluble must necessarily grant they are without any beginning. That which has no beginning certainly has no end; and that which has no end is without a beginning. Moreover, he calls the power of reason—that is, Reason—by the title "divine and immortal." But the "mortal and associated" parts—or rather, as he says, the "woven-in" parts—are, I believe, found in the vices: namely, anger and pleasure.

<margin>The generation of souls.</margin>
Next, he follows with the generation of human souls, so that their nature may be clearly understood; and he persists in the myth he has interposed so that what is said may be manifest. For he again proposes the mixing bowl <term>Cratera: the vessel used for mixing wine and water, here used metaphorically for the soul's ingredients</term>, and the mixture and blending of those powers from which the Soul of the World had been composed. From the remnants of those, He fashions ours: namely, from that double nature of the "Same" and the "Different," and of "Divided Substance." These were not of the same sincere purity as before. For a soul forged from the most sincere elements could not fall into such vices of "matter" <note>original: "sylvae," referring to the Greek 'hyle' or primordial matter</note>, nor would it agree with the fragility of a mortal body. He mixed them, he says, in almost the same way; yet the same purity and serenity of results did not arise. Rightly so: since in these souls which give life to types of animals subject to death, there is neither pure reason nor sincere intellect.

<summary>This page provides a commentary on the Demiurge's speech in Plato's Timaeus, explaining why God uses lesser deities to create mortals and describing the "mixing" of human souls from the remnants of the World Soul.</summary>
<keywords>Demiurge, Timaeus, creation of souls, immortality, Reason, mixing bowl, celestial bodies, Plato, cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the Latin translation and commentary by Chalcidius on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the mathematical and harmonic division of the World Soul and the introduction of the concept of Fate.</meta>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

...but a certain amount of both anger and desire is found. Therefore, he says, the soul itself, having been mixed, is also cut lengthwise; and one part of it is left whole according to the nature of what is called the "same" rotation <note>original: *circumactionem eandem*. This refers to the uniform motion of the fixed stars</note>. From this, all divine things are understood; those who understand are wisely perfected. But he cuts the part of the "diverse" nature six times, according to the irrational manner of the planets, using harmonic, arithmetic, and geometric means, as was demonstrated above. 

<margin>Opinion</margin>
This is the power of the soul which is called **opinion**, by which guidance we come to know things that are born and pass away. This constitution of the soul was made, I believe, so that the same soul might possess knowledge of both intelligible things <note>abstract realities known by the mind</note> and sensible substance <note>physical things known by the senses</note>, inasmuch as it holds the principles of both natures within itself. This is the rational institution of the soul made by the venerable God and woven into the face of man, perceiving the occasions of vices through the provident moderation of eternal law.

Next, he says that He chose souls equal in number to the stars, and assigned each to each, and having placed them upon their suitable vehicles <note>the "chariots" of the souls</note>, He commanded them to behold the nature of the universe. Before He performed the sowing of the souls, He placed individual souls upon individual stars, so that by these same vehicles they might contemplate the entire nature of the world throughout the circuit of the stars. He taught this: that without the aid of divinity, the soul by itself is worth nothing in beholding and understanding the divine; and He taught the laws of the immutable decree. 

Here he now stirs up a great and difficult reasoning, about which much dispute has been held among the ancients and persists even now. Therefore, a superficial treatment is not appropriate according to Platonic doctrine. It would be a long task to follow the opinions of others. Most of them think nothing happens by fate; others think everything does, and nothing by judgment or will. Some think there are some things which happen by fate, and nonetheless others which happen by will. 

According to Plato, therefore, providence precedes; fate follows. For he says that God, after the constitution of the world, divided the souls equal in number to the stars, assigned each to each, showed them the nature of the entire world, and revealed the whole series of fates. For those things which are first indicate providence; the second indicate the laws of fate. Therefore, according to Plato, providence is known beforehand. And so we say that fate indeed comes from providence, but not providence from fate. 

<margin>Fate</margin>
Fate, therefore, according to Plato, is understood and spoken of in two ways. One, when we behold its substance with the mind. The other, when we recognize both what it is and what kind of power it has from the things it does. This same fate is called an "inevitable decree" in the *Phaedrus*; in the *Timaeus*, it is the "laws" which God spoke to the celestial souls concerning the nature of the universe; furthermore, in the *Republic* <note>original: *Politia*</note>, he calls it the "speech of Lachesis," not in a tragic sense but in the manner of theologians. 

<margin>Lachesis</margin>
We can therefore interpret the "inevitable decree" as a law that is not at all changeable, arising from an inevitable cause; the "laws" which God spoke to the souls about the universal nature as the law which follows the nature of the world and by which all worldly things are governed; and the "speech of Lachesis"—that is, of necessity—as the divine law by which future things are connected to both past and present things. 

But fate, placed in substance, is the tripartite soul of the world: in the sphere of the fixed stars <note>original: *aplane*</note>, in that which is thought to be the wandering stars <note>the planets</note>, and in the third, the sublunary sphere. Of these, the one raised to the highest things is called **Atropos**; the middle one **Clotho**; the lowest **Lachesis**. Atropos, because the fixed sphere is subject to no deflection. Clotho, because of the variously complex and twisting revolution by which those things of a diverse nature are brought about by a wandering motion. Lachesis, as if having been allotted the duty to receive all the works and effects of the aforementioned. And so some think...

<summary>Chalcidius explains the division of the World Soul into the "Same" and "Diverse" and introduces the Platonic hierarchy where Providence precedes Fate. He defines Fate both as a divine law and as a substance embodied by the three Fates (Atropos, Clotho, Lachesis) governing different celestial spheres.</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, Providence, Fate, Timaeus, Chalcidius, Atropos, Clotho, Lachesis, celestial spheres, free will, divine law</keywords>
