<meta>This page continues a philosophical commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the relationship between Providence and Fate.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus

### Folio XXXIX.

...the difference between providence and fate, though they are one and the same thing. For they hold that the providence of God is his will. Furthermore, his will is a series of causes; and because it is his will, it is called providence; further, because it is that same series of causes, it is named fate. From this it follows: that those things which are according to fate are also from providence. And in the same way, those things which are 
<margin>Chrysippus <note>A Stoic philosopher who argued that fate and providence were identical.</note></margin>
according to providence are from fate, as Chrysippus thinks. But others hold that while things which proceed from the authority of providence also come about by fate, yet not all things that happen by fate are from 
<margin>Cleanthes <note>Successor to Zeno in the Stoic school; he held a slightly different view on the limits of providence.</note></margin>
providence: such is the view of Cleanthes. But Plato’s view is: that not all things are from providence, nor is the nature of the things being dispensed of a single uniform kind. Thus, some things are from providence alone, some 
<margin>Providence</margin>
from decree, some from our own will, some even from the variety of fortune, and many by chance, which happen as they will. Indeed, divine and intelligible things, and those closest to them, are according to providence alone; but natural and corporeal things are according to fate. 
<margin>Fate</margin>
Furthermore, those things which are within our own choice and right occur by our own spontaneity. Moreover, those things placed outside of us which happen without reason and unexpectedly: if they arise from a manifest disposition already begun, they are called "fortuitous"; if they occur without our own institution, they are said to happen by "chance." All of these matters he arranges more clearly in the *Timaeus*, saying: "When these things were thus ordained, the Creator remained in his own purpose." What had he ordained? Namely, that he had joined the soul of the entire universe and its whole body with a fitting harmony. He says that the Intelligences <note>Refers to the lesser gods or celestial beings.</note>, following the command of the Father and the prescribed instruction, took the immortal beginning of a mortal living being and borrowed from the materials of the world—fire, earth, water, and air—an "elementary loan" which was to be paid back when necessary. They glued together what they had received, though not with those indissoluble bonds by which they themselves were held together. 
<margin>† The Gods</margin>
For the command of God, which the secondary gods † obey, is, I believe, the reason containing the perpetual 
<margin>† The Gods</margin>
order: which is called fate. And this draws its origin from providence. What of when he says: "And soon, the machinery of the whole universe having been increased, he chose souls equal in number to the stars, and matched each soul to a star, and having placed them upon these souls as suitable vehicles, he commanded them to behold the nature of the whole universe, and taught them the laws of the immutable decree." 
<margin>Fate</margin>
Indeed, to complete the machinery of the world, to choose souls equal in number to the stars, to place them upon fit vehicles, to show them the nature of the whole universe, and to teach the laws of the immutable decree: all these are the duties of providence. But the laws themselves which were spoken are fate, and this is the divine law instilled into the soul of the world, the healthful government of all things. Thus, fate is indeed from providence, yet providence is not from fate.
<margin>Fate</margin>
Now we shall speak of fate as it is placed in office and action. For concerning this, many moral, natural, and logical disputes are held. For although all things that happen are infinite, and proceed from the infinite through immense time and infinite space, yet fate itself, embracing all things from every side, is finite and determined. For neither law, nor reason, nor anything endowed with divinity is indeterminate. And this very thing is made manifest from the state and conformation of the heaven at that time which they call the "Perfect Year." 
<margin>The Perfect Year</margin>
Finally, Plato says: "It is easy for the intellect to understand that the perfect number of time completes the perfect year <note>The 'Great Year' or 'Platonic Year,' the period in which all planets return to the same relative positions.</note> only then, when the courses of all eight revolutions are finished and return, as if to their origin and the head of another revolution, which the same year and uniform motion will always measure."
Because in this finite time—since its determination is observed by the space of a certain circuit—all things which happen either in heaven or on earth must necessarily return anew to their past conditions. For instance, the current state of the constellations will be restored after a long series of time. And in the same way, that which follows will happen next, and so on forever. From which it appears that fate, placed in action, [deals with] an infinite variety of accidents...

<summary>This page discusses the Platonic and Stoic distinctions between Providence (the divine will) and Fate (the chain of causes), explaining how the "Perfect Year" governs the cyclical return of all events in the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Providence, Fate, Plato, Timaeus, Chrysippus, Cleanthes, Great Year, cosmology, determinism, free will</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the philosophical definitions of Fate, Providence, and human free will.</meta>

-># Chalcidius's Interpretation<-

...things, and those which happen from infinite time unto infinite time, yet that it is itself determined, and possesses an immutable and always identical property. For just as circular motion and likewise the time that measures it are both circles, so it is necessary that all those things which are carried around in revolutions are circles. Therefore, he <note>likely referring to Plato</note> calls that which is known and inevitable "Fate," understanding an inevitable force and power as the principal cause of all things which occur in the world in a consequential and continuous manner. Furthermore, this belongs to the tripartite soul of the world, which we previously said was Fate established in substance.
<margin>This known thing</margin>
Furthermore, this "known thing" is the law of God, which we have asserted is inexcusable <note>meaning unavoidable or without exception</note> because of its inevitable cause. Moreover, for this [soul], there is law, speech, and a sanction: by which God sanctioned the soul of the world for the perpetual governance of all things. For he had cared not only that the world should exist, but that it should be eternal and indissoluble. This sanction contains all things within itself: some by a certain precedence, others according to a concession. This is just as in the principles of geometry, where some things exist by precedence, while theorems exist according to concession. For once the principles are conceded—such as points, lines, and the origins and elements of other such things—the theorems are opened up according to that concession, as if they possess the consequence of the conceded precedence. So also the sanction, being an ordering and a law embracing all things, holds preceding causes based on our merits as if they were certain principles. Furthermore, those things which come forth bound by necessities happen consequently according to that precedence and necessity. Therefore,
<margin>Providence.</margin>
the beginning of the divine law—that is, of Fate—is Providence. But Fate is that which embraces, as if by an edict, both the obedience of complying with it and the contumacy of not complying.
<margin>Fate.</margin>
Furthermore, punishments or rewards arise according to the precedence of the merit †bestowed.
<margin>†bestowed</margin>
Moreover, the precedence of merit bestowed on either side is the movement of our souls: their judgment, consensus, appetite, or turning away. These are placed within us, since the choice of these, as well as those things contrary to them, is in our power. Therefore, in this ordering of things and law of nature, all the most ancient things are said to be from precedence: and they are within our power. But those things which follow after them are bound by precedence and necessity. And just as the law is one thing and that which follows the law is another (that is, the "lawful"), so Fate is one thing, and those things which follow Fate out of inevitable necessity are another: namely, "fated" things. Therefore, the soul of the entire universe is Fate established in substance. Likewise, an instruction for managing every matter rightly has been given to it. The Law, which contains Fate when placed in duty and action, has such a texture and consequence: "If this shall be, that will follow." Therefore, that which precedes from these is within us; that which follows is according to Fate. This is called "fated" by another name, differing greatly from Fate itself. Thus there are three things: that which is placed within us; Fate itself; and that which looms according to the law of Fate based on merits. Next, he sets down the words of the law itself, by which the soul makes itself a companion to God: "The soul of those which has seen something of the things that truly are, shall be unharmed until the time of another circuit." And if it always does this, it will remain always unharmed. Therefore, this whole thing is the law and edict which is properly called Fate. But that Socrates followed the edict of the law and offered himself as a companion to God was Socrates's own work. Furthermore, because Socrates lived in such a way, his soul perseveres unharmed until the time of another circuit; this occurs according to the fated decree. And if he always does this which is within Socrates's power, he will always be unharmed according to Fate.
<margin>Predicted to Laius.</margin>
By this reasoning, it was predicted to Laius by Apollo:

> Beware of sowing forbidden furrows of children. He who is fathered by you shall impiously slay you, and the entire hall shall be splattered with blood. <note>A reference to the Oedipus myth; Laius was warned not to have children.</note>

Indeed, by these oracles he showed that it was in Laius's power not to sow; which is the precedence. Furthermore, that which followed was no longer in the power of Laius, but rather in fated necessity according to the merit of the precedence. Because if it were necessary for Laius...

<summary>Chalcidius distinguishes between Fate (the divine law), Providence (the origin of law), and "fated" events (the necessary consequences of human choices). He uses the examples of Socrates's virtue and the tragedy of Laius to illustrate how free will interacts with divine necessity.</summary>
<keywords>Fate, Providence, free will, Socrates, Laius, Apollo, divine law, necessity, Chalcidius, Timaeus commentary</keywords>

# On Plato’s Timaeus

<margin>Laius</margin>
<margin>Thetis</margin>
<margin>Moses</margin>
<margin>Adam & Eve</margin>
If a brave man were to encounter [a disaster], or if that slaughter had long been imminent by an inescapable necessity, then inquiry would be useless, and prediction would be equally useless. But he <note>referring to Apollo or the oracle</note>, as one who foreknew what would follow, forbade the sowing [of a child] according to fate: knowing that it was placed in his power if he wished to abstain. Laius, however, as a man ignorant of future things, inquired of him who knew what he ought to do. He perished, moreover, not because fate drew him out, but because he was conquered by his own lack of self-control. In the same way, Thetis had foretold to her son <note>Achilles</note> that if he fought at Troy, a fate would prepare death for him—a premature end, but with great fame. If he returned to his fatherland, a long span of life was portended, but ending without glory. He fought, nevertheless, with no violence of fate compelling him; for he received nothing in his choice by force, but rather by the violence of his own passion, and with a favor inclined toward glory. With these things that saying of Plato also harmonizes: *The cause lies with the chooser; God is without blame.* <note>original: "Causa penes optantem, Deus extra culpam." A famous quote from the Republic, Book X.</note> Likewise, that virtue is free and not subject to any necessity. Or when he says to the souls that Virtue, the daughter of Lachesis <note>one of the three Fates</note>, will come to none of them by a lot under the power of spirits <note>daemons</note>; but they themselves will freely and spontaneously choose for themselves the spirit which each thinks should be selected. According to Moses, God forbade the first-born of life <note>Adam and Eve</note> to eat from the trees from which the knowledge of good and evil might creep into their minds. Because the power of both abstaining and not abstaining was in their hands, God, who wished to consult for them, showed what was to be avoided; He would not have prohibited it in vain if it were necessary for it to happen.

<margin>The Possible</margin>
<margin>The Necessary</margin>
<margin>The Doubtful</margin>
<margin>Reason and Counsel</margin>
<margin>Imagination</margin>
<margin>Divination</margin>
Now we shall deal with those things which are in man's power. All things that exist were divided by the ancients into three parts: the possible, the necessary, and the ambiguous <note>or "doubtful"</note>. The possible is like a genus; the necessary and the doubtful are like species. Therefore, everything possible is either doubtful or necessary. Furthermore, that is called "necessary" which is constrained by necessity. And because most possible things cannot be prevented from occurring, certain things are prohibited or turned aside by counsels, and are sketched out by definitions of this kind. The "necessary" is a possibility whose contrary is impossible: such as that all things that have risen must set, and things that have grown must grow old. For it is necessary for everything that is born to die, and that which has advanced to old age to grow old. Nor is there room for contradiction—namely, that what is born should not perish. The definition of "doubtful" things, however, is as follows: The "doubtful" is a possibility whose contrary is also possible: such as that it will rain today after sunset. For this is possible; and its contrary is equally possible: that it may not rain at all after sunset. Now, there are many differences in doubtful outcomes. For some are frequent; some are equally frequent, such as growing a beard, or knowing letters, or pleading cases. Furthermore, those which are of rare example are opposed to the frequent ones; and to those which happen equally, those which do not happen equally are opposed. Therefore, the choice of those things which are equally doubtful will be in the power of man, who, as a rational animal, refers all things to reason and counsel. Furthermore, reason and counsel are the innermost movement of that which is the "ruling part" <note>the *hegemonikon*</note> in the soul. This, indeed, moves from itself; and its movement is either assent or appetite. Therefore, assent and appetite move from themselves; yet not without imagination, which the Greeks call <term>phantasia</term> <note>original: φαντασίαν; the mental image or representation</note>. From this it happens that very often, when the image deceives, that principal movement of the soul's power or consensus is corrupted, and it chooses the vicious instead of the best. The cause of this is manifold: either a rustic lack of elegance in deliberating, or ignorance, or a mind too devoted to unseasonable favor, or the anticipation of a false opinion, or a depraved habit, or certainly the tyrannical domination of some vice; for that reason, we are said to sin by force or by violent charms rather than by will. Since these things are so, divination is, I believe, preserved, so that authority is not taken away from presage. For one who foreknows can, with such information of fate having been made, give counsel on whether to approach or not to approach. And rightly...

<summary>This page discusses the compatibility of divine foreknowledge and human free will, using examples from Greek mythology (Laius, Achilles) and the Bible (Adam and Eve). It categorizes events into the necessary, the possible, and the doubtful to explain how human choice operates within a rational framework.</summary>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the philosophical distinctions between fate, free will, and the nature of chance.</meta>

-># Chalcidius’s Interpretation<-

And reasonably, the mathematician <note>Here referring to an astrologer or one who calculates celestial movements</note> will take the starting point of an action to be performed from the favorable positions of the stars and signs: so that if this is done, that result may follow. Furthermore, these and similar remedies are for uncertain outcomes, in which the healthiness of counsel acts as a medicine.
<margin>Law is a command</margin> Discipline also has its place, and especially the making of laws. For what is law but a command decreeing what is honorable and prohibiting the opposite? Therefore, because the choice of these things is within our power, the honor of praise and the mark of censure, the grace of reward and the severity of punishments, as well as other encouragements to virtue and restraints against malice, have been provided for <margin>† by right</margin> life.

Now, since we have followed through on what providence is, what fate is when considered as a substance, and likewise what is found in its function and act; what is within the power of man, and what occurs according to the decree of fate; we shall now discuss fortune and those things which happen by chance.
<margin>Fortune / Chance</margin> He says that the whole power of Fortune lies in the affairs of men, while Chance <note>original: "Casus"</note> has another kind of jurisdiction. For those things which happen either to inanimate objects or to animals living without reason, not arising from nature or art—all these are said to happen by chance. But those things which happen to men, whether favoring their business or opposing it, are considered fortuitous and resulting from the judgment of Fortune.
<margin>Causes</margin> Of causes, one is principal, the other accidental. The principal cause is, for example, making a journey for the sake of business, or inspecting the countryside, or something of this kind.
<margin>Accidental</margin> The accidental cause is when, for those who have begun the journey, the sun and heat burn them, which is followed by a staining and coloring of the face. Certainly, the journey was not undertaken for the sake of that staining. Generally, therefore, we shall say that both fortune and chance are accidental causes to a principal cause: so that the principal cause is indeed in fate, but in fortune and chance, the cause is accidental. And because things that happen partly have authority from necessity, partly are of common and frequent example, and partly happen rarely: both fortune and chance are found in those things which happen rarely.
<margin>Fortune / Chance</margin> And indeed, the unexpected outcome of fortune, which is irrational and accompanied by wonder, takes its origin from human purpose. But chance <note>original: "Casus"</note> occurs apart from human purpose. Indeed, whatever happens by chance is found either in inanimate things or in mute animals. Briefly, then, when two causes having their beginning from purpose do not concur in such a way that what was intended happens, but something far different and contrary to expectation occurs, it is the play of fortune. For example, if someone should secretly commit a treasure to the earth, and thereafter a farmer, having the purpose of planting a vine or any other kind of stock, should find that treasure while digging a trench: certainly, neither did the one hide it so that another might find it (but rather so that he might recover it himself when there was need), nor did the farmer labor so that he might find treasure (but rather to make a trench); and yet each use is an unexpected fortune.
<margin>Definition of Fortune.</margin> Wherefore, fortune can also be rightly defined thus: Fortune is the concurrence of two causes falling together, drawing their origin from purpose, from which concurrence something happens beyond hope and with wonder. As if a creditor, on account of a debt long sought in vain, should go to the forum for the sake of preparing advocates, and the debtor should go to the same place for the sake of trading; then, the debtor having been met, he pays the long-drawn debt in the presence of the advocates. Indeed, the cause for going was different for both: and that was achieved which was not intended, rather than what was already being acted upon.
<margin>Description of Chance.</margin> There will also be Chance, the concurrence and simultaneous happening of accidental causes without reason in inanimate things or mute animals: as when wild beasts, having escaped from their pens, return to the same pens of their own accord; and when we say a stone has fallen of its own accord. And so, concerning fate, and those things which are in the power of man, and also concerning Fortune and Chance, enough has been said.

But because there are some things which are said to the contrary against these, they must be set forth: and...

<summary>Chalcidius defines the distinctions between Fate, Fortune, and Chance, arguing that while Fate governs the principal order, Fortune and Chance are accidental concurrences of purposeful or natural actions.</summary>
<keywords>Fortune, Chance, Fate, Free Will, Law, Causality, Chalcidius, Philosophy of Action</keywords>

# On Plato’s Timaeus
## Folio XLI.

<note>original: "In Timeū Platonis"</note>

...then at last will the opinion of Plato be established upon firm foundations. For they say: 
If God knows all things from the beginning before they happen—not only the celestial things which are held by a certain fate <note>original: "fato"</note> because of the blessed necessity of their own happiness, but if He also knows those thoughts and wills of ours—He knows also that doubtful nature; He holds the past, the present, and the future, and this from the beginning. Since God cannot be deceived, all things were certainly arranged and decreed from the beginning, as much those things said to be placed in our power as those that are fortuitous or subject to chance. Furthermore, since all these things have been decreed for a long time, they conclude that everything which happens happens by fate. Even laws, exhortations, rebukes, and disciplines, and all things of this sort, are held by fatal conditions. 

Whenever it is decreed that something should happen to someone, it is also decreed by whose help or benefit it ought to happen. For example, if safety is to come to someone while sailing, it should come to him by no other means than by that specific pilot steering the ship. Or if it is to happen that a city makes use of good institutions and customs, as in Sparta, this must come about through the laws of Lycurgus. Likewise, if someone is to be just, like Aristides <note>An Athenian statesman known as "the Just."</note>, the education of his parents serves as an aid in the attainment of justice and equity. They say it is manifest that the arts also fall under the decree of fate. For from this it was ordained long ago which sick person should recover, and by which healer. Finally, it frequently happens that a sick person is cured not by a doctor, but by an unskilled person, when such is the condition of the decree. A similar reasoning applies to praise, blame, punishments, and rewards. For it often happens that, with fate opposing, things which were done rightly bring not only no praise, but on the contrary, bring reproach and punishment. 

But truly, they say that divination clearly demonstrates that outcomes have been decreed long ago. For unless a decree preceded it, those who foresee could not have access to the reason for it. Indeed, they claim the movements of our souls are nothing other than the ministries of fatal decrees. Since it is necessary that things be done through us while fate is the actor. Thus, humans hold the place of those things which are called "indispensable" <note>original: "sine quibus," referring to necessary conditions or instruments.</note>, just as there can be no motion or rest without a place. 

<margin>It is fought against.</margin>
Against these arguments, which are instituted so pugnaciously and more violently than fate itself, what shall we respond? That God indeed knows all things, but He knows each thing according to its own nature: that which is subjected to necessity, He knows as liable to necessity; but that which is wavering, He knows as being endowed with that nature to which deliberation opens a path. For God does not know a nature of ambiguity as if it were something certain and constrained by necessity—for then He would be deceived and would not know—but He knows it as truly doubtful according to its nature. 

What then do we say? That God knows all things, and His knowledge is solidified from eternity. Furthermore, those things which follow 
<margin>They follow</margin>
are partly divine and immortal, and partly perishable and temporal. The substance of immortal things is fixed and stable. The substance of mortal things is changeable and doubtful, being one way at one time and another way at another, due to the inconstancy of nature. Therefore, God's knowledge concerning divine things—whose happiness is certain and fortified by perpetual necessity—is a certain and necessary knowledge, as much because of the certain comprehension of the knowledge itself as because of the substance of the things known. 

But truly, regarding uncertain things, there is indeed a necessary knowledge that they are uncertain and placed in an ambiguous outcome. For they cannot be otherwise than their nature is; yet they themselves are possible in either direction, rather than being subjugated to necessities. Therefore, even doubtful things were not rigidly arranged and decreed from the beginning, unless perhaps this very thing: that they ought to be uncertain and hang upon a wavering outcome. Wherefore, since the nature of the human soul is such that it sometimes applies itself to virtue and sometimes inclines toward malice—just as the body is sometimes close to health and sometimes to sickness—this is clearly fixed and decreed from the origin. Who, furthermore, is evil...

<sig>f</sig>

<summary>This page examines the conflict between divine foreknowledge and free will, arguing against the Stoic view that God's knowledge necessitates all human actions. It posits that God knows "uncertain" things precisely as uncertain, thereby preserving the possibility of human choice within a providential framework.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Timaeus, Fate, God, Lycurgus, Aristides, divine knowledge, necessity, providence, free will, determinism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the philosophical debate regarding free will, virtue, and the origins of human error.</meta>

# Chalcidius's Interpretation

<note>original: "Chalcidij Interpretatio"</note>

...whether he will be good or bad is neither a fixed decree nor a command. For this reason, there are laws, teachers, deliberations, exhortations, warnings, institutions, and the careful observation of upbringing, as well as praise and blame, and all similar things; this is because the choice of living rightly lies within us. If, therefore, most things that exist are within our own right, while others are outside our power: our own include desire, judgment, will, consent, preparation, choice, and aversion. But foreign things include riches, glory, physical appearance, strength, and other things which we can wish for rather than claim as our own. It is rightly said that if anyone should happen to want things that are not in our power, or presume them to be within our right, he knows nothing. Consequently, he who presumes that what is properly ours belongs to another also knows nothing, in my opinion. Finally, no one is praised for obtaining favorable circumstances which are not in a person's power—unless perhaps he is considered "blessed" <note>The author distinguishes between being "praiseworthy" for effort and "blessed" by luck.</note>. For prosperity is not within his own judgment. But indeed, in the contracts of justice, in the business of temperance <term>temperantia: the virtue of self-control and moderation</term>, and in the observance of the other virtues, we are rightly praised, since virtue is free. On the other hand, we are rebuked for acting against them, because we are considered to have applied ourselves to sinning.

<margin>
Whether sins are spontaneous.
The highest good.
</margin>
Furthermore, they say that sins are not spontaneous <note>original: "spontanea"; meaning voluntary or intentional</note>; for this reason, every soul, being a participant in divinity, always seeks the good by a natural appetite, yet it sometimes errs in its judgment of good and evil. For some of us think the highest good <term>summum bonum: the ultimate goal of human life</term> is pleasure; others think it is riches; many think it is glory; and all prefer these things more than the true good itself. The cause of error is manifold. The first is what the Stoics <note>A school of Hellenistic philosophy that emphasized ethics and logic.</note> call a "double perversion." This arises as much from the things themselves as from the spreading of common opinion. Indeed, for those just born and falling from the mother's womb, birth comes with considerable pain, because they migrate from a warm and moist place to the cold and dryness of the surrounding air. Against this pain, and for the protection of infants, the skillful foresight of midwives is applied in place of medicine: so that the newborn may be cherished with warm water, and a substitute and likeness of the mother's womb may be provided through heat and warmth. By this, the tender body is relaxed, delighted, and finds rest. Therefore, from both sensations—of pain as well as delight—a certain natural opinion arises: that everything sweet and delightful is good, and conversely, that whatever brings pain is evil and to be avoided. An equal and identical opinion is held regarding want and satisfaction, and regarding flattery and rebukes, when they have grown older. Because they are strengthened at that same age, they persist in this preconceived opinion: thinking every flattering thing is good, even if it is useless; and thinking everything laborious is evil, even if it brings benefit. Consequently, they exceedingly love riches, which are the most ready instrument for these pleasures, and they embrace glory in place of honor.

<margin>
Honor.
The error of the common people.
</margin>
By nature, indeed, every human is desirous of praise and honor. For honor is the testimony of virtue. But wise men, practiced in the pursuit of wisdom, know what kind of virtue they ought to cultivate and how. The unlearned common people, however, because of their ignorance of things, cultivate glory and popular reputation instead of honor; instead of virtue, they pursue a life soaked in pleasures, thinking that the power to do what they want is a kind of kingly excellence. For by nature, man is a "kingly" animal; and because kingship always accompanies power, they suspect that kingship also obeys power—whereas true kingship is the just protection of parents <note>Likely referring to the paternal/protective nature of a true ruler.</note>. At the same time, because it is necessary for a blessed person to live pleasantly, they think that those who live with pleasure will be blessed. Such is the error, I believe, which arises from things and possesses the minds of men. Furthermore, following the aforementioned error, there is the whispering of mothers and nurses regarding riches, glory, and other falsely-judged goods; also the terrors by which a tender age is more violently moved; and also in...

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the philosophical distinction between things within our control (will, judgment) and external things (wealth, health), arguing that virtue is a free choice. It also explores the Stoic concept of how humans fall into error by confusing physical pleasure with the "highest good" from the moment of birth.</summary>
<keywords>free will, Stoicism, highest good, virtue, pleasure vs. pain, Chalcidius, moral philosophy, education of children</keywords>

# On Plato’s Timaeus

XLII.

...disturbances from the usual things, and all such matters. Furthermore, poetry—the soother of strengthened minds—and the other magnificent works of writers and authors, how much do they lead unrefined minds toward pleasure and toil by a leaning of favor? What of painters
<margin>Poetry.</margin>
and sculptors too? Do they not skillfully snatch away minds toward sweetness? But the greatest
<margin>Painters.</margin>
arousal of vices exists in the union of the body and the humors <note>The four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—thought to determine temperament.</note> of the soul. By their abundance or deficiency, we are more prone to lust or anger. To these are added the distinctions of life’s conduct and fate: sickness, and the lack of necessary things. Occupied by these, we are led away from honorable studies toward the duties consistent with an established life, and we are recalled from the knowledge of the true good. Therefore, future wise men need both a liberal education and precepts leading to honor, as well as an erudition separated from the common crowd; all chosen things pertaining to wisdom must be commanded and observed by them. Above all, divine protection is needed for the perception of the greatest goods. Since these are the property of divinity, they are then shared with men. The obedience of the body must also be sufficient for the powers of the soul to endure exercise and labor. Moreover, good teachers ought to be sufficient, as well as that divine spirit <note>original: "numen"</note> which we have each been allotted as our goal.
<margin>Divine spirit or guardian.</margin>
Indeed, a companion spirit <note>original: "demon"; referring to the *daimonion*, the internal voice Socrates claimed guided him.</note> is said to have been the teacher of things to be done for Socrates from his boyhood—not to urge him toward any given act, but to forbid those things which were not expedient to be done. Therefore, if things within a man's power are done through imprudence when it is useless to do them, they would bring disaster to Socrates; but the benevolent spirit kept such things away from him. Truly, divination
<margin>Divination.</margin>
concerning things subject to necessity is certain, and even concerning ambiguous things whose outcome is already determined by decrees, it is true and comprehensible—if indeed it should be called divination. For things that have once happened cannot be undone. But concerning ambiguous things whose outcomes still hang in the balance, and where preceding merits do not yet exist, divination is ambiguous and indirect, such as that of Apollo: *Crossing the Halys, Croesus will destroy a great kingdom.* <note>A famous ambiguous oracle; Croesus thought he would destroy the Persian Empire, but he destroyed his own.</note> In that instance, if I am not mistaken, three things were ambiguous. One: whether the kingdom of Cyrus and the Persians would perish. Second: whether it would rather be that of Croesus himself and the Lydians. Third: whether the war would be set aside on just conditions. For this could have happened, and several examples of wars being set aside had preceded it; but because the will of both was opposed to the laying down of arms—since Cyrus was of a certain fierce and vainglorious nature, and Croesus was confident and overly greedy for power—the decree that followed was made firm by the purpose of both that there would be no peace between them. Therefore, one of the remaining two options was left, and it was doubtful whose kingdom would be extinguished. Because the lot was doubtful, the oracle proceeded with a doubtful meaning, so that whatever happened would seem to have been predicted by Apollo. There are other predictions similar to the weighing of counsel. Since it is in our power to choose one of two uncertain things, the propitious divinity advises men what is to be desired so that they do not err in their choice through ignorance. Thus, to the Argives asking through the oracle whether it was fitting to undertake a war against the Persians, the response was:

> O people hated by your neighbors, but most dear to God, keep back the protection of your arms; the shadow of the head alone will guard every danger of the body.

For the divinity knew what ought to be chosen, and that the choice was in the power of man; but that which follows the choice is in the power of fate. To the Hebrews also, counsel was given by God with a prediction of future things in this manner:

> If you obey my precepts, all the good things of the earth shall be yours. Therefore, flows of milk and honey shall not be lacking.

If you despise them, a series of imminent punishments followed from the divine voice. Indeed, because that which was placed in the power of man was doubtful—whether to obey or despise the heavenly commands. For if an inevitable decree proceeded from their choice, and it was necessary to be despised, the prediction would be superfluous, and even...

<sig>f ii</sig>

<summary>This page discusses how external arts and internal bodily humors distract the soul, the role of Socrates' personal 'daemon' in providing guidance, and the nature of ambiguous oracles like those given to Croesus and the Argives.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically addressing the philosophical problem of fate and free will. It follows arguments regarding the nature of human responsibility.</meta>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

<margin>Against those who use fate as an excuse.</margin>
promises and threats. There is, therefore, something within the power of man: nor are men merely the "material" of those things which are done (as was asserted by those of the opposing view) through which actions are carried out; rather, man is the preceding cause, which that which proceeds from fate then follows. <note>Chalcidius argues that human choice acts as the primary trigger, and fate is the subsequent unfolding of consequences.</note>

But they say that many things happen contrary to expectation. We know this: and of all these things there are two types. One consists of those which happen rarely: which occur either by hap <note>original: "forte"</note> or are brought about by some chance <note>original: "casu"</note>, such as the birth of monsters among men. The other type happens more frequently, but takes its origin from the corruption of human judgment: when matters are judged either by angry men in power or by enemies. This happened to Socrates; and it was by the judgment of his people that Aristides, a most just man, was condemned; or when prophets <note>likely referring to Biblical or legendary figures</note> were killed by the wicked—one sawn limb from limb, another overwhelmed by stones. Surely the cause of these things does not rest with fate? Nor do they understand that they are assigning diverse and contrary powers—that is, virtue and likewise vices—to a single decree, which is impossible; yet they say such crimes are proper to fate. Let them finally determine what they wish fate to be. Is it a divine virtue? But then it would not be the cause of evils. Or is it a malignant soul?

But surely nothing good can come from malice: and yet good things are also said to come about by fate. Perhaps they will say it is a certain mixed substance? But how can it be that one and the same thing is endowed with both malice and goodness at once, creating both intemperance and chastity, and bringing in the rest of the contrariety of virtues and vices? Furthermore, what shall be the estimation of fate? That it certainly wishes all things to be good, but is nevertheless unable? It would therefore be something weak and without strength. Or is it able, yet does not wish to? This would truly be a certain wild and monstrous envy. Or does it neither wish nor have the power? To say this of fate is especially shameful. Or is it both able and willing? It will therefore be the cause of all good things, and the authority of evils will not pertain to fate. From where, then, do evils come? From the motion of the stars, they say. But from where does that motion itself come? And is that motion such that both evil and good come from it by the will of the stars, or against their will? If by their will, the stars are living beings <note>original: "animalia"</note> and move according to a purpose. If against their will, it is no act of theirs. Certainly, either all stars are divine and good, and do nothing of evil; or some are evildoers <note>term: "maleficae" - a technical astrological term for planets like Saturn or Mars thought to have a negative influence</note>. But how is it fitting for there to be evildoers in that holy place full of goodness? Since all the stars are full of celestial wisdom, and we know that malice is born from madness, how is it fitting to say the stars are evildoers? Unless perhaps—which is not right to believe—one thinks the same stars are sometimes good and sometimes malignant, and for that reason provide benefits and injuries indiscriminately. But it is absurd to think that the celestial substance, endowed with one and the same nature, is not the same in all stars, but that many degenerate from their own nature. But doubtless the stars suffer this against their will; and what great necessity would that be, which compels them to do wrong against their will? And would this necessity itself be a divine soul, or a malignant one? Or is it a certain "reason" <note>original: "ratio"</note>, as they say, by which all things are done that are currently happening and all that will be in the future? But surely it is like a miracle <note>or "monstrosity"</note> to say that evils happen by reason; they would much more truly be said to happen by no reason. An unjust or lustful man is also without reason. From where shall that inevitable series of causes take its beginning, unless our merits are first placed on one side or the other? But who could bear this: that besides the other things which are said and thought irreligiously, the providence of God is also removed by this assertion of theirs, and at the same time all divinity is banished? For what will God do if all things happen according to this affirmation of crafty men, and are carried along by a rapid impulse according to the instinct of necessity? Yet this vain presumption makes the case easier for the guilty, who will be allowed not to condemn the perversity of their own minds, but to complain of fatal violence; it makes the prayers of the good for a laudable life and the impulses of wisdom more sluggish. Therefore, this kind of [argument] must be dismissed...

<summary>Chalcidius argues against a deterministic view of fate, asserting that human choice is the primary cause of actions and that attributing evil to divine stars or a "rational" fate is logically and theologically inconsistent.</summary>
<keywords>fate, free will, astrology, stars, Socrates, Aristides, providence, problem of evil, Chalcidius, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato’s Timaeus, specifically addressing the relationship between Divine Providence, Fate, and the soul of the world.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus.

...composed of the craftiness and vanity of men: who, as they themselves believe, are born with an adverse fate—those to whom it has fallen by fate to hold such opinions. We, however, following the divine law, shall repeat from the beginning in an orderly arrangement what Plato spoke concerning fate by the instinct of truth itself (as it seems to me).

In the beginning, all things that exist, and the world itself, are contained and governed primarily by God and from the highest God: who is the supreme good, beyond all substance and all nature, better than any estimation or intellect; whom all things seek, since he himself is full of perfection and rest, and in need of no company. To say more of him now would be to wander from the path. Next comes the government of 

<margin>Providence</margin>
Providence, which holds the second rank of eminence after that supreme one, which the Greeks call *Nous* <note>original: "υσῦ" (likely a corruption of νους), meaning Mind or Intellect</note>. It is an intelligible essence, emulating goodness because of its unwearied turning toward the highest God. From Him, it draws a draught of goodness, by which it is itself adorned, just as all other things are made honorable by its authorship. This will of God, then, acting as a wise guardianship over all things, men call "Providence." It is not named so (as many think) 

<margin>From whence it is named.</margin>
because it runs ahead in seeing and understanding future events, but because it is the property of the Divine Mind to understand—which is the proper act of the mind—and the Mind of God is eternal. Therefore, the Mind of God is an eternal act of understanding. Following this Providence is 

<margin>Fate. Soul of the world. Second Mind.</margin>
**Fate**: a divine law promulgated to the intelligence with a wise harmony for the government of all things. This is obeyed by that which is called the "Second Mind," that is, the **Soul of the World**, which is tripartite (as was understood above); it is as if one were to call the soul of a skilled lawgiver "the law." According to this law—that is, Fate—all things are governed according to their own specific nature: celestial things indeed by a blessed necessity and unchangeable constancy, since they are near and contiguous to Providence. Natural events, however, occur frequently, because all things that happen by the law of nature rise and set. At the same time, because 

<margin>Art and discipline</margin>
art and discipline imitate nature, even those things produced by the arts are frequent and common occurrences. Those things governed by this law are governed by reason, order, and without violence. For nothing lacking reason and order is non-violent. Whatever is of such a nature does not endure long, as it is pulled apart against its own nature. Individual things therefore follow their own God: and as Plato says, the King and Commander of heaven, the prince of the host and sublime leader, dispensing and moderating all things from a winged chariot, distributes the legions of celestial 

<margin>Legions of angels</margin>
angelic powers into eleven parts. For he says that **Vesta** alone remains in her seat. By Vesta, he means the soul of the entire universe; and the mind of that soul 

<margin>Vesta. <unclear>Sanctified</unclear></margin>
moderating the reins of the starry heaven according to the law <insert>sanctified</insert> by Providence. This law we have often said is Fate, creating a certain series of consequences and orders. The "winged chariot" of the Commander God must be understood as the *aplanes* <note>The "aplanes" refers to the fixed, non-wandering sphere of the stars.</note>, because it is first in order and 

<margin>The fixed sphere</margin>
more agile than all other movements, as has been shown. The eleven parts of the divine army were reckoned thus far: First, the fixed sphere; then the seven planets; the ninth, the seat of the ether, which the ethereal demons inhabit; the tenth, the aerial; the eleventh, of the moist substance; the twelfth, the Earth, which remains immobile from the revolution of the world. But perhaps this is outside the purpose of the present discourse: that Fate is administered without violence and without any inextricable necessity, but rather in a healthy manner and order. 

<margin>Fate</margin>
From the order of these things flows those which are of rare occurrence: over some of which **Fortune** has power; others happen unforeseen and of their own accord, over which 

<margin>Chance</margin>
**Chance** is said to rule. These are indeed "fatal" <note>In the sense of being included within the jurisdiction of Fate.</note>, for they are contained within the edicts of Fate; yet they are not by violent necessity, just like the things done by us. For they are contained by our laws, yet they do not happen according to the laws we use. For example, the law commands that a traitor to the country be put to death. What then...

<sig>f iii</sig>

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic hierarchy of governance: starting with the Supreme God, followed by Providence (Divine Intellect), and then Fate (the law of the World Soul). It describes how the universe is organized into spheres and how human actions and chance fit into the overarching "law" of Fate without being strictly coerced.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary by Chalcidius on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the relationship between human free will, the law, and fate.</meta>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

<margin>† has fought</margin>
Because the law calls him a traitor for whom it has appointed a punishment, is it necessary that the law *makes* him a traitor? I think not. For a traitor, by his own evil mind—or rather by madness—breaks out into crime; he is, however, punished according to the law. Conversely, there is a law that whoever has fought bravely <note>original: *bellauerit*</note> should be rewarded. This law commands it, yet the law does not create the victor or the victory; nevertheless, the law gives the reward. Therefore, the law generally commands all things that ought to be done and prohibits everyone from inappropriate acts. But not all obey, nor do all do what is commanded. This reality shows that there is indeed a choice <note>term: *optionem* — the capacity for free choice or volition</note> in humans, though it is not the same in all. Those things which follow, however—that is, the legal consequences, namely punishments or rewards—stem from the sacred law. Such, I believe, is also that celestial law which is called Fate, decreeing honorable things for men and forbidding the opposite. To follow it, furthermore, is within our power and free from the yoke of fate. To praise one who acts well is consistent both with the law and with common judgment. Similarly, therefore, regarding the opposites: to lie and to live most wickedly is contained in the edict, and it is in a man's judgment as a preceding cause. To arrange one's life badly is the property of the man; therefore, to be punished is clearly out of fatal necessity, insofar as it follows the law.

<margin>† disposition</margin>
Furthermore, all these things have their seat in the souls of men; which soul is free and acts by its own judgment. The best part of the soul is that which Plato described as having a twofold virtue: one in the comprehension of divine things, which is Wisdom; the other in the management <note>original: *disputatione*, here meaning the handling or ordering of affairs</note> of mortal things, which is named Prudence. But if anyone, looking toward the humors of the body and that natural composition, thinks that many men are intemperate and others moderate not without fate—and that the intemperate suffer from an imbalance of humors, while the moderate are relieved by a fortunate composition—and thinks all these things happen by decree: he perceives the truth. For it is a tribute to nature that the weak race of men either suffers from an unlucky composition or is aided by a moderate one in the pursuit <note>original: *prouentu*</note> of honor. Therefore, against vices of this kind, the healthiness of reason and counsel is opposed by the law of providence.

<margin>† portent / outcome</margin>
Indeed, if greed, lust, cruelty, and other such plagues designate nothing grand in childhood, they are confirmed by a harmful age; then, I believe, when the healthiness of counsel is also confirmed. This is aided by the grace of honorable studies; it is profited by the rebuke of the benevolent; it is healed by punishment. Conversely, in adverse circumstances, the depravity of the mind is dulled and becomes insolent. Therefore, because of the fellowship of the body, there is among men, beasts, and other things lacking life a society and communion of bodily outcomes. Indeed, to be born, to be nourished, and to grow is common to humans and all others. But to feel and to desire is common only to humans and to mute animals lacking reason. For desire and anger—the appetite of either wild or tame creatures—is irrational. But for a man, whose property it is to apply his mind to reason, it is rational. However, the appetite for reasoning, understanding, and knowing the truth is proper to man alone, which differs greatly from desire and anger. For those things are seen even in mute animals, and indeed much more sharply. But the perfection of reason and intellect is proper to God and man only. And among men themselves, it is not equal. For desire, anger, and likewise reason fight against each other in the commotions of men, and they prevail against one another in turn. Reason prevails, as in Homer, when the young son of Laertes <note>note: referring to Odysseus</note>:

> Striking his breast, he rebuked his heart sharply:
> "Endure, my heart; for you have borne even more grievous things."

Indeed, in his soul, reason then subjugated anger. In Euripides, on the contrary, in the mind of Medea, savage anger had extinguished the light of reason: "For it does not escape me now what bloody things I contemplate; but anger overcomes the health of my heart."

<summary>Chalcidius discusses the interplay between human free will and the "law of fate," arguing that while fate decrees consequences, the initial choice to act rightly or wrongly belongs to the individual. He uses examples from Homer and Euripides to illustrate the internal struggle between reason and irrational passions like anger.</summary>
<keywords>Fate, Free Will, Law, Reason, Anger, Plato, Homer, Euripides, Medea, Odysseus, Stoicism, Moral Philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the conflict between reason and the lower parts of the soul, and the nature of fate versus divination.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus.
### f iiii

To such an extent had immoderate, seductive heat closed off the path of healthy counsel. Therefore, in the mind of a self-controlled man, counsel always has more power; whereas the weakness of the intemperate man supports the vicious parts of the soul against reason. Often these very vices even fight amongst themselves: as in the young man from Terence <note>The Roman playwright Terence; the reference is to his play *The Eunuch*.</note>, who, resisting the most bitter flames of love—
<margin>Terence.</margin>
Phaedria—strives with an honorable anger: when he says he will not go into the sight of his mistress who is summoning him of her own accord, so that she might not call him back, after he was unworthily shut out, by the seductive lure of a prostitute's flatteries. Therefore, these things which we debate within the secrets of our breast, and whether we deliberate if they should be done or not, and finally decree as if a sentence were passed, are not our own property—unless perhaps because, being moved or corrupted by lust, we are not suitable judges; since a just judgment ought to be free from the anticipations of favor and mercy.

<margin>The power of Fate</margin>
But they say that the prediction of future events testifies that they have long since been disposed and ordained. Furthermore, this disposition and ordination is called Fate. On the contrary, this very prediction denies that fatal necessity dominates everywhere. Indeed, prediction is a rational estimation of a future lot, which prevails not in certain things constrained by necessity, but in uncertain and ambiguous ones. For who would consult a seer about a newborn, whether he is to be mortal or immortal? <note>Since mortality is a certainty, divination is unnecessary.</note> But rather that which is doubtful is usually sought: what length of life he seems to have been allotted, and whether he will be rich or poor, and of a high or common and humble fortune. All these things are gathered by observation, science, and also by skillful ingenuity. For men are forewarned either by the flight of birds, or by entrails,
<margin>Types of divination.</margin>
or by oracles; some propitious spirit <note>original: "daemone"</note> foretelling, who is knowledgeable of all those things which follow in succession. Just as if a doctor, according to the discipline of healing, predicts either death or health; or even a pilot of the sky, not ignorant of the conditions, foretells a future storm from some small cloud. All these things are understood not by fate, but by skillful reasoning, use, and experience. Likewise, when a prediction is held from the motion of the stars, signs are usually observed. The rising and setting of stars and their configurations are reduced to a system, according to which fertility or sterility comes forth. And every reasoning of this kind is nothing other than a conjecture of those things which either pertain to the body, or to those things which are the body's own, or to the soul serving the fates of the body. Whence I believe Plato says that God the Craftsman <note>The Demiurge or Creator in Plato's philosophy.</note> presided over the building of souls; but the task and duty of those things which are woven beneath the souls was mandated to other inferior divine powers. Thus, while pure souls, sincere, vigorous, and flourishing in reason, were made by God—
<margin>Beware of thinking this is true.</margin>
these powers, to whom such care was mandated by God the Craftsman, are considered the authors of its vicious parts. Furthermore, the vicious parts of the soul which are woven beneath are anger and desire—instruments suitable for conducting the fate of life. Indeed, there are many things which are rightly done and vindicated in this life through a manly commotion of the mind, whenever just anger offers itself as a companion and helper to reason. There are also many honorable or moderate things from desire, beyond the filth of lusts. Therefore, just as the kingdom of the pure soul of the world is permitted in the perpetual agitation of the world, so in those souls which inspire men, there was a need for reason interspersed with anger and desires. Thus, when the whole living being turns itself toward reason, it might care for and even gaze upon celestial things; but when it looks down toward earthly things, the looking down would not be idle, but from that same inclination, the care for earthly things would be born.

To embrace many things in brief, the disposition of this matter must be conceived in the mind as follows:

<meta>This page continues the commentary by Chalcidius on Plato’s Timaeus, specifically focusing on the hierarchy of divine providence and the relationship between fate and human free will.</meta>

# The Interpretation of Chalcidius

<margin>
God is the origin of all.
The Lawgiver.
The Second Mind.
The Triple God.
The Law.
</margin>
God is the origin of all. Indeed, the origin of things, from which the substance of all other existing things is provided, is the supreme and ineffable God. After Him is His Providence, the Second God, who is the giver of the law for both lives, the eternal as well as the temporary. Furthermore, there is a third substance, which is called the second mind and intellect, acting as a kind of guardian of the eternal law. Subject to these are the rational souls that obey the law, and the ministering powers: Nature, Fortune, Chance, and the Daemons <note>In this context, "daemons" refers to guardian spirits or intermediary beings, not evil spirits.</note> who are the inspectors and observers of merits. Therefore, the supreme God commands; the second orders; the third intimates. Souls, in turn, carry out the law. Furthermore, the law itself is Fate, as we have often said. Whoever obeys this law and follows the venerable footsteps of the leading God always leads a blessed life, according to the sanction of the perpetual law, which is according to fate. But as for those souls who neglect the company of God, they lead their lives according to fate in another and contrary manner, until they repent of their transgressions. Once their crimes are expiated, they may then return to the choirs of the immortal God and the eternal divine powers; and that rigor of the law allows for a transition from a worse fortune to a blessed one. This certainly could not happen if all things were bound by a certain uniform, rigid, and irrevocable necessity. From which

<margin>
Providence.
Fate.
† collection.
† arrangement.
</margin>
it appears that Providence indeed encompasses all things within itself; for it holds all things that are rightly administered according to it. But Fate is the decree of Providence. It contains those things which are in our power as preceding causes; it also contains the † comparison <note>original: "collatio"</note> of merits. Punishment and approval follow, which are fated, as are all those things which happen by chance or fortune. What, therefore, was the purpose of such a long and detailed discussion? It is because many people have no care for knowing the truth, but rather for resisting true reasons, and they are both deceived themselves and involve others in the slippery path of inescapable error. Indeed, looking at only one part of the world's administration, they pronounce as if upon the solid and universal arrangement. Whatever they find in one part, they assert to be in all parts of the world as well. Therefore, when they say something true, they are considered † likely to be true <note>original: "verisimiles"</note>, even though they say things that are mutually contradictory.

<margin>
† Trustworthy to the faithful.
</margin>
However, when they hold opinions about a part as if they were opinions about the solid whole and the universe, they are refuted by one another. For it is true that some things are driven by fate, and it has also been shown to be true that some things are in our power. Therefore, those who say that all things happen by fate are rightly rebuked by those who prove that something is in our power. Finally, those who place everything in our power and leave nothing to fate are discovered to be deceived. For who is ignorant that there is something in fate and outside of our jurisdiction? Therefore, the only true reasoning, and the fixed and stable opinion, is that which teaches that some things happen by fate, while others proceed from the choice and will of men. Next, he subjoins the laws themselves and the fated rights.

<margin>
Laws and fated rights.
</margin>
The primary law is that by which an equal capacity is granted by fate to all souls—due to their uniform nature—for the claim of virtue, without any prior distinction of person. He adds a second law, by which it is ordained that all souls who have followed the divine procession and beheld the nature of eternal and truly existing things, shall put on the form of a human to look after earthly matters. He signifies man as a "pious nation of animal" <note>A reference to man's unique capacity for religion among living creatures.</note>; therefore, in mute animals, there is no knowledge of God. Furthermore, among those beings endowed with reason, the most excellent animal is that which bestows substance from itself upon the other celestial animals, yet worships another far more excellent being in whom is the origin of things. There follow, as if by a certain order, powers of inferior eminence, each of which venerates the higher deities. Because, therefore, man is the last of those who use reason...

<summary>This page outlines Chalcidius's Neoplatonic hierarchy of divinity (the Triple God) and argues for a middle ground between total determinism and total free will, defining Fate as a law decreed by Providence.</summary>
<keywords>Chalcidius, Providence, Fate, Free Will, Neoplatonism, Divine Law, Hierarchy of Being, Human Soul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato’s Timaeus, specifically discussing the incarnation of the soul and the origins of human sensation and emotion.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus. Folio XLV.

f v

<note>The author continues discussing the hierarchy of living beings.</note>
...among animals, and since the human venerates all things better and more sublime, it is said that he is rightly endowed with a primary religious nature. And since the nature of man is twofold—one part better, the other inferior—there was need for a third law to distinguish these parts. Furthermore, the male sex is the better; the female <margin>The reason for the sexes.</margin> is the inferior. This is not because there is any inherent difference in their souls—for indeed, their nature is one—but because a diversity in sex was necessary for the sake of fleeting mortality, which requires the birth of offspring and the substitution of generations. Finally, although the virtue of the male and female is one and the same, the strengths of their bodies are different, since one is hotter and denser, while the other is colder and thinner. Another law follows, which he designates with these words: *And when by the necessity of a decree they shall be inserted into bodies, the bodily equipment shall be variously changed, with some members falling away and others succeeding in their turn.* <margin>Necessity.</margin> He calls "necessity" here the inexcusable use and the duty of incorporation; for earthly things could not otherwise be ruled by a governing rational soul unless the union of a body were applied.

<margin>The outlets of the body.</margin>
For this earthly body does not remain stable for long; but like a rushing torrent, it both flows out itself and is inundated by things flowing in. Indeed, there are many passages in it, and likewise many fragile parts—some obvious and visible, others hidden and blind—through which moisture, nourishment, and spirit <note>original: "spiritus," referring to the vital breath or pneuma</note> are carried. There are also other superfluous remains of food and drink, for which nature has devised exits for the sake of the animals' preservation; and against the losses of excretion, she restores the balance in a competent manner from moist and dry food, and from the respiration of the surrounding air from without. Therefore, nature has fabricated passages for all things entering, according to their measure and size. This is exactly what the same Plato says: *With some members hidden and others succeeding in their turn.* This law, therefore, brought it about above all else that sensation is excited by violent passions <note>In this context, "passions" refers to external impressions or "undergoings" that affect the body.</note>. Justly so. For life cannot exist without sensation. Indeed, between those things which live and those which lack life, this is, I believe, the difference: that the former feel, while the latter are without sensation. Those things being born begin their sensation simultaneously with life, <margin>The separation of soul and body.</margin> and in the separation of the body and soul, they cease to feel. Certainly, whether it be a passion, a power, or some affection, this sensation is proper to the living being, forged from the coupling of the body and the soul. He says "from violent passions" rightly, for the soul contemplates with a similar ease. Indeed, contemplation is an easy application of the mind. <margin>The origin of sensation.</margin> Sensation, however, is born from the fellowship of the passions of the body and soul. There are many such pulsations of the body that do not reach the soul or sensation, but vanish hidden away, such as a slip in seeing or hearing. However, certain pulses of the body are more violent, and these reach all the way to the soul.

<margin>What sensation is. † Light.</margin>
Sensation is therefore a passion of the body, with certain external things pulsing in various ways, and that same passion traveling all the way to the seat of the soul. When this passion is smooth, moving the body by stroking it gently rather than pulsing against it, the soul consents to the tickling of the body. And this kind of sensation is named pleasure. Furthermore, if the passion is endowed with a certain roughness, it shall receive the name of pain. Thus it is clear that pleasure and pain are species of sensation, while sensation itself is the genus. Moreover, every such desire is mixed with the affection of pleasure and pain. Consequently, the love of such things is found positioned between pain and pleasure. <margin>Hope.</margin> For the hope of enjoying creates pleasure, while delay creates sadness and pain. Therefore, the law of loving follows the law of sensing. <margin>Love.</margin> Furthermore, love contributes greatly to the preservation of the body. It is also the author of successions. This same thing is found in anger as well, whenever we are moved without reason and without measure by a certain wild heat of the mind. <margin>Caution.</margin> Against this, the soul is equipped with the healthfulness of caution. When this becomes excessive, it passes into the vice of dread. And so, when the mind's excess...

<meta>This page continues Chalcidius's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the moral consequences of the soul's embodiment and the process of reincarnation based on one's conduct.</meta>

# Chalcidius's Interpretation

vigor, which having become immoderate, has advanced to the limit of wrath; and likewise caution was given to the soul for the protection of the body: but when these grow immoderately, they disturb the soul rather than rule it.

<margin>Innate vices</margin>
And he says the other "handmaidens" of these disturbances move the soul with diverse affections according to their nature, until they take on the fitting forms of wild beasts. Indeed, he calls the passions that are related to and consistent with desire and pain "handmaidens"—namely emulation <note>original: "aemulationem"; here meaning a jealous rivalry</note>, envy, detraction, and others of this sort. Truly, to pleasure he assigns joy in the misfortunes of others, boasting, and the vanity of glory. Furthermore, to fear he assigns flight and dread. To wrath, nonetheless, he assigns cruelty, ferocity, heat, and whatever is closest to these. He says they move with diverse affection according to their nature—namely those ornaments of virtue which are contrary to these very things, which are created by the virtues. If they were to bridle these vices, he says, 

<margin>Souls free in choosing</margin>
their life would be just and mild. Clearly, and in such a way that we cannot doubt it, he teaches that souls are of their own right <note>original: "sui iuris"; possessing free will or legal autonomy</note> in their selection and choice. He even established rewards for all of these.

For souls that are victors over vices, there is a return to the celestial seat and perpetual immunity. But those which have defiled themselves with lust, in the gift of a second life, shall have a harsher life, fitting for their morals and habits. For instance, they may pass from the male sex into the form of a woman: not because the soul itself changes sex, but because it receives a worse body. The soul which has chosen a better and more temperate life will be recalled to a better <margin>†form.</margin> fortune <note>The dagger symbol indicates a textual corruption or variation in the manuscript between "form" and "fortune".</note>. Furthermore, if it has become worse, and lives more poorly because of an excessive embracing of the body, it will be destined for a more unhappy life until it reaches the ferocity of a wild beast's nature. However, Empedocles, following Pythagoras, says that they do not only obtain a wild and fierce nature, but even various forms, when he speaks thus:

> For I have already lived as a boy, and a sturdy tree,
> And an animal of such sex, and then a milky maiden.

This is not according to a wild conversion of sex, but into diverse living creatures, as he himself testifies in other verses in which he judges that one ought to abstain from animals:

> An impious father slaughters the changed limbs of his offspring,
> Offering a feast to the gods with a cruel prayer in his terrified mind.
> The table is defiled with a mournful feast of the victim.
> Likewise a son kills both mother and father as if they were cattle,
<margin>Likewise in another place.</margin>
> Nor does he feel his dear ones' limbs as he chews them beneath his teeth.
> Restrain yourselves, O nations, from homicides: do you not see
> That you are chewing your own limbs and your own entrails?

<margin>Transformation of humans into beasts.</margin>
But Plato does not think it reasonable for a rational soul to put on the face and mouth of an animal lacking reason; rather, as the body approaches the remnants of vices, the embodiment becomes wild from the habits of the previous life as the soul's vices increase. Thus, a wrathful man who is also brave is carried forward even to the ferocity of a lion; while one who is fierce and also rapacious reaches the closest likeness to the nature of wolves; and likewise for others. But when a return to their former fortune occurs for souls, this cannot happen unless the return has first been made pure and mild, and the habit is worthy of a human. Furthermore, the correction of a rational <margin>Repentance.</margin> counsel, which is repentance <note>original: "poenitudo"</note>, does not occur in those things which live without reason. The soul that was once human by no means passes to beasts according to Plato. Then he says: 
There shall be no pause from evils until that similar and always identical flight of the world's rotation has followed them, and wiped away all their vices contracted from fire, water, earth, and air, and all the filth; and their ill-advised and immoderate errors are reduced to the measure and temperance of reason.
