<meta>This page continues a commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the creation of the human soul and body by the Divine Craftsman and the lesser gods.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus.

Signifying that the same circular motion always belongs to the understanding soul: it is similar to the soul when it deliberates in order to understand. Indeed, these are the virtues of the rational soul. Furthermore, he says that the unadvised and immoderate errors of the soul are those which arise from fire, earth, and the other bodily elements. Struggling against these, the soul will return to its ancient divinity, and will possess a nature suitable to its good merits. He says these laws were made known to souls by the great craftsman, God: and he shows the cause of this disclosure: so that no authority for wrongdoing should henceforth reside with Him through silence. He wishes to show that malice does not proceed to men from the decree and will of God, but rather from their own imprudence or depravity. Then, after the explanation of the <margin>† of fate</margin> laws of fate,† God performs the sowing of our souls, partly in the earth, <margin>Men in the Moon.</margin> partly in the moon: which he says are the instruments of time. According to Pythagoras, indeed, men exist on the lunar globe just as they do on earth: and also in the other wandering stars <note>The planets</note>. But according to his own view, he asserts in other books that souls, to whom the guardianship of earthly things is mandated, perform their duty in alternating turns: so that those who now perform the primary duty are carried on the earth; those after these, on the lunar globe; and then the other globes in succession. Then he commands the gods made by him—that is, the stars—to fashion human bodies as receptacles for the appropriate souls, and to apply to them all the supports of vital substance. In the beginning, the appetite, which is divided into anger and desire, <margin>Division of appetite.</margin> without which the body of a mortal living being can neither exist nor indeed be managed. Then the arts which grant necessary sustenance, and all other things according to their strength, so that the mortal nature may be ruled as well as possible. "According to their strength" refers clearly not to those who were effective, but to those who were being fashioned: because men have inclinations toward both goodness and malice. From this it happens that they are often the authors of their own evils. Such, therefore, was the ordination of the highest craftsman, God: that whatever was in the mortal body that was least mortal—but rather divine and eternal—he himself should construct. Furthermore, those things which were to be dissoluble, he commanded the lesser [gods] to fashion. Finally, the disposition being completed, he remained, he says, in his own state of being. What does this mean? Surely that by his providence he might consult for all things, both from eternity and those born and generated: propagating immortality to the divine and eternal things, but to the temporary things a perennity that is not continuous and whole, but through succession. ¶ Then he teaches the very building of the human body, the cause of feeling, and the other things that follow sense. From the materials of the world, he says—fire, earth, and water with spirit <note>original: "spiritu," often referring to air or breath</note>—having borrowed a "loan of elements" which would be returned when there was need, and so forth. It is evident that our bodies are composed of four <margin>Bodies from four materials</margin> materials. For there is something contiguous in them which resists touch, and that is not without earthly solidity. There is also another thing, hot and likewise visible: furthermore, such things cannot exist without fire. It is, moreover, full of spirit: such as these veins which are called arteries. There is also moisture: such as blood, and other things which flow from the body. But neither moisture without water, nor spirit without air, will ever consist. There is, therefore, in our bodies a portion of water, and likewise of air, as well as of fire and earth. Whence I think man was called a "brief world" <note>A microcosm</note> by the ancients, <margin>Man a brief world</margin> and not without merit. Because the whole world, and likewise the whole man, are from all the same things; the body indeed having the same materials, and the soul also being of one and the same nature. Furthermore, he calls the invisible joinings *Gomphoi* <note>Greek: γόμφοι, meaning "pegs" or "bolts"</note>; either a <margin>Gomphoi</margin> heaping together of smaller corpuscles, as Diodorus says; or a shapable balling together of similar things among themselves, as Anaxagoras says; or a multiform entanglement of the aforementioned things, as Democritus and Leucippus say; or sometimes a concretion, sometimes a discretion <note>separation</note>: as...

<meta>This page continues the commentary by Chalcidius on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the union of the soul and body and the turbulent nature of physical existence.</meta>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

<margin>Pegs.</margin>
Empedocles <note>A pre-Socratic philosopher who theorized that the universe is composed of four elements joined by Love and separated by Strife.</note> calls union "friendship" and, conversely, calls distraction and separation "enmity." Or, as the Stoics <note>A school of Hellenistic philosophy</note> put it, the union of diverse bodies everywhere. He says the "knot" or chain of all these things exists in minute and solid little bodies, which he names *gomphoi* <note>original: γόμφους (gomphous). These are "pegs" or "bolts" used in shipbuilding, here serving as a metaphor for the biological bonds of the body.</note>. In such a construction of the human body, he says God added two circuits of the eternal soul. These circuits are the works of the soul: one of which is intelligence, the other is opinion. Furthermore, everything that acts must necessarily exist. Therefore, the substance of the soul is by no means idle. Thus, the human body is bound on all sides by the two circular motions of the immortal soul, and the whole is sustained by vital vigor from every region.

<margin>Circuits of the soul. The Torrent.</margin>
Furthermore, he calls the body "irrigated" and "fluid" because of the aforementioned cycles of depletion and saturation. He says the circuits are bound as if to a rapid and downward-flowing torrent. He calls the corporeal "forest" <note>original: *sylvam*. In Platonic Latin, *silva* (Greek: *hyle*) refers to raw matter or "wood."</note> a torrent because it never ceases to flow down, nor does it ever remain in a certain and stable constancy, nor can it be held back. He says that souls did not "hold" the bodies, nor indeed could they be restrained by the bodies. Therefore, the souls did not rule the bodies, because the bodies did not then obey the soul's nod or restraint at will. Furthermore, the souls were not ultimately held by the bodies, since the bodies themselves were held together by the circuit of the soul, and without its embrace, they would soon dissipate. But he says they "bore" and "were borne" in turn. They were "borne" toward vices by the compelling need of the body. But they "bore" those same bodies by providing life and health to them, which is the proper duty of souls. However, neither of these happens without violence: since souls did not fall into vice by their own will, nor did it please the bodies to endure and sustain natural needs patiently, as they were enduring the dissolution and dissipation of their own nature. It happened, therefore, that the whole living being was moved, yet with a headlong and disordered tossing. This is excellent. For that which moves cannot produce motion without that which is moved; nor, finally, can that which is moved exist without a mover; rather, the presence of both is required. And it is, I believe, the soul that moves, and the body that is moved: from which both living beings and their motions are constituted. And all these things look toward conceiving the "folly" of the soul in a child's life, and the way in which sense is excited in all living beings according to their nature.

<margin>The six motions</margin>
Those six motions are familiar. For there is fire in these, whose nature always seeks the highest and loftiest places. There is also a certain amount of earth, which occupies the middle seat of the world; there is something of air and moisture: these two are carried hither and thither widely and toward all things by their nature. The living being is master of these motions. Furthermore, the soul of a child is more mobile and restless, chiefly because of the irrational weakness of the mind, as reason is not yet firm. Then, because of the excessive heat and moisture abounding in that age—just as he says in the *Laws* <note>Plato's last and longest dialogue</note>—children struggle with an excessive heat of fire; therefore, natural motions are celebrated in children in a disordered way, without judgment, and altogether wickedly. Whence he says, "Irrigated by an immense whirlpool and flowing out immoderately." Irrigating, indeed, when food is consumed more often because it is quickly used up by excessive heat; flowing out, however, according to the necessity of excretion.

<margin>Hippocrates.</margin>
Hippocrates <note>The famous Greek physician, often called the "Father of Medicine."</note> bears witness to this, decreeing that the bodies of children must be refreshed with much food, lest they be consumed by the losses of excretion. But when food is applied, the tender age is delighted; when it is diminished or denied, it suffers. From this, it happens that it embraces pleasure as a good, but is offended by pain as an evil. Also those things which happen from without: cold and dryness, or a rougher and harder touch. All these excite harsh sensations; and those things contrary to these excite pleasant and delightful sensations in touch, smell, and taste. Which senses, he says, and...

<summary>This page discusses the physical and metaphysical union of the soul and body, using the metaphor of "pegs" to describe their connection. It explains the turbulent nature of childhood development as a struggle between the soul's rational circuits and the "torrent" of material needs and bodily motions.</summary>

# On Plato’s Timaeus. Folio XLVII.

...from the beginning and even until now, they stir up the greatest and most violent motions. Indeed, the senses—aided by the "forest" <note>original: "sylua," referring to *hyle* or primordial matter</note>—disturb the entire soul and all its actions with a violent shaking through an excessive and disordered flux. And since one part of the soul is more holy and divine—which is the mind and the intellect, never wandering out of orbit nor steerable by any errors or false opinions—

<margin>The Mind.</margin>
they have indeed restrained the motion of this part, and checked its dominion over the whole soul by enacting contrary motions: so that there might indeed be an intellect in the soul, but that it should achieve nothing while the senses act as impediments. Yet, they were entirely unable to destroy that other part of the soul, which is "opinion" (for this too was endowed with divine power), nor was it able to be dissolved except by Him alone who had woven it together, prior to the fated separation of soul and body. Nevertheless, they forced it to strive against its own nature and to perform unsuitable acts, compelling it to believe in false opinions.

Then, reminding us of those things which he had taught in the *Psychogenia* <note>The "generation of the soul," a technical term for the section of the Timaeus describing the soul's mathematical composition</note>, he says those means and proportions were driven out, as if from their own region and place—those which used to direct the harmonious motions of the soul along celestial paths. He signifies by this that in children, the mind and its intellect are entirely idle, and as if bound by chains that prohibit its motion. Yet reason does perform some action when that tender age either desires something, or judges, or imagines—though not rightly—because the senses drag it toward these things and deceive it in that manner, due to the aforementioned causes and an ignorance of reality. In this, Aristotle also agrees, saying that in the beginning, children—even into the year of their nursing age—think all men are fathers and all women are mothers; however, with the passing of age, they learn to discern, though sometimes they fail in that discernment. Often they even stretch out their hands toward a false image as if it were real. He calls all these "supine opinions," when they judge things that are harmful to be beneficial, and things that are useful to be noxious—that is, when they are carried toward ruinous pleasure but are offended by healthy labor. This would certainly never happen unless they trusted the senses too much, which by nature are most potent in deceiving. And so that he might clearly explain the whole matter, he uses an example of transparent clarity. It is the highest madness when someone is not only ignorant, but is unaware of the very fact that he is ignorant; and because of this, he consents to false images and presumes that things which are true are false: as when they judge that malice is profitable, but that virtue is a hindrance and brings ruin. This opinion indeed accompanies most people even into their latest age—those who think that doing injury is most advantageous and doing right is damaging, and therefore they curse the right. Aristotle calls these old men "children," because their mind differs very little from the mind of a child. The example we mentioned is widespread: standing opposite someone, we certainly have the right-hand parts of those facing us on our left; likewise, they have ours; and again, left parts on the right. If, therefore, he says, one of two people stands upright and according to nature, but the other stands so that he fixes his head on the ground and raises his feet in place of his head, and they are facing each other: then, looking at one another, they will mutually judge right parts to be left, and left parts to be right. He says the soul suffers something similar regarding the contemplation of things during its beginnings and when recently incorporated into a body. When, therefore, the soul is in such a state and hears that there is a nature that is "intelligible" <note>meaning graspable only by the mind, not the senses</note> and without a body—which alone is truly and perpetually the same—and hears again concerning those things which are born and die (that is, concerning corporeal and sensible things) that they are fluid and changeable; I believe that then, having summoned the senses, the childish soul easily believing their testimony, concludes that there is no "intelligible" nature at all, since the senses do not even dream of such a thing. It presumes that only sensible things—which can be seen, touched, and perceived by the other senses—exist for certain. And so, presuming this, I do not think the intel[lect] of the mind...

<summary>This page discusses the disorientation of the human soul upon entering a physical body, explaining how sensory input overwhelms the intellect and leads to "supine opinions" or false judgments. It uses the analogy of an upside-down person to illustrate how the soul misinterprets reality, favoring the physical over the eternal.</summary>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

and he will be in possession of his faculties. Therefore he added: *Nor does such a wandering have any certain leader.* For neither does the intellect offer itself as a leader to the other parts of the soul—as it is then idle—nor does that other part of the soul in which opinions reside; for the senses have subdued it and reduced it into servitude, to such an extent that while it serves, it thinks it rules, and retains something of its former splendor and dignity. And lest anyone should think he was saying these things about the soul of just any animal, he added:

<margin>day by day</margin>
But as the human grows, and after the surge of nourishment flows out now in a thin stream of passage, the circuits of the soul will complete their path with a more tranquil motion, and with the passage of time, they will become more settled. For a person rightly becomes better day by day as he becomes more perfect and more practiced in the knowledge of things. For then the messengers of the senses will be of less authority and of less credit to the soul, once their habit of deceiving has been detected. Especially if he is educated, he says, in the more elegant studies, he will spend his life free from all disturbance and distress. If he is negligent, then crawling along the limping path of life, he will at last be called back to the lower regions with his familiar folly. Consequently, he composes the human being from soul and body. Whosoever, therefore, cares for both will be safe and strong. He who cares for neither will be maimed and weak. He who cares for only one of the two—limping, unteachable, and bestowing diligence only upon the body—will undoubtedly have a "limping" life. His soul will also be "limping," even if his opinions are correct, if he has by no means been initiated into the sacred mysteries of intelligible things. At the same time, because he has discussed the constitution of the whole human being through its parts, he now reveals the utility of each member piece by piece, just as he had done in the constitution of the worldly body; and he gives the reasons for our sight and hearing. He also pursues the utility of the senses and of those members which assist the senses. He speaks also of memory. He speaks of dreams. He also gives the reasons for those images seen in mirrors, and he subdivides the causes and reasons for all of them. He demonstrates also what the primary causes are, and what likewise happens to the primary ones; which are themselves indeed immovable, yet move others; which finally are moved by these very things out of some necessity; which move the affection of some useful thing with reason; and which move without reason and at will. Finally, he begins from the head, which part of the body he says is of a certain principal eminence. And for that reason, it ought to have been placed in a high and eminent location like the citadel of the whole body, so that it might be the dwelling place of the principal part of the soul, which is called <term>hēgemonikon</term> <note>original: ἡγεμονικὸν; the "ruling part" or "governing faculty" in Greek philosophy</note> by the philosophers, that is, reason.

<margin>Reason.</margin>
The virtue of which is twofold: one part is understanding, the other is opining; according to which wisdom with discipline, and likewise prudence with correct opinions, grow strong. These two the same author calls the "divine revolutions" of the soul; and according to them, he says the divine powers who fashioned the bodies of men in the likeness of the worldly body formed the head round and spherical, and connected the rational motion to the head thus formed. This rational motion is the power of the principal soul; and for that reason, he asserts that the principal power of the soul has its seat in the head. But since there have existed various opinions on this among philosophers both ancient and new, we must review each one so that, having made a comparison, it may become clear how much Plato’s view excels the others in truth.

Those who judged that the substance of matter <note>original: "sylvae," a Latin translation of the Greek "hyle," meaning wood or raw matter</note> is divisible—interposing an immense void, sometimes from a third part, or indeed parts that are indifferent and similar to themselves, such as atoms or solid masses—gave no certain and defined place to the principal part of the soul. For the spirit <note>original: "spiritus," often referring to the "pneuma" or breath of life</note>, as they themselves assert, passing through the throat to the lungs, being thinned in respiration, makes its passage to the seat of the heart. Then through...

<summary>Chalcidius explains Plato's view that the rational soul is seated in the head (the "citadel") and describes how education and maturity stabilize the soul's motions. He begins to contrast Plato's anatomical theories with those of atomists and other philosophers who place the soul's center elsewhere.</summary>
<keywords>Chalcidius, Plato, Timaeus, soul and body, reason, hegemonikon, education, anatomy of the soul, atoms, pneuma</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the physiological location of the soul and the nature of sensory perception.</meta>

# On Plato’s Timaeus. Folio XLVIII.

The arteries which are extended from the heart reach the Carotids—veins so named <note>original: "Caroticas"</note> because these same arteries, if wounded, bring about a sleep-inducing death. Through these, the same spirit <note>The "spiritus" or pneuma, thought to be a vital gas or fluid</note> is carried to the head through thin and narrow nerve passages. There, they say, the beginning of sensation first arises and flows into the rest of the body. This common sense is touch, but it becomes specific due to the diversity of the members with which we feel. For whatever the organs of sensing may be, such a sense exists: as sight through the eyes, hearing through the ears, and the others in the same manner. Yet it is one spirit which is shaped in many ways. For either there are certain light and spherical masses, and these same are quite delicate, from which the soul subsists—which is entirely spirit, as Asclepiades thinks—or they are ignited atoms according to Democritus, who judges that both fire and soul are excluded from these same bodies. Or perhaps it is the atoms themselves, by a certain chance and without reason, rushing together into one and creating the soul, as pleases Epicurus, because of the similarity of the atoms; when one of these is moved, the whole spirit—that is, the soul—is moved at the same time. Hence, for the most part, when whiteness is heard of in connection with snow, men remember the cold at the same time; or when someone eats something bitter, those who see this constantly spit with an increase of saliva; and when some yawn, others yawn at the same time; and we are moved in harmonious rhythms according to sounds. Since these things are so, they ask: why do we have more wisdom in the head than in the feet, since knowledge and disciplines are strengthened by the constant exercise of the senses? And the beginning of prudence, having started from some temperament and promoted by constant use, reaches perfection, while frequent observation creates rudiments; rudiments likewise create art, which, confirmed by time and use, becomes a discipline.

<margin>The power of the soul: whether animals live for a time with the head cut off, and perform their usual actions, as if no damage were done, the head being the principal part.</margin>
These same people deny that the principal power of the soul consists in the head, because many animals live for a time with the head cut off and perform their usual actions, as if no damage were brought to the whole of the body—such as bees, and likewise drones, which, although their heads are cut off, live for a moment and fly, and defend themselves with their stings according to their nature. They would not do this if that which is principal in the soul consisted in the head. They also deny it is in the heart.
<margin>Whether in the heart.</margin>
For crocodiles, even with their hearts torn out, live for some time and resist violence. This same thing has been observed in sea turtles and land goats.
<margin>Crocodiles</margin>
To these things it is responded by others: it is no wonder if, when the beginnings and, as it were, certain foundations of a dogma are laid weak and less suitable, their whole dogma totters. For what is their opinion of the soul? Namely, that it consists of minute corpuscles; therefore the body is the beginning of the soul, and there will be no substance proper to the soul, but rather to the body. For the body creates the soul according to them, and the life of the soul. It happens, therefore, according to this opinion of theirs, that the soul is never the same, but is held to be diverse and changeable. For since they say the body is older than the soul, they give the body the primacy. That, furthermore, always flows and is changed. And the soul, therefore, which is of a secondary condition, will suffer the same things as the body. It will be changed, therefore, by time and old age; it will likewise be attacked by the other things which the nature of the body brings. And so, a poorly founded opinion cannot find an honorable end.

<margin>Empedocles.</margin>
But truly, from those who think there is a continuous matter <note>original: "syluam," referring to hyle or primordial matter</note> and one continued to itself by a certain unification, Empedocles indeed established the principal power of the soul in the heart, saying thus: "For our intellect flourishes in the blood of the heart." <note>Empedocles argued that thought happens in the blood around the heart.</note> Indeed, we feel those things which are outside of us with our innermost senses because of kinship. And therefore he says:
> By earth we perceive earth, and by ether, flames,
> By moisture, the moist, and by our breath, the breathable.
But concerning all these things, what kind they are, or how much difference they have among themselves, [the power] of the heart...

<meta>This page continues the commentary of Chalcidius on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing various philosophical definitions of the soul and its location within the body, contrasting the views of the Hebrews, Empedocles, and the Stoics.</meta>

# Chalcidius’s Interpretation

Blood; we judge by means of blood. The Hebrews also seem to hold this opinion regarding the principal part of the soul <note>The "principal part" or *hegemonikon* refers to the governing center of the soul.</note>, when they say: *The blood of your brother cries out to me.* <note>Genesis 4:10</note> And likewise in another place: *You shall not eat flesh with the blood, because the blood is the soul of all animals.* <note>Leviticus 17:11-14</note> If these things are understood as they ought to be—that blood is the soul of animals because it is the vehicle of the irrational soul, whose parts consist of restless desires—then such an assertion clearly has a basis in reason. However, if they admit that the soul of man is rational, let them believe that God breathed into the men He made a divine spirit by which we reason, by which we understand, and by which we piously worship God:
<margin>We are gods and sons of the Most High.</margin>
and there is a kinship between us and divinity; we are even called gods and sons of the Most High God. <note>Psalm 82:6</note> To think that this kinship with God, and the entire faculty by which we reason, is merely blood, is not a correct opinion. Let all these things be said by us against the views of Empedocles as well.

<margin>The Stoics.</margin>
The Stoics, indeed, agree that the heart is the seat of the principal part of the soul; yet they do not say it is the blood that is born with the body. For Zeno <note>Zeno of Citium, founder of Stoicism.</note> seeks the spirit <note>original: *spiritus*, often translated as *pneuma* in Stoic philosophy.</note> in this way: That which, upon receding from the body, causes the animal to die, is certainly the soul. Furthermore, when the natural spirit recedes, the animal dies. Therefore, the natural spirit is the soul. Likewise, Chrysippus says:
> "By one and the same thing," he says, "we certainly breathe and live. But we breathe by the natural spirit; therefore we also live by that same spirit. But we live by the soul. Therefore, the natural spirit is found to be the soul."

<margin>Parts of the soul. † vital.</margin>
This soul, he says, is found to be divided into eight parts. For it consists of the principal part, the five senses, and also the † vocal substance, and the power of movement and procreation. Furthermore, the parts of the soul, flowing out from the seat of the heart like the head of a spring, are stretched through the whole body; they fill, rule, and govern all the members everywhere with vital spirit: nourishing them with innumerable and diverse powers, making them grow, moving them, providing them with local motions, and compelling the senses to act. The whole soul spreads out the senses, which are its duties, like branches from that principal part as if from a trunk, to be the messengers of those things which they perceive; the soul itself judges what they have reported like a king. Furthermore, these things which are perceived are composite, such as bodies; and each individual sense perceives one specific thing: this one colors, another sounds; yet another discerns the flavors of juices; this one the vapors of odors; that one roughness or smoothness by touch. And all these things belong to the present. Yet, no sense remembers the past, nor does it suspect the future.

<margin>The inner deliberation of the common sense.</margin>
Indeed, it is the property of inner deliberation and consideration to understand the experience of each sense: to gather from what they report what that thing is, to receive the present, to remember the absent, and to foresee the future. He defines the inner deliberation of the mind thus: "Inner deliberation is the movement of the soul's rational power." For even mute animals have a principal power of the soul by which they discern food, form mental images, avoid snares, leap over cliffs and precipices, and recognize their offspring; yet this is not a rational power, but rather a natural one. Man alone among mortals uses the principal good of the mind—that is, reason—as the same Chrysippus says.

<margin>The principal part of the soul in the heart.</margin>
Just as a spider in the middle of its web holds all the beginnings of the threads with its feet, so that whenever any little creature falls into the snares it feels it immediately from whatever part; so the principal part of the soul, placed in the middle seat of the heart, holds the beginnings of the senses, so that it may recognize immediately whatever they report.

<margin>The Voice.</margin>
They also say that the voice is sent from the inner chamber of the chest, that is, the heart, with the spirit striving from the lap of the heart, where the boundary of the nerves is placed to separate the heart from both the lungs and the other vital organs; by striking against the narrowness of the throat and being formed by the tongue and other vocal organs, the articulated sounds—the elements of speech—are emitted. By which interpreter...

<summary>Chalcidius discusses the nature of the soul, refuting the idea that the rational soul is merely blood, and detailing the Stoic view that the soul is a "natural spirit" (pneuma) centered in the heart, which governs the body and senses like a king or a spider in its web.</summary>
